Regarding This Volume

Volume 4 is a record of uprisings, protests, regime overthrows, civil wars, and coups in 23 countries of the Middle East and North Africa. The regional composition and country order follow the Country Analysis Series (2026-10-07). The method of description, dataset definitions, rules for connecting countries, and axioms and formulas are Vol. 1.

The figures in the table below represent the number of records linked to this country in each dataset, as counted by the program from the raw data. A value of 0 indicates that there are no records in the dataset; the magnitude of the figures varies depending on the dataset's definition, threshold, and scope of reporting (Vol. 1, Part 4).

2. Pre-Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

The 1611 Deys' Coup Ottoman Regency (Tripoli) · 1611

During the period when the Ottoman Empire governed Tripoli as a regency, actual power gradually shifted from the Pasha (governor) to the Janissary corps and their subordinate commanders, the deys. The Library of Congress Country Study notes that during this period, Janissary mutinies and coups were 'frequent,' and the corps generally supported whichever faction paid salaries and rations most reliably. In 1611, the deys staged a coup led by Sulayman Safar, and the Pasha had no choice but to appoint him head of government. Subsequently, he and his successors retained the title of dey, but succession among the deys typically occurred 'through intrigue and violence' (country study description). The dates and casualties of individual rebellions are not recorded in this source.

  1. Library of Congress Country Study: Libya (1987) — Pashas and Deys · Government Report · Orientation: Library of Congress Federal Research Division (as of 1987), synthesis of Ottoman and European sources — http://countrystudies.us/libya/15.htm

Ahmad al-Karamani's Seizure of Tripoli in 1711 Ottoman Regency (Tripoli) · 1711

In 1711, as military coups continued, the cavalry officer Ahmad al-Karamani seized Tripoli and massacred Ottoman officials. He presented the spoils he had taken from officials to the sultan in exchange for recognition as Pasha and regent, and ruled until 1745, founding a hereditary dynasty (the Karamanli dynasty) that nominally recognized Ottoman authority (Library of Congress Country Study description). From the perspective of the Ottoman center, this was a seizure by a local warlord; in subsequent accounts of Libyan regional history, it is treated as the establishment of an autonomous monarchy in fact.

  1. Library of Congress Country Study: Libya (1987) — Karamanlis · Government Report · Orientation: Library of Congress Federal Research Division (as of 1987) — http://countrystudies.us/libya/16.htm

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

Ali Ben Ghal's Coup in 1793 and Yusuf Karamanli's Return in 1795 1793–1795

In 1793, the Turkish officer Ali Benghul overthrew the Karmanlid dynasty and restored direct Ottoman rule in Tripoli. In 1795, Yusuf ibn Ali Karmanli returned with support from the Bey of Tunis, proclaimed himself pasha, and ruled until 1832 (Library of Congress Country Study description). The sources record the 1793 event as a coup rather than an internal war, and casualty figures are not substantial.

  1. Library of Congress Country Study: Libya (1987) — Karamanlis · Government report · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress Federal Research Division (as of 1987) — http://countrystudies.us/libya/16.htm

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

The Late Karmanlid Civil War and the Restoration of Ottoman Direct Rule in 1835 Civil War · 1815 onwards–1835

Following the Napoleonic Wars, France and Britain pressured the Diwan (Council) to levy special taxes, and unrest among cities, tribes, and rival claimants to the throne erupted into civil war. Yusuf Pasha abdicated in favor of his son Ali II (1832), but rebellions continued under Ali II's rule (1832–1835). Sultan Mahmud II sent Turkish troops under the pretext of restoring order; Ali II was exiled aboard an Ottoman warship, and direct Ottoman rule returned to Tripoli (Library of Congress Country Study description). The country study subsequently summarizes the 19th-century Ottoman rule as 'corruption, rebellion, and repression' and mentions 'strong local resistance' in the 1850s but does not specify individual leaders or dates.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations / Other
CasualtiesUnconfirmedUnconfirmed (no figures in country study)

Data Record: Although the event occurred after 1816, Libya (Tripoli) is not included under the COW civil war collection or NAVCO.

  1. Library of Congress Country Study: Libya (1987) — Karamanlis · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress Federal Research Division (as of 1987) — http://countrystudies.us/libya/16.htm
  2. Library of Congress Country Study: Libya (1987) — The Ottoman Revival · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress Federal Research Division (as of 1987) — http://countrystudies.us/libya/17.htm

Second Italian–Sanusi War and Omar Mukhtar Uprising · 1923–1931

The war began in early 1923 when Italian forces occupied Sanusi territory around Benghazi. The same year, northern Tripolitania was subdued, and the south and Fezzan were 'pacified' over the next few years; the main theater of operations was Cyrenaica. With Idris absent, the elderly Sheikh Omar Mukhtar commanded the Sanusi forces, whose numbers never exceeded several thousand and who employed mobile desert guerrilla tactics. Gracieani, appointed commander in 1929, mainly mobilized Eritrean troops to wage a war of attrition with armored columns, air support, and search-and-destroy operations; Italian forces herded Bedouins into concentration camps, blocked wells, and slaughtered livestock. In 1930, along the Egyptian border, a barbed-wire fence about 320 km long was erected to sever supply lines, and the surrounding area was declared a free-fire zone. The last stronghold, Kufra, fell in 1931; Mukhtar was arrested in September and, after a summary military tribunal, hanged before some 20,000 Arabs (according to the U.S. Library of Congress Country Studies account). The Italian side recorded it as the 'pacification' of the colony, while the Libyan side records it as an anti-colonial struggle; Mukhtar subsequently became a national symbol in Libya.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Concentration Camps / War DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed (no figures in the country study)
Crowd at Mukhtar's ExecutionUnconfirmedApprox. 20,000 (country study)

Data Record: As an external power (Italy) versus colonial resistance, it is not included in the COW intra-state collection. Recorded under NAVCO 1.3 'Italo-Libyan War (Sanusi)' (1920–1931, violence, failure) — see generator table.

  1. Library of Congress Country Study: Libya (1987) — The Second Italo-Sanusi War · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress Federal Research Division (as of 1987) — http://countrystudies.us/libya/21.htm

September 1, 1969 Free Officers Coup Coup · 1969

On September 1, 1969, approximately 70 young officers and enlisted men from the Signal Corps launched a coup in Benghazi, completing it within two hours. King Idris was abroad; he subsequently settled in Egypt at his own request and with Nasser's approval, remaining there until his death in 1983. The 12-member Revolutionary Command Council (RCC) became the government, abolishing parliament and seizing legislative power, while Colonel Muammar al-Qaddafi emerged as its head and de facto head of state. The country study notes that 'no deaths or violent incidents related to the coup have been reported.' The new regime called it a revolution, while the royalist side called it a military coup.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Number of ParticipantsUnconfirmedApprox. 70 officers and enlisted men (country study)
DeathsUnconfirmedNo reported deaths (country study)

Data Record: Powell & Thyne 1969-09-01 (success); Archigos notes Idris's departure as 'ousted by a military without foreign support' (exile) — see generator table.

  1. Library of Congress Country Study: Libya (1987) — The September 1969 Coup · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress Federal Research Division (as of 1987) — http://countrystudies.us/libya/28.htm

Coup Attempts, Student Protests, and the 1984 Attack on Bab al-Azizia During the Gaddafi Regime Coup · 1969–1987

According to the country study, a plot involving two ministers not belonging to the RCC (lieutenants) was uncovered in December 1969; a plot involving a distant relative of the former king based in Fezzan was uncovered in July 1970; and in 1975, an RCC member, Umar al-Muhaisi, attempted a coup. In January 1976, when Benghazi University students protested against interference in student union elections, security forces entered the campus and clashes broke out; the government denied reports of student deaths. In April of the same year, Tripoli University was temporarily closed after clashes during a purge process. On May 8, 1984, the National Front for the Salvation of Libya (LNSF, as designated in the country study) attacked Gaddafi's Bab al-Azizia compound but failed; anti-regime sources claimed that about 80 Libyans, Cubans, and East Germans died. Subsequently, approximately 2,000 people were arrested, and 8 were publicly hanged (accounted by the country study). The country study notes that several sources mention U.S. CIA training and support for the LNSF.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths in the 1984-05-08 AttackUnconfirmedApprox. 80 (anti-regime claim, cited by the country study)
Arrests and Public Executions After the 1984 AttackUnconfirmedApprox. 2,000 arrests, 8 public hangings (country study)
Estimated Domestic Executions in 1984Unconfirmed20–120 (range of estimates cited by the country study)

Data Record: Powell & Thyne records only the 1975-08-05 attempt as a coup; the 1969 and 1970 plots and the 1984 attack are absent. The 1993-10-23 (failed) attempt was described in sources consulted for this round, but its account could not be verified.

  1. Library of Congress Country Study: Libya (1987) — Opposition to Qadhafi · Government report · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress Federal Research Division (as of 1987), including figures from anti-regime sources — http://countrystudies.us/libya/76.htm

1996 Abu Salim Prison Incident Uprising · 1996

On June 28–29, 1996, many inmates at Abu Salim prison in Tripoli were killed by security forces. Former inmate Hussein al-Shaafi, interviewed by Human Rights Watch (HRW), testified that on June 28, inmates in Block 4 protested restrictions on family visits and poor treatment by seizing a guard delivering food, and that gunfire continued until around 2:00 PM the following day. The prison population at the time was approximately 1,600–1,700. The government initially denied the incident altogether; in April 2004, Qaddafi publicly acknowledged that killings had occurred; and in May 2005, the head of the Internal Security Organization stated that inmates had seized guards and weapons, and deaths had occurred as security forces restored order. The government denied that a crime had taken place and did not disclose the number of fatalities or a list of victims (HRW 2006).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
DeathsUnconfirmed (government acknowledged only that fatalities occurred)Approx. 1,200 (former inmate testimony; HRW independent verification impossible) / 1,170 (Libyan National Transitional Council tally)

Data Record: As an incident within a detention facility, it is not recorded in MM, ACLED (which begins in 1997), or COW.

  1. Human Rights Watch — Libya: June 1996 Killings at Abu Salim Prison (2006-06-27) · Human rights organization report · Orientation: International human rights organization; relies on testimony (explicitly states that independent verification is not possible) — https://www.hrw.org/news/2006/06/27/libya-june-1996-killings-abu-salim-prison

February 2011 Uprising and Civil War Civil War · 2011

On February 17, 2011, violence against protests in Benghazi, Bayda, Ajdabiya, and Derna killed 20, 23, 3, and 3 people respectively within a single day; on the 18th, 35 people died of gunshot wounds in Benghazi protests that began at the funeral of the previous day's victims (HRW; tallies by hospital staff and witnesses). The protests escalated into demands for regime change, turning into an armed uprising and civil war. The UN Human Rights Council's International Commission of Inquiry on Libya (A/HRC/19/68, 2012) concluded that both the Qaddafi and anti-Qaddafi (Tawergha) sides had committed war crimes; while viewing NATO as having conducted 'very precise operations,' it recommended further investigation into cases of civilian casualties and strikes on non-military targets. The Qaddafi side characterized the rebels as armed terrorist groups, while the rebel side characterized the uprising as a revolution.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Deaths in February 17–18 ProtestsUnconfirmed (no government announcement)At least 84 (HRW; hospitals and witnesses)
Combat Deaths (2011)UnconfirmedCOW 9,000

Data Record: COW 'Libyan Civil War of 2011' (9,000, internationalized), UCDP 11346 (2011–2020 government targets), NAVCO 'Libyan Civil War' (violence, success), Archigos Qaddafi 'ousted by foreign-backed rebels' (fatalities), ACLED 5,532 deaths in 2011 — there are large discrepancies between datasets in fatalities tallies (see generator table).

  1. Human Rights Watch — Libya: Security Forces Kill 84 Over Three Days (2011-02-18) · Human rights organization report · Orientation: International human rights organization; relies on phone interviews with hospital staff and witnesses — https://www.hrw.org/news/2011/02/18/libya-security-forces-kill-84-over-three-days
  2. Security Council Report — A/HRC/19/68 Report of the International Commission of Inquiry on Libya (2012) Summary · International Organization · Orientation: Independent research institute summary of the UN Human Rights Council investigation committee report — https://securitycouncilreport.org/un-documents/document/ahrc1968.php

'Dignity' vs. 'Dawn' Rivalry and the Second Civil War Since 2014 Civil War · 2014–2020

In May 2014, Khalifa Haftar, a former officer of the Qaddafi regime, declared the 'Operation Dignity' in Benghazi to oust Islamist militias in the east. The Misrata-based and Islamist coalition that lost the parliamentary elections launched the 'Libya Dawn' offensive against pro-Dignity forces in Tripoli, and Libya split into two parliaments and rival territorial spheres. Al-Qaeda and IS-affiliated organizations established bases exploiting this security vacuum (ICCT 2015). Using national registry data, Daw et al. (2019) tallied 16,126 conflict deaths and 42,633 injuries in 2012–2017; men accounted for approximately 81% of fatalities, and mortality rates were highest in eastern Benghazi and Derna (approximately 4 per 1,000 population); civilian casualties were excluded from the study. Each side characterized the other as either a terrorist force or a coup force.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Conflict Deaths 2012–2017Unconfirmed16,126 (Daw et al. 2019; national registry data; civilians excluded)
Conflict Injuries 2012–2017Unconfirmed42,633 (Daw et al. 2019)
Combat DeathsUnconfirmedCOW 'Second Libyan Civil War' (2014–ongoing) 1,500

Data Record: COW records 1,500, but ACLED fatalities are in the 3,000s only for 2014–2016; the discrepancy between the two figures is large. UCDP 13694 (government vs. IS, 2015–2019) recorded separately — see generator table.

2. Pre-Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

Abu Mahalli's Mahdist Rebellion Late Saadi Dynasty · Early 17th Century (1610s)

During the early 17th century, when the Saadi dynasty was shaken by succession disputes, the Sufi leader Abu Mahalli rose in rebellion against the Saadi rulers. The rebellion carried apocalyptic goals, with Abu Mahalli claiming to be a messiah (Mahdi), and later historians such as al-Ifrani recorded that his followers and worshippers grew in number. Matar (2014) analyzed how this Mahdist rebellion intersected with the commercial and apocalyptic visions of the British merchant John Harrison, who operated in Morocco at the same time, and also notes in a footnote evidence that Dutch officials supported Saadi ruler Mulay Zidan against the rebels. Saadi dynasty records describe it as treason, while those following Abu Mahalli portrayed it as a movement of religious renewal. The exact dates, territories occupied, and end of the rebellion could not be verified from sources consulted this round (unverified reference).

  1. Nabil Matar, 'The English Merchant and the Moroccan Sufi: Messianism and Mahdism in the Early Seventeenth Century', Journal of Ecclesiastical History 65(1) (2014) · Academic · Orientation: Peer-reviewed journal, cross-referencing British and Arabic sources — https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/journal-of-ecclesiastical-history/article/english-merchant-and-the-moroccan-sufi-messianism-and-mahdism-in-the-early-seventeenth-century/E0DD740EC3A17A2769F6B26B1EE2A2CB

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

Hafiziyya Uprising — Seizure of the Throne by Mawlay Hafid Overthrow of Government · 1907–1908

As resentment grew over the expansion of French influence and a fiscal crisis under Sultan Abdelaziz, forces led by his brother Mawlay Hafid rose up and seized the throne. The datasets classify this event differently — COW as the 'Hafiziyya Uprising of 1907-1908' (government vs. Mawlay Hafid's forces, 1,400 combat deaths, internationalized civil war), NAVCO as the 'Fez Caids Rebellion' (1907–1908, goal: expulsion of French occupation, violence, failure), and Archigos records Abdelaziz's departure (1908-01-05) as an irregular exit (reason unknown). Because the original public records describing this event could not be accessed in this round, the narrative is limited to cross-checking with dataset records.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations / Other
Combat DeathsUnconfirmedCOW 1,400

Data record: COW classifies the same event as a civil war over succession (Mawlay Hafid's faction victorious), while NAVCO classifies it as a pro-independence movement against France (failed), resulting in conflicting characterizations and outcomes.

  1. Correlates of War — Intra-State Wars v5.1 (Hafiziyya Uprising, war #646) · Academic · Orientation: academic conflict data set (combat deaths ≥ 1,000) — https://correlatesofwar.org/data-sets/cow-war/

The July 1971 Skhirat Palace Coup Attempt and the 1972 Attack on the Royal Jet Coup · 1971–1972

In July 1971, a bloody coup attempt took place at King Hassan II's Skhirat summer palace but was suppressed; it later emerged that five of thirteen active generals were involved. A year later, in August 1972, an attempt to shoot down the royal jet nearly succeeded. General Oufkir, the Minister of Defense, was clearly involved in the 1972 plot and likely also in the 1971 one; he met a death 'appearing to be suicide' on the morning of August 17. Both plots were conducted by small groups of officers largely disconnected from the general public; most ringleaders appeared to be conservative rural Berbers seeking the purge of corrupt cronies and curbs on royal power, though motives remain insufficiently clarified. Hassan II reorganized the military, abolishing the posts of Minister of Defense and Chief of Staff and dispersing ammunition into local governor-controlled warehouses (GlobalSecurity account, based on U.S. government country analysis data).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations / Other
1971 Skhirat DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed (no figures in open sources; unverified reference)
1971 Involved GeneralsUnconfirmedFive of thirteen active generals (GlobalSecurity)

Data record: Powell & Thyne 1971-07-10·1972-08-16 (both failed), UCDP 311 (1971, government vs. Medebu general's faction) — no 1972 prayer as a UCDP armed conflict.

  1. GlobalSecurity.org — Morocco's Military under Hassan II (1961-1999) · Security research institute · Orientation: US security information site, many articles reprinted from the Library of Congress Country Studies series — https://www.globalsecurity.org/military/world/morocco/far-hassan-ii.htm

Suppression of 'era of lead' riots and IER investigation Protests · 1956–1999

Morocco's Equity and Reconciliation Commission (IER) investigated major human rights violations from independence (1956) to the death of Hassan II (1999), submitting its final report to King Mohammed VI in December 2005. The commission received 16,861 applications and confirmed at least 9,779 human rights violations; reviewing the 592 individuals classified as 'disappeared,' it found that 322 had died during the suppression of riots in the 1960s, 1980s, and 1990s at the hands of excessive force, while 174 had died in illegal detention facilities. A mass grave containing up to 100 victims of the June 1981 Casablanca uprising over the cost of living was discovered, and human rights organizations claimed that the government had buried bodies in at least seven mass graves across Casablanca (Al Jazeera 2005).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths from Riot Suppression (1960s–1990s)322 (IER 2005)Unconfirmed
Deaths in Illegal Detention174 (IER 2005)Unconfirmed
Casablanca Uprising Deaths in 1981Unconfirmed (IER figures unverified in this round)One mass grave up to 100, human rights organizations claim 'at least seven mass graves'

Data record: The urban riots of 1965, 1981, 1984, and 1990 predate MM (recorded from 1990) and ACLED (started in 1997) or fall outside their scope, and are absent from COW and UCDP.

  1. Al Jazeera — Morocco to address past abuses (2005-12-17) · Media · Orientation: Qatari state-funded international broadcaster, citing figures from the IER report — https://www.aljazeera.com/news/2005/12/17/morocco-to-address-past-abuses

Rif Hirak protests and leadership trial Protests · 2016–2019

The Rif region's socioeconomic protest movement 'Hirak,' centered on Al Hoceima, continued (including the June 3, 2017 Al Hoceima protest). Over 40 protesters and activists, including leader Nasser Zefzafi, were prosecuted; in the first-instance trial in June 2018, Zefzafi was sentenced to 20 years in prison and co-defendants received sentences of 1 to 20 years, which the Casablanca Court of Appeals confirmed on April 6, 2019. The defendants retracted their confessions, alleging torture, and some medical reports suggested police violence; however, the court admitted the confessions as evidence (HRW 2019). Moroccan authorities maintain that the judicial process meets international standards.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Indictments / Verdicts ConfirmedOver 40 (sentences of 1 to 20 years)Over 40 (HRW)
Protest FatalitiesUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data record: MM recorded 5 events in 2017 and 6 in 2018; ACLED protests increased from 158 in 2017 to 208 in 2018 — see generator table.

  1. Human Rights Watch — Morocco: Shocking Verdict Against Activists, Journalists (2019-04-10) · Human rights organization report · Orientation: International human rights organization; torture allegations by defendants account for a significant portion of the report — https://www.hrw.org/news/2019/04/10/morocco-shocking-verdict-against-activists-journalists

GenZ 212 youth protests Protests · 2025

In late September 2025, protests organized by an anonymous online youth group called 'GenZ 212' spread to multiple cities. Protesters demanded healthcare and education reforms and the eradication of corruption, contrasting investments in the 2030 World Cup stadiums with underfunded schools and hospitals ('the stadium is here, where is the hospital?'). The group rejected violence and vowed to continue peaceful protests. As of October 1 reports, police fired on a group attempting to 'raid' a police station in the south, killing two (state media), while the Ministry of Interior announced that clashes overnight left 263 security personnel and 23 civilians injured and 409 people detained, with 142 security vehicles and 20 civilian vehicles burned. In Agadir, Tiznit, Oujda, and other cities, government offices, banks, and shops were looted and damaged. The Ministry of Interior stated that it would guarantee the right to protest under the rule of law and respond with 'restraint and moderation' (Al Jazeera 2025).

Injuries (overnight)Security personnel 263, civilians 23 (Ministry of Interior)Unconfirmed

2. Pre-Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

The Janissary (Ouzda) Rebellion and the Establishment of the Aga and Dey Regimes Ottoman Rule in Algiers · 1659, 1671

During the mid-17th-century Ottoman rule in Algiers, the Janissary corps (Ouzda) repeatedly rebelled against the Pashas sent by the Ottomans because they frequently failed to receive their pay. In 1659, the Aga of Ouzda accused the Pasha of corruption and incompetence and seized power; in 1671, a group of maritime forces (tayfas) rebelled, killed the Aga, and installed their own man, who took the title 'Dey.' From 1689 onward, a Diwan composed of approximately 60 influential figures elected the Dey, but initially Ouzda and later in the 18th century the Dey himself dominated it. The U.S. Library of Congress Country Study records that 14 out of 29 Deys between 1671 and 1830 were removed by assassination, mentioning alongside usurpations, military coups, and intermittent crowd rule. It also describes heavy taxation as a frequent source of rural unrest, and notes that the authority of the regency government extended only weakly into autonomous tribal areas such as Kabylie. Individual event names and dates of rural unrest are not provided in this source.

  1. U.S. Library of Congress, Algeria: A Country Study — 'Ottoman Rule' · Government Research Material · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress Federal Research Division Country Study (compendium of French- and English-language secondary literature), publication year unmarked on the page — http://countrystudies.us/algeria/16.htm

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

Abdelkader's War of Resistance Uprising · 1832–1847

Following the French occupation of Algiers in 1830, Muhieddin attacked French troops and their allied tribes (Makhzan) in Oran in 1832, and tribal elders elected his son Abdelkader as leader of the jihad that same year. After being defeated by Buzo in 1836, Abdelkader signed a treaty the following year granting conditional recognition to his regime. Reinforced after 1840, Buzo commanded one-third of the French Army, some 108,000 men, and employed a strategy of destroying the means of livelihood of the inhabitants—crops, orchards, and livestock—to starve them; cases are also recorded of non-combatants hiding in caves being burned or suffocated. Abdelkader surrendered to General Lamoricière, commander of the Oran district, at the end of 1847. French records treat this as a pacification operation, while Algerian accounts describe it as the starting point of national resistance. The number of deaths for this period does not appear in annual data.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
French Troops (1840s)108,000 (one-third of the French Army, LoC country study)Unconfirmed
DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data Record: Not recorded in COW internal war, UCDP (1946–), or NAVCO (1900–) — colonial conquest wars are included in the COW interstate and non-state war lists and are not present in this data/DZA.json.

  1. U.S. Library of Congress, Algeria: A Country Study — 'Abd al Qadir' · Government research material · Orientation: U.S. Congressional Library country study; secondary literature synthesis — http://countrystudies.us/algeria/21.htm

1871 Mokrani and Rahmaniya Uprisings (Kabyle Uprising) Uprising · 1871–1873

Following the fall of the Second French Empire to Prussia on September 4, 1870, and the transition from military to civilian rule in Algeria, Basha Mokrani of Mascara sent a letter to General Ouchou on March 15, 1871, announcing his resignation of office and his intention to fight on. On April 8, Sheikh El Hadad of the Rahmaniya sect issued a declaration calling for an uprising; military records estimated the sect's followers (Kouans) at around 200,000. Hachi (2018) describes this as the largest uprising in 19th-century Algeria and, borrowing the expressions of Hanoto and Lourton, as an event in which all Kabyles rose up. The U.S. Congressional Library country study notes that the expansion of civilian rule under Cremieux and the revocation of large land grants during the military regime were the triggers. After suppression, a special system excluding due process and the Code de l'indigénat (Indigénat) were applied in Kabylia, and more than 500,000 hectares of tribal land were confiscated. The leaders were tried in Constantine from March to September 1873; defendants claimed they had been promised an amnesty (aman). The French recorded it as a rebellion, while Algerians record it as national resistance.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Land ConfiscationUnconfirmedMore than 500,000 hectares (LoC country study)
Rahmaniya Sect FollowersApprox. 200,000 (French military account, cited by Hachi 2018)Unconfirmed
DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data Record: Not present in the Algeria entry of COW internal war v5.1 (Colonial period — non-member of state system). Quantitative data set gap.

  1. Hachi, I. 'Nnfaq[n]Urumi : le nom kabyle de l'insurrection de 1871', Insaniyat 22(82), 2018, pp.101–112 · Academic · Orientation: Algerian CRASC peer-reviewed academic journal; cross-checking of oral accounts and colonial records — https://www.insaniyat.crasc.dz/en/article/nnfaqnurumi-lenom-kabyle-de-linsurrection-de-1871
  2. U.S. Library of Congress, Algeria: A Country Study — 'Colonization and Military Control' · Government research material · Orientation: U.S. Congressional Library country study — http://countrystudies.us/algeria/22.htm

Setif and Guelma Massacres Uprising · 1945

On May 8, 1945, European Victory Day, approximately 8,000 Muslim Algerians gathered in Sétif for a celebration of Germany's defeat; the procession turned into an anti-colonial protest, and during a scuffle, police shot one onlooker. Over the next five days, attacks against European settlers and administrators spread across the region; after restoring order, the military launched reprisals involving mass arrests, summary executions, and bombings, while European vigilantes also killed Muslims. Approximately 4,500 people were arrested, about 100 were executed, and 64 received life sentences. Estimates of Muslim deaths vary widely from hundreds to nearly 50,000 — French army officers estimated 6,000–8,000, the New York Times approximately 18,000, and Algerian domestic media 50,000. Official European casualties were recorded as 89 killed and 150 wounded. It is noted that residents' dispersal from their villages contributed to the numerical discrepancy.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources

Data Record: An event not recorded in COW, UCDP, NAVCO, or MM — non-war repression prior to 1946 is a quantitative data set gap.

  1. EBSCO Research Starters — 'Algerian Nationalists Riot in Sétif' · Academic Reference Source · Tone: Academic database overview, some numerical sources unattributed — https://www.ebsco.com/research-starters/history/algerian-nationalists-riot-setif

Algerian War of Independence Uprising · 1954–1962

The war for independence, which began with the Front de Libération Nationale (FLN)'s armed uprising in 1954, ended with General de Gaulle's declaration of independence on July 3, 1962, after passing through the 'Generals' Putsch' in Algiers in April 1961 (collapsed within four days), the Évian ceasefire on March 19, 1962, and a referendum on independence on July 1. In March 1962, the Organisation armée secrète (OAS) detonated an average of 120 bombs per day; after OAS and FLN reached a truce on June 17, more than 350,000 settlers left in that month alone. The number of deaths varies greatly among the parties involved. The FLN estimated war-related deaths in 1962 at 300,000; subsequent Algerian sources have put the figure at approximately 1.5 million, and the Algerian government has adopted 1 million. French authorities' estimate is approximately 350,000. Historian Alice L. Conklin holds that the actual number is far higher than early FLN or French official estimates but falls short of 1 million. Recorded French military deaths stand at approximately 18,000 (non-combat approximately 6,000), European civilian deaths at 3,000, internal FLN purges at more than 12,000, 'café wars' between rival FLN and competing organizations in France at approximately 5,000, and Muslim civilians killed or kidnapped by the FLN at 70,000 (French estimate), with over 2 million displaced from their homes.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Algerian DeathsApprox. 1.5 million by Algerian sources / Algerian government adopted 1 million / FLN 1962 estimate of 300,000Approx. 350,000 by French authorities / Conklin: higher than early FLN and French estimates but under 1 million
French Military DeathsApprox. 18,000 (non-combat approx. 6,000, LoC Country Analysis)Unconfirmed
Displaced PersonsUnconfirmedOver 2 million (LoC Country Analysis)

Data Record: UCDP 246 (French government vs. FLN·MNA, non-state conflict, 1954–1962, war scale 7) and NAVCO (FLN, violence, success) record this war, but the Algeria entry in the COW list of internal wars contains only the 'Algerian Revolution of 1962' immediately after independence (combat deaths 2,000) — the main body of the war is classified as a COW interstate war and does not appear in this table.

  1. U.S. Library of Congress, Algeria: A Country Study — 'The Generals' Putsch' · Government Research Material · Tone: U.S. Congressional Library country study, estimates from both French and Algerian sides presented side by side — https://countrystudies.us/algeria/34.htm

Armed Uprising of the FFS and CNDR and the 1965 Boumédiène Coup Coup · 1963–1967

Immediately after independence, Aït Ahmed left the National Assembly to protest the regime's authoritarian turn and founded the socialist Front of Socialist Forces (FFS) in Kabylie, aiming to overthrow the Ben Bella government by force. In late summer 1963, sporadic clashes related to the FFS broke out, and regular troops were deployed to Kabylie; in 1964, more serious fighting occurred in Kabylie and southern Sahara. The Revolutionary Council for the Defense of the Revolution (CNDR), which united remaining FFS elements, Boudiaf's PRS, and local military leaders, organized an uprising, but the military suppressed it swiftly. In 1965, Aït Ahmed and Colonel Chahab were captured and sentenced to death; Chahab was executed, while Aït Ahmed's sentence was commuted to life imprisonment and he escaped in 1966. Conflict escalated in April 1965 when Ben Bella bypassed the Ministry of the Interior and ordered local police chiefs to report directly to him, and on June 19 Boumédiène, the defense minister, ousted Ben Bella in a 'swift and bloodless' military coup, detaining him in isolation. Thereafter, a revolutionary council dominated by 26 military officers took power; attempts at coups and assassination plots in 1967–68 failed.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths in the 1965 CoupUnconfirmedBloodless (LoC Country Study)
Deaths in the 1963–64 UprisingUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data records: Powell & Thyne 1964-06-30 (failure), 1965-06-19 (success), 1967-12-14 (failure); Archigos Ben Bella 'ouster by military without foreign support' · imprisoned; NAVCO 'former rebel leaders' (1962–1963, targeting Ben Bella's regime, violence, failure). The 1964 CNDR uprising is absent from COW and UCDP. Because the LoC country study does not specify the mastermind or date of the 1967 plot, it could not be directly cross-checked against P&T 1967-12-14.

  1. U.S. Library of Congress, Algeria: A Country Study — 'Ben Bella and the FLN' · Government research material · Orientation: U.S. Congressional Library country study — http://countrystudies.us/algeria/37.htm
  2. U.S. Library of Congress, Algeria: A Country Study — 'Boumediene Regime' · Government research material · Orientation: U.S. Congressional Library country study — http://countrystudies.us/algeria/38.htm

October 1988 Uprising Protests · October 1988

On October 4, 1988, youth riots broke out in the popular districts of El Harrach and Bab el Oued in Algiers; on the 5th, state-run shops, public offices, and FLN symbols were looted and burned, and unrest spread nationwide. Martial law (expressed as a state of emergency by the country study) was declared around Algiers on the 6th, with tanks entering the capital, and on the 10th, a march of some 20,000 people called by Islamist leader Ali Benhadj broke out in gunfire near the police headquarters. Martial law was lifted on the 12th. Testimonies of youth torture in military barracks emerged, but no official investigation was opened and no one was punished. Subsequently, a constitutional amendment on November 3 divided presidential and prime ministerial powers, and on February 23, 1989, a new constitution was approved with 73% support, allowing a multi-party system and removing the 'socialist' definition of the state. Economic hardship, corruption, and one-party rule are cited as the backdrop to resistance.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths159 (5 days, official tally — Belkaid 2011)Hospital sources: over 400 (Stora · Belkaid) / unofficial: over 500 (LoC country study) / maximum: 500 (Zadulaya)
Deaths and Injuries on October 10Unconfirmed36 dead, approx. 200 injured (Belkaid 2011)
ArrestsUnconfirmedOver 3,500 (unofficial, LoC country study)

Data records: MM (1990–) and ACLED (1997–) collections began after this event, and it is also absent from COW, UCDP, and NAVCO — an event that produced the regime transition (the 1989 constitution), but a quantitative data set gap.

The 1992 election suspension coup and the 'Dark Decade' civil war Civil War · 1992–2002

The Islamic Salvation Front (FIS) won the June 1990 local elections against the FLN and in the first round of the December 1991 general election it won an absolute majority in 188 of 430 constituencies. On January 11, 1992, Prime Minister Gholi and ministers Nacer and Belkeïr forced President Chadli Bendjedid to dissolve parliament and resign; after declaring the elections invalid, they formed the High Council of State (HCE) with Boudiaf. A state of emergency for one year was declared on February 9, and FIS was banned the following month. An internal war between government forces and armed Islamist groups continued until early 2002. The death toll is estimated at up to 200,000; while authorities acknowledge approximately 7,000 missing persons, non-governmental organizations estimate forced disappearances at 10,000–20,000. The Charter for Peace and National Reconciliation, adopted by referendum in September 2005, entered into force on February 28, 2006, under Decree 06-01; Article 45 bars prosecution of security forces while Article 46 provides three to five years' imprisonment for speech or conduct exploiting 'the national tragedy.' The government describes it as a reconciliation process, while victims' organizations regard it as an obstacle to accountability.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources

Forced DisappearancesApprox. 7,000 (officially acknowledged)10,000–20,000 (NGO estimates)
FIS 1991 General Election Round 1Absolute majority in 188 of 430 constituencies (LoC Country Analysis)Unconfirmed

COW 'Islamic Front War of 1992–2002' (combat deaths 25,200), UCDP 386 (1991–2020, AIS, GIA, AQIM, etc., war scale 6 years); NAVCO FIS (1992–2002, violence, failure). ACLED recorded 3,579 deaths in 1997 as the highest figure within its data collection window. The circumstances of Boudiaf's assassination were not confirmed by sources consulted in this round.

  1. U.S. Library of Congress, Algeria: A Country Study — 'Chadli Bendjedid and Afterward' · Government Research Data · Orientation: U.S. Congressional Library country study — http://countrystudies.us/algeria/39.htm

Kabylie 'Black Spring' Protests · 2001–2002

On April 18, 2001, near Tizi Ouzou in Beni Doual, a 18-year-old named Massinissa Germa was detained after a minor altercation between youths and gendarmes turned violent; he was seriously wounded by Kalashnikov fire and died two days later in an Algiers hospital. Following his funeral, residents across Kabylie rose up demanding the withdrawal of local gendarmerie, and the protests escalated into clashes in which security forces fired live ammunition. A representative of the RCD, then a member of the coalition government, criticized the Bouteflika government, saying 'I cannot stay in a government that shoots at its own children.' The movement of tribal organizations (arous) secured the withdrawal of most Kabylie gendarmerie in spring 2002; Tamazight was recognized as a 'language' by presidential decree and became the second official language in the 2016 constitutional revision. No one had been brought to court over this incident by the time of the article (2021).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsUnconfirmed126 (AFP, 2021)
InjuriesUnconfirmedOver 5,000 (AFP, 2021)

Data Record: MM recorded 6 events in 2001 (3 involving protester violence); ACLED recorded 94 protests in 2001, a sharp increase from 2 in 2000. Not present in COW, UCDP, or NAVCO.

  1. Hirak ('Revolution of Smiles' 2019) Protests · 2019–2020

In 2019, large-scale protests broke out against President Abdelaziz Bouteflika's bid for a fifth term; Bouteflika subsequently stepped down. The protests continued to demand wholesale change of the regime, and they halted in early 2020 amid the coronavirus pandemic and a ban on protests. In February 2024, Amnesty International stated that the authorities had intensified repression of peaceful dissent after the movement ended, particularly since 2021. Hundreds were arbitrarily detained; dozens of peaceful protesters, journalists, and activists remain imprisoned, and journalist Ikhlas El Kadi lost two appeals in his seven-year prison sentence. The government describes this as law enforcement, while Amnesty characterizes it as a violation of freedom of expression and assembly.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DetentionsUnconfirmedHundreds arbitrarily detained and held, dozens still imprisoned (Amnesty, 2024)
DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed (no figures in open articles)

Data record: NAVCO 'Smile Revolution' (2019, nonviolent, success) and 'Anti-Abdelaziz Bouteflika Campaign' (2010–2012, nonviolent, partial success); MM 66 events in 2019 (peak of the collection period); ACLED protests: 2,098 in 2019, 2,114 in 2020, 1,793 in 2021. Archigos 4.1 does not include Bouteflika's departure (outside the scope of inclusion).

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

Bashmur Coptic and Peasant Uprising Abbasid Period · 831–832 (AH 216–217)

An uprising against the Abbasid governor broke out in Bashmur, a coastal marshy area of the northern delta between the Rosetta and Damietta branches of the Nile. Al-Maqrizi and Severus ibn al-Muqaffa date the uprising to AH 216 (831), while some studies place it in 217. Heavy taxes levied on peasants, workers, and Copts, especially an increase in land tax, are cited as causes; interpretations that view it as a social uprising coexist with those that emphasize religious motives. Al-Maqrizi and Ibn Iyas describe the uprising as a joint rising of Arabs, Copts, and residents of northern and western areas, while Severus describes it primarily as an uprising of Bashmur Coptic peasants. Caliph al-Ma'mun sent Afshin to Cyrenaica; Isma'il ibn Mansur joined him, and after several battles they defeated the rebels. Al-Ma'mun personally came to Egypt in Muharram AH 217, rebuked the governor, and disbanded his forces. Afshin besieged Bashmur and accepted its surrender; according to al-Maqrizi, the caliph ordered the execution of the men and the sale of the women and children into slavery. Severus recorded it as 'the last and greatest rebellion of Coptic peasants.'

  1. Ma'ath Saad Farouk, 'It was farmers and Copts who led the first uprising against the Abbasid rule in Egypt', Raseef22, 2023-05-12 (citing al-Maqrizi, Severus, Ibn Iyas, and al-Qindi) · Media (historical commentary) · Affiliation: Lebanon-based Arabic digital outlet; cites primary sources from both Arab and Coptic traditions side by side — https://raseef22.net/article/1093222-it-was-farmers-and-copts-who-led-the-firrst-uprising-against-the-abbasid-rule-in

Seizure of the sultanate by Mamluk generals and the assassination of Qutuz Founding period of the Mamluk Sultanate · 1250–1260

After the death of the last sultan of the Ayyubid dynasty, political control of the state passed to the Mamluk guard, whose generals seized the sultanate. In 1258, as Mongol forces reached Syria, Mamluk commander Qutuz proclaimed himself sultan; in 1260, immediately after defeating the Mongols at Ain Jalut, Baybars I assassinated Qutuz and was acclaimed sultan. The account of the Mamluk era in the U.S. Library of Congress Country Studies does not mention individual cases of popular uprisings or peasant revolts; power transfers during this period are recorded as coups within the military elite.

  1. U.S. Library of Congress, Egypt: A Country Study — 'The Mamluks, 1250-1517' · Government research material · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress Country Studies, secondary literature synthesis; publication year not indicated on page — http://countrystudies.us/egypt/17.htm

A series of mutinies by Ottoman garrisons and the rise of Mamluk beys Ottoman rule · Late 16th century–early 17th century (individual years unspecified)

After Selim I defeated the Mamluk forces near Ridwanah, outside Cairo, in 1517 and left Khayrbak as a vassal, mutinies by several garrison groups followed from the late 16th to the early 17th century, while the Mamluk military structure revived at the same time. In the mid-17th century political leadership passed to the Mamluk beys, and in the late 18th century a single Mamluk bey took unprecedented control of the military and political structures, driving out the Ottoman governor. This source does not specify the rebels or individual years.

  1. U.S. Library of Congress, Egypt: A Country Study — 'The Ottoman Empire' · Government research material · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress Country Studies — http://countrystudies.us/egypt/18.htm

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

October 1798 Cairo uprising 1798

On October 21, 1798, following Napoleon's Egyptian campaign, the residents of Cairo rose up against the French forces, viewing them not as liberators but as 'foreign occupiers.' The uprising had a religious and ethnic character centered on Al-Azhar Mosque and was led by the ulama (the Islamic scholar class), whom Napoleon had sought to win over. The U.S. Library of Congress Country Study describes this uprising as the occasion on which residents came to view the ulama as their moral and political leaders. Casualties and the circumstances of suppression are not mentioned in this source.

  1. U.S. Library of Congress, Egypt: A Country Study — 'The French Invasion and Occupation, 1798-1801' · Government research material · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress Country Studies — http://countrystudies.us/egypt/20.htm

Ulama and merchant classes' endorsement of Muhammad Ali and the massacre of Mamluks in the Citadel in 1811 1805–1811

Amid continuing hostilities between the forces appointed by the Ottomans after the withdrawal of French troops—the pashas—and the Mamluks, the ulama and urban merchants saw Muhammad Ali as the only leader capable of bringing order and security. The Ottoman government sought to send him back to his post as governor of Hejaz, but under pressure from Egyptian forces it appointed him governor of Egypt in June 1805. In March 1811, 64 Mamluks—including 24 beys—were killed in the Cairo Citadel, and Muhammad Ali subsequently became the sole ruler of Egypt. The country study does not mention popular uprisings against the Ottoman governor Kursid Pasha during his rise to power.

  1. U.S. Library of Congress, Egypt: A Country Study — 'Muhammad Ali, 1805-48' · Government research material · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress Country Studies — http://countrystudies.us/egypt/21.htm

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

The Urabi Revolt and British Occupation Uprising · 1881–1882

As European creditor nations deepened their financial intervention after 1876, from 1881 officers led by Ahmad Urabi began to defy authority and assert their power. In Alexandria in 1882 anti-European riots broke out, causing substantial casualties on both sides; after the French fleet withdrew, the British fleet shelled Alexandria in July. On September 13 at Tel el-Kebir the Urabi forces were annihilated and the capital was occupied. Urabi was captured, tried, and sentenced to death by an Egyptian court but had his sentence commuted to exile on Ceylon (Sri Lanka) by British intervention. The British side regarded it as an intervention to restore order; Egyptian nationalist accounts record it as a constitutional and anti-foreign revolution.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Deaths in the 1882 Alexandria RiotsUnconfirmed'Substantial casualties on both sides' (LoC, no figures)
Casualties at the Battle of Tel el-KebirUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data record: The Egypt link item in COW Civil War v5.1 does not include this event; instead, Argentina's 'Fourth Buenos Aires War' of 1880 is linked to Egypt (linking error — anomaly recorded). The Anglo-Egyptian War falls under the COW interstate war category.

  1. U.S. Library of Congress, Egypt: A Country Study — 'From Intervention to Occupation, 1876-82' · Government Research Data · Orientation: U.S. Congressional Library country study — http://countrystudies.us/egypt/25.htm

The 1919 Revolution Uprising · 1919–1922

On March 8, 1919, Saad Zaghloul of the Wafd and three colleagues were arrested and imprisoned in Kasr el-Nil prison before being deported to Malta the next day. This triggered a wave of protests in March–April that paralyzed Egypt within a week through general strikes and unrest, with violence spreading from Cairo and Heliopolis to Upper Egypt's Assiut governorate. By the summer of 1919 more than 800 Egyptians, 31 Europeans, and 29 British soldiers had died. Britain maintained the protectorate while appointing a Milner Mission in May to consider granting 'self-governing institutions,' but the mission, which arrived in December, was boycotted by nationalist forces. On February 28, 1922, Britain unilaterally declared Egypt's independence, reserving four issues — imperial communications, defense, foreign interests, and minorities and Sudan — for later settlement.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Egyptian deaths (by summer 1919)UnconfirmedMore than 800 (LoC country study)
European and British military deathsUnconfirmed31 Europeans, 29 British soldiers (LoC country study)

Data record: NAVCO 'Egyptian Revolution for Independence' (1918–1922, targeted the British colonial government, nonviolent, successful). The event in which more than 800 people died is classified by NAVCO as a 'nonviolent' campaign (based on the primary mode of resistance). Not present in COW or UCDP.

  1. U.S. Library of Congress, Egypt: A Country Study — 'Egypt under the Protectorate and the 1919 Revolution' · Government Research Data · Orientation: U.S. Congressional Library country study — http://countrystudies.us/egypt/28.htm

The 1952 Free Officers Coup and the 1954 Power Struggle Coup · 1952–1954

In 1952 the Free Officers handed power to former Prime Minister Ali Mahir after a coup, and King Farouk went into exile. On June 18, 1953, a republic was proclaimed and the monarchy abolished. Nasser and Naguib's conflict reached its peak on February 23, 1954, with Naguib's resignation, but amid popular backlash Naguib returned as president and Nasser became prime minister. In February 1954 Nasser outlawed the Muslim Brotherhood, and on October 26 a Brotherhood member attempted to assassinate Nasser while he was explaining the Suez Canal Agreement in Alexandria. When the Brotherhood leadership implicated Naguib, Naguib stepped down in November.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
1952 Coup FatalitiesUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data records: Powell & Thyne 1952-07-23 (success) · 1954-02-27 (success); Archigos Farouk 'overthrown by military without foreign support' (terminated 1952-07-22) · exile; Naguib regular departure (1954-11-14) · exile; Nasser irregular accession (1954-11-17). P&T records the February 1954 Naguib resignation phase as a successful coup, while Archigos records Naguib's November 1954 departure as 'regular,' resulting in conflicting characterizations of the same power transfer. The January 1977 Cairo 'Black Saturday' fire is not recorded in the consulted sources and was therefore omitted.

  1. U.S. Library of Congress, Egypt: A Country Study — 'The Revolution and the Early Years of the New Government: 1952-56' · Government Research Data · Orientation: U.S. Congressional Library country study — http://countrystudies.us/egypt/32.htm

1977 Bread Uprising Protests · 1977

When President Sadat, following World Bank and International Monetary Fund recommendations, cut subsidies on bread and other foodstuffs, so-called 'bread intifadas' broke out in several cities. Approximately 80 people were killed and hundreds injured. Sadat called in the military to disperse the protests, but he withdrew the price increases. The government frames the unrest as a matter of public order, while participants' accounts characterize it as a livelihood uprising.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsUnconfirmedApproximately 80 (Globe and Mail, 2022)
InjuriesUnconfirmedHundreds (Globe and Mail, 2022)

Data record: Preceding MM (1990–) and ACLED (1997–); also absent from COW, UCDP, and NAVCO — the policy reversal of subsidy withdrawal produced it, but it remains a quantitative data gap.

  1. Eric Reguly, 'Food fury: Egypt's soaring bread prices are chewing a hole in the …', The Globe and Mail, 2022-12-07 · Media · Orientation: Canadian daily newspaper, retrospective account from the 2022 vantage point — https://www.theglobeandmail.com/world/article-food-fury-egypts-soaring-bread-prices-are-chewing-a-hole-in-the/

Mass Arrests and Sadat's Assassination in 1981 Regime Overthrow · 1981

In September 1981, Sadat ordered the mass arrest of opposition forces; official figures recorded at least 1,500 arrests, while unofficial reports indicated a higher number. The Muslim Brotherhood was the primary target of the crackdown. On October 6, Sadat was killed by members of the al-Jihad organization while watching a military parade; the gunman was Lieutenant Colonel Khalid el-Islambuli.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
September 1981 ArrestsAt least 1,500 (official)More than that (unofficial reports, LoC country study)

Data record: Archigos lists Sadat as 'irregular, other' (1981-10-06) · death. Powell & Thyne does not count the assassination as a coup. The armed clashes in Asyut immediately thereafter are not recorded in the consulted sources and were therefore omitted.

  1. U.S. Library of Congress, Egypt: A Country Study — 'The Aftermath of Camp David and the Assassination of Sadat' · Government Research Data · Orientation: U.S. Congressional Library country study — http://countrystudies.us/egypt/45.htm

2011 January 25 Revolution Regime Overthrow · 2011

Mass protests that began on January 25, 2011, ended Hosni Mubarak's three-decade rule on February 11. The fact-finding committee appointed by the government concluded in April that police had used excessive force against pro-democracy protesters and that there had been an Interior Ministry order authorizing live ammunition to disperse the protests. The committee tallied 846 protester deaths, more than 6,000 injuries, and 26 police deaths, more than double the approximately 380 initially announced by authorities. A military officer testified to receiving orders to open fire. Concurrently, Amnesty International criticized the Supreme Council of the Armed Forces for continuing torture, arbitrary detention, and military trials of civilians.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Protester DeathsAuthorities' initial announcement: approx. 380 → Government fact-finding committee: 846 (2011-04)Unconfirmed (original independent tally not consulted in this round)
InjuriesMore than 6,000 (fact-finding committee)Unconfirmed
Police Deaths26 (fact-finding committee)Unconfirmed

Data record: Archigos records Mubarak's ouster as 'popular protests without foreign support,' while Powell & Thyne records 2011-02-11 as a 'successful coup,' resulting in conflicting classifications of the same event. NAVCO 'Anti-Mubarak movement' (2007–2011, nonviolent, successful), MM 20 events in 2011, ACLED 1,254 protests and 1,255 deaths in 2011.

  1.   2. Democracy Now!, 'Report: 846 Egyptian Civilians Killed in Uprising', 2011-04-20 · Media · Orientation: US independent/progressive broadcaster — https://www.democracynow.org/2011/4/20/headlines/report_846_egyptian_civilians_killed_in_uprising

The Ouster of Morsi and the Dissolution of the Rabaa Sit-in in 2013 Coup · 2013

On July 3, 2013, the military ousted President Muhammad Morsi, and Morsi supporters began an indefinite sit-in at Rabaa al-Adawiyah Square from that day until August 14 (estimated at about 85,000 on the night of August 2). Security forces dispersed the sit-in on August 14. Human Rights Watch (HRW) recorded at least 817 deaths in this dispersal and estimated that the actual toll exceeded 1,000; for July–August as a whole, it tallied at least 1,150 protesters killed. Prime Minister Hazem al-Beblawi cited approximately 1,000 deaths from the dispersals of Rabaa and Nahda, while the official forensic authority reported the deaths of eight police officers during the dispersal. The minister of interior stated that 15 firearms were found in the square. The government explained the dispersal as necessary to prevent the sit-in from disrupting residents' lives and becoming a platform for incitement and terrorism; HRW judged it an unbalanced use of lethal force disproportionate to the threat and likely to constitute a crime against humanity. The National Council for Human Rights, the body responsible for the inauguration ceremony, acknowledged in March 2014 that security forces had used excessive force, but HRW recorded that no credible judicial investigation or prosecution had taken place.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Rabaa Dispersal DeathsPrime Minister: Rabaa and Nahda combined total of approximately 1,000HRW at least 817 (estimated over 1,000)
Protest Deaths in July–August 2013UnconfirmedHRW at least 1,150
Police Deaths (Rabaa)8 (forensic authority)Unconfirmed

Data Record: Powell & Thyne 2013-07-03 (successful coup) vs. Archigos characterizing the ouster of Morsi as 'popular protests without foreign support' and his imprisonment — classifications diverge. NAVCO 'Anti-Morsi Protests' (success), 'Pro-Morsi Protests' (2013–2014, nonviolent, failure); MM 22 events in 2013 (highest within the collection period); ACLED: 1,521 protests and 2,479 deaths in 2013.

  1. 5. Modern Events through the Literature

Ali Ben Ghedhem's Mezba Uprising Uprising · 1864–1865

When Prime Minister Mustafa Khaznadhar of the Husseinite dynasty decided to double the tax 'mezba' to alleviate fiscal difficulties, an uprising broke out in the north in March 1864 and soon spread nationwide. The rebels also protested local administration, judicial practices, and bribery, and leader Ali Ben Ghedhem (born c. 1815), drawing on his ties with the Tijaniyya Sufi order, rallied several tribes. An entry in the Islamic Encyclopedia describes this as the largest popular and nomadic uprising in 19th-century Tunisian history. The government initially granted general amnesty, established a new court, abolished certain laws and the constitution (1861), and reduced taxes; French, British, Italian, and Ottoman fleets entered the waters off Tunis (the Ottoman special envoy arrived on 1864-05-11; the fleet withdrew on 1864-09-23). After preparing, government forces defeated the rebels in January 1865. Ben Ghedhem fled to Algeria but returned in January 1866 under a promise of safe conduct; he was arrested and died in prison on October 10, 1867. Contemporary Tunisian government records treat it as rebellion, while later nationalist accounts portray it as popular resistance.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations · Other
Combat DeathsUnconfirmedCOW 2,000

Data Record: Recorded under COW 'Sahil Revolt of 1864-1865' (government vs. Ghadam-led rebels; 2,000 combat deaths; government victory) — the event name is named after a Sahel region rather than the rebel leader's name.

  1. Atilla Çetin, 'İBN GIZÂHUM', İslam Ansiklopedisi (Küre Ansiklopedi included) · Academic · Orientation: Entry in the Turkish Islamic Encyclopedia, citing Ibn Abi Diyaf's chronicle, Slama, Chater, etc. — https://kureansiklopedi.com/en/detay/ibn-gizahum
  2. Correlates of War — Intra-State Wars v5.1 (Sahil Revolt, war #581) · Academic · Orientation: Academic conflict data set (threshold of 1,000 combat deaths) — https://correlatesofwar.org/data-sets/cow-war/

'Black Thursday' — General strike of the Tunisian General Labour Union (UGTT) Protests · 1978

When a general strike called by the UGTT on January 26, 1978 escalated into riots, troops were deployed; military command was assumed by Zine El Abidine Ben Ali, whom the then-prime minister had hastily appointed. Hundreds of unionists were arrested and more than 500 were sentenced to prison terms; Hussein Kouchi, deputy general secretary of the bank and insurance federations in Sousse, died after torture. The state of emergency and curfew lasted for more than a month. The Truth and Dignity Commission (IVD), established after the revolution, referred the case to a special criminal chamber, and at the October 2019 trial, fewer than 15 defendants appeared in court (JusticeInfo 2019).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths52 (official announcement)Up to 200 (Tunisian League for the Defense of Human Rights · opposition parties)
InjuriesMore than 365Approx. 1,000 (Tunisian League for the Defense of Human Rights · opposition parties)
ConvictionsMore than 500Unconfirmed

Data record: Not found in COW, UCDP, NAVCO, MM (from 1990), or ACLED (from 1997).

  1. JusticeInfo.net — Tunisie : les chambres spécialisées toujours en panne (Olfa Belhassine, 2019-10-24) · Media · Orientation: Swiss foundation-run transitional justice outlet, emphasis on victim testimony — https://www.justiceinfo.net/fr/42702-tunisie-chambres-specialisees-en-panne.html

1984 Bread Riots Protests · 1983–1984

As the government sought to stem a worsening balance of payments by cutting subsidies on basic foodstuffs such as bread, bread prices doubled and those of semolina and pasta rose nearly as much. Protests began in the south before the official announcement on January 1, 1984, spread through Gabès and Kasserine to the whole country, a state of emergency was declared on January 3, and troops used automatic weapons against crowds behind barricades in Tunis. Order was restored only after Bourguiba announced on radio and television on January 6 that the price increases had been revoked. The U.S. government's Country Analysis Report describes this as the most widespread rioting in Tunisian history, with approximately 500,000 participants (ten times the number in 1978). The Tunisian League for the Defense of Human Rights identified regional and class disparities and the non-fulfillment of promises of political reform as causes; a government investigation committee concluded that the interior minister, Driss Guiga, had neglected his duty to maintain public order; and Guiga himself rejected this as an 'unfounded calumny.'

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths89 (official announcement, final) / more than 50 (government officials on 1984-01-06)Unconfirmed
InjuriesMore than 938 (including 348 security personnel)Unconfirmed
ArrestsMore than 1,000Unconfirmed

Data record: All outside the scope of COW, UCDP, NAVCO, MM, and ACLED or not included.

  2. Christian Science Monitor — News In Brief (1984-01-06) · Media · Orientation: U.S. daily newspaper, citing government officials' figures — https://csmonitor.com/1984/0106/010654.html

Ben Ali's 'Medical Coup' — Ousting Bourguiba Coup · 1987

On October 2, 1987, Bourguiba appointed Ben Ali (51), the former general and interior minister, as prime minister. Ben Ali then forced him to step down on November 7 through a constitutional procedure, arguing that Bourguiba was medically unfit to perform his duties. The basis of this provision was a clause allowing the prime minister to declare the president's incapacity for office on the basis of diagnoses by seven physicians. Bourguiba had been declared president for life by parliament in 1975 and died on April 6, 2000. Al Jazeera described this takeover as a 'bloodless coup,' and some Bourguiba supporters and commentators questioned the diagnosis itself.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsNone (bloodless)None (Al Jazeera 'bloodless coup')

Data record: Powell & Thyne 1987-11-07 successful coup; Archigos Bourguiba's departure from office was recorded as an irregular removal by another government actor. The leader name in the Archigos record is listed as 'Ben Ali Bourguiba' (anomaly noted).

  1. GlobalSecurity.org — Habib Bourguiba · Security Research Institute · Orientation: U.S. security information site, republication of a U.S. Congressional Library Country Analysis Report — https://www.globalsecurity.org/military/world/tunisia/bourguiba.htm
  2. Al Jazeera — Tunisia's Ben Ali flees amid unrest (2011-01-15) · Media · Orientation: Qatari state-funded international broadcaster — https://www.aljazeera.com/news/2011/1/15/tunisias-ben-ali-flees-amid-unrest

Gafsa Mining Basin Uprising Protests · 2008

Protests against corruption, nepotism, and opacity in the hiring practices of the state-owned Gafsa Phosphate Company (CPG) began in Redeyef and spread to several mining basin cities, lasting approximately six months. Excessive force by security forces resulted in the deaths of three protesters; dozens were arbitrarily arrested, tortured, and mistreated, and received heavy sentences after unfair trials (Amnesty). Ben Ali later acknowledged the fraud in the hiring process, and the governor of Gafsa and the CPG president were replaced. The trial of this case, handed over by the Truth and Dignity Commission on September 26, 2018, was held at the First Instance Court of Gafsa; 51 victims and 16 defendants (including former President Ben Ali and a former Minister of the Interior) were involved, but none of the 16 defendants appeared.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsUnconfirmed3 (Amnesty)
ArrestsUnconfirmedDozens (Amnesty 2018) / Approximately 300 arbitrary detentions (Amnesty 2009, search summary — unverified)

Data Record: ACLED recorded 27 protests in 2008 (a sharp increase from 1–2 events in post-conflict years), with 4 deaths. MM has no record for 2008.

  1. Amnesty International — Révolte de Gafsa & justice transitionnelle : la portée symbolique ne suffit pas (2018-10-04) · Human Rights Organization Report · Orientation: International human rights organization, victim perspective — https://www.amnesty.org/fr/latest/news/2018/10/gafsa-uprising-transitional-justice-trials-must-not-stop-at-being-symbolic/

Jasmine Revolution — Ben Ali's Exile Regime Overthrow · 2010–2011

On December 17, 2010, the self-immolation of a 26-year-old unemployed youth, Muhammad Bouazizi, sparked protests against rising prices and unemployment that spread nationwide. Ben Ali declared a state of emergency and promised general elections within six months, but on January 14, 2011 (Friday), after more than 23 years in power, he left the country for Saudi Arabia (France did not admit him). Prime Minister Muhammad Ghannouchi declared himself acting president, but the next day the Constitutional Council designated Fouad Mebazaa, president of the National Assembly, as acting president with a 60-day mandate. The National Inquiry Commission (headed by Taoufik Boulbela, 15-month investigation) reported in its May 2012 report that 338 people died and 2,147 were injured, and that 66% of the deaths were caused by gunfire, with primary responsibility lying with Ben Ali. Previous estimates had put the toll at approximately 300 dead and 700 wounded.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths338 (National Inquiry Commission 2012)Approximately 300 (previous estimate, AP report)
Injuries2,147 (National Inquiry Commission 2012)Approximately 700 (previous estimate)

Data Record: NAVCO 'Anti-Ben Ali Campaign (Jasmin Revolution)' 2010–2011 nonviolent and successful; Archigos records Ben Ali's departure and exile following mass protests on 2011-01-14. ACLED recorded 221 deaths in 2011, which differs from the 338 reported by the inquiry commission.

  1. AP via Fox News — Report: 338 killed during Tunisia revolution (2012-05-05) · Media · Orientation: AP wire report (published by a US conservative outlet), citing figures from the inquiry commission — https://www.foxnews.com/world/report-338-killed-during-tunisia-revolution
  2. Al Jazeera — Tunisia's Ben Ali flees amid unrest (2011-01-15) · Media · Orientation: Qatari state-funded international broadcaster — https://www.aljazeera.com/news/2011/1/15/tunisias-ben-ali-flees-amid-unrest

President Saied's emergency measures between July 25, 2021 Regime Overthrow · 2021

On July 25, 2021, President Kais Saied invoked Article 80 of the 2014 Constitution (authorizing 'all necessary measures' in the event of an 'imminent threat to the state') to dismiss Prime Minister Hichem Mechichi, suspend parliament, lift parliamentary immunity, and assume control of the judiciary. The presidency stated that the suspension period would be 30 days. He cited months of deadlock between the president, prime minister, and parliament (where Ennahda and its allies held a majority) and the government's failure to respond to the coronavirus pandemic as reasons. On July 26, the military blocked members of parliament, including speaker Rached Ghannouchi, from entering the parliament building, and police closed the Al Jazeera bureau in Tunis without a warrant. Ghannouchi and former president Moncef Marzouki called it a 'coup,' while Saied maintained that his actions were in accordance with the constitution, and crowds supporting the president gathered in several cities. HRW noted that the Constitution stipulates that parliament must be in permanent session when Article 80 is invoked.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data Record: Powell & Thyne and Archigos do not record this as a coup or an irregular transfer of power (expansion of presidential authority). ACLED recorded 1,303 protests in 2021.

  1. Human Rights Watch — Tunisia: President's Seizure of Powers Threatens Rights (2021-07-27) · Human Rights Organization Report · Orientation: International human rights organization, perspective on rights violations — https://www.hrw.org/news/2021/07/27/tunisia-presidents-seizure-powers-threatens-rights

2. Pre-Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

Fakhreddin II's Ottoman Revolt and Downfall Maan Emirate in the Ottoman Period · 1613–1635

The Maan family entered Lebanon in 1120 under orders from the Damascus governor to defend against the Crusaders, and their power reached its peak under Fakhreddin II (1570–1635). When he secretly allied with Grand Duke Ferdinand I of Tuscany and the Ottomans against each other, the sultan ordered Ahmed al-Hafiz, the Damascus governor, to attack him; Fakhreddin fled to Tuscany in 1613 and returned in 1618. In 1623, his army decisively defeated Mustafa Pasha at Anjar in the Bekaa Valley, and the sultan conferred on him the title of 'Sultan of the Mountains (Sultan al Barr)'. Later, Murad IV ordered Kuchuk, the Damascus governor, to attack again, and he was defeated; he was executed in Constantinople in 1635. The U.S. Library of Congress Country Studies describes his actions as an attempt toward complete independence for Lebanon without using the term 'rebellion' — on the Ottoman side it is read as a revolt by a local power, and in Lebanese nationalist narratives as a movement for self-determination.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Lebanon: The Maans (1987) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress Country Study (1987), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/lebanon/16.htm
