5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

Uprising against Bashir II and Egyptian Rule Uprising · 1840

Bashir II of the Shihab family broke away from the Ottomans and allied with Muhammad Ali, assisting Ibrahim Pasha's siege of Acre (fall on 1832-05-27) and the occupation of Damascus (1832-06-14). His and Ibrahim's rule was harsh, levying high taxes that repeatedly provoked uprisings; in May 1840 Maronites and Druze joined forces to resist Egypt. Following the London Treaty of July 15, 1840, Ottoman and British troops landed on September 10; when Muhammad Ali withdrew, Bashir surrendered to Britain on October 14 and went into exile.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data Record: Not included in COW, UCDP, or NAVCO.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Lebanon: The Shihabs (1987) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress Country Study (1987), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/lebanon/17.htm

The Kisrawan Peasant Uprising and the 1860 Mount Lebanon Civil War Civil War · 1858–1861

Following the division of Mount Lebanon into two districts in 1842, clashes between Christians and Druze resumed from May 1845. In 1858, Maronite peasant leader Tanios Shehab demanded the abolition of feudal privileges from the sheikhs of Kisrawan; when they refused, poor peasants plundered the sheikhs' land and burned their homes. This tension escalated into massacres between Druze and Christians in 1860. The U.S. Library of Congress Country Study notes that approximately 10,000 Maronites died in the 1860 massacres, along with Greek Catholics and Greek Orthodox killed by Druze. Following the 1861 Convention, Mount Lebanon was separated from Syria and came under the rule of a Mutasarrif (a Christian Lebanese ruler) and a 12-member administrative council. Each community remembers this event through different narratives of victimhood — the Maronite account portrays it as Druze massacres, while Druze accounts frame it as a response to peasant uprisings and foreign intervention.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Maronite Deaths in 1860UnconfirmedApprox. 10,000 (U.S. Library of Congress Country Study)

Data Record: The 1860 Mount Lebanon Civil War is not listed in the COW civil war list (1816–) — because it was an event within Ottoman territory and not a separate state unit, it does not appear under Lebanon's country code.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Lebanon: Religious Conflicts (1987) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress Country Study (1987), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/lebanon/18.htm

The 1958 Crisis — Chamoun's Bid for a Third Term and the U.S. Military Landing Civil War · 1957–1958

Under the National Pact, sectarian tensions accumulated, and after the 1956 Suez Crisis and the 1958 merger of Egypt and Syria, the conflict between Lebanese nationalism and pan-Arabism deepened. President Camille Chamoun sought a constitutional amendment to allow his reelection; when his supporters won the May–June 1957 general election, the opposition called it a 'fix' and some resorted to extra-legal means. The assassination of anti-Chamoun journalist Nasib Mattar nearly turned the rebellion into a Christian-Muslim confrontation. When the Iraqi monarchy collapsed on July 14, 1958, Chamoun requested support from US, British, and French ambassadors; under the Eisenhower Doctrine, US troops landed in the afternoon of July 15 (about 14,000 personnel). The role of US forces was more symbolic than actual. Army Commander Fuad Chehab refused to use the military against Muslim protesters, and parliament elected him president.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
CasualtiesUnconfirmed2,000–4,000 (US Congressional Research Service, mainly Beirut Muslim areas and Tripoli)
Combat DeathsUnconfirmedCOW 2,500

Data Record: COW 'First Lebanese War of 1958' (combat deaths 2,500, internationalization), UCDP 260 (1958–1990), NAVCO records 'leftists (anti-Shamun)' violence and failure — NAVCO records it as a failure, so assessments of the outcome diverge; Chamoun's reelection bid ultimately failed.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Lebanon: The Shamun Era (1987) · Government Report · Orientation: US Congressional Research Service (1987), US government publication — https://countrystudies.us/lebanon/23.htm
  2. GlobalSecurity.org — Operation Blue Bat 1958 · Security Research Institute · Orientation: US security information site, based on US military records — https://www.globalsecurity.org/military/ops/blue_bat.htm

Lebanese Civil War Civil War · 1975–1990

On April 13, 1975, gunmen attempted to assassinate Pierre Gemayel and killed four Phalangist party members; later that day, the Phalangist side attacked a bus carrying Palestinian passengers traveling through Christian areas, killing approximately 26 people. The 'Lebanese Front,' centered on Maronite militia seeking to maintain the status quo, confronted the 'Lebanese National Movement' led by Kamal Jumblatt and leftist/Palestinian guerrillas. In January 1976, the Lebanese Front besieged the Tal Zaatar refugee camp in East Beirut; Muslim soldiers led by Lt. Ahmad Khatib mutinied to form the 'Lebanese Arab Army,' attacked the presidential palace, and President Franjieh fled to the mountains. Syria intervened militarily against the Lebanese National Movement in late May 1976. The US Congressional Research Service estimates that deaths in the 1975–1976 phase 'may have reached 44,000' with approximately 180,000 wounded. For the entire war period (1975–1990), about 150,000 are estimated to have died and approximately 17,000 to have gone missing (Al Jazeera 2018; no public databases or precise tallies exist). In November 2018, parliament passed a law establishing a national committee to investigate the fate of the disappeared; the International Committee of the Red Cross recorded about 3,000 missing persons. The 1991 amnesty law covered crimes committed before March 28, 1991.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths (1975–76)UnconfirmedApproximately 44,000 possible (US Congressional Research Service)
Deaths (entire 1975–90 period)UnconfirmedApproximately 150,000 (estimates including Al Jazeera 2018)
MissingUnconfirmed (no national registry)Estimated at approximately 17,000 / ICRC records approximately 3,000

Data Record: COW records only the 1958 (first), 1983–84 (fourth, 1,368), and 1989–90 (fifth, 2,400) phases as Lebanese civil wars; data for the 1975–76 phase are absent. UCDP 260 records activity for 10 years within 1958–1990; NAVCO 'leftists' records violence and failure for 1975–1990; P&T records a failed coup on 1976-03-12; Archigos records the assassination of President Muawad in 1989 (ouster and death at the hands of rebels).

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Lebanon: The Civil War (1987) · Government Report · Orientation: US Congressional Research Service (1987), US government publication — https://countrystudies.us/lebanon/27.htm
  2. Al Jazeera — Lebanon aims to resolve pain over 17,000 missing since civil war (2018-11-14) · Media · Orientation: Qatari state-funded international broadcaster; substantial weight given to perspectives of organizations representing the disappeared — https://www.aljazeera.com/news/2018/11/14/lebanon-aims-to-resolve-pain-over-17000-missing-since-civil-war

Cedar Revolution — Hariri's Assassination and Syrian Troop Withdrawal Protests · 2005

Former Prime Minister Rafiq Hariri was killed in a Beirut bombing on February 14, 2005, and the funeral on February 16, attended by at least 150,000 people, turned into anti-Syrian protests. On February 28, Prime Minister Omar Karami resigned, and on March 5, Syrian President Bashar al-Assad announced a phased withdrawal. On March 8, Hezbollah held a large pro-Syrian rally in downtown Beirut, and on March 14, hundreds of thousands gathered at Martyrs' Square demanding an international investigation into Hariri's assassination, the dismissal of pro-Syrian security chiefs, and the complete withdrawal of Syrian troops. The last Syrian troops left on April 26 (Al Jazeera chronology). The two rallies subsequently became the names of political blocs: the 'March 8' and 'March 14' camps.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Scale of March 14 RallyUnconfirmedHundred of thousands (Al Jazeera; organizers also pre-announced hundreds of thousands)
Funeral AttendanceUnconfirmedAt least 150,000 (Al Jazeera)

Data Record: NAVCO 'Cedar Revolution' 2005 nonviolent and successful, MM 7 events in 2005. The UN report records withdrawal for May 2005 (verified 5-23), differing from Al Jazeera's 4-26 (search summary, unverified).

  1. Al Jazeera — Key events after al-Hariri's death (2005-06-02) · Media · Orientation: Qatari state-funded international broadcaster — https://www.aljazeera.com/news/2005/6/2/key-events-after-al-hariris-death
  2. Al Jazeera — Thousands rally against Syria in Beirut (2005-03-14) · Media · Orientation: Qatari state-funded international broadcaster — https://www.aljazeera.com/news/2005/3/14/thousands-rally-against-syria-in-beirut

October 17 Revolution (Touaour) Protest · 2019–2020

On October 17, 2019, nationwide protests broke out with tens of thousands of peaceful demonstrators after the cabinet announced tax measures to address the economic crisis (including a levy on internet voice calls such as WhatsApp). Protesters demanded socioeconomic rights, accountability, an end to corruption, and the resignation of all political representatives. Amnesty recorded security forces using batons, tear gas, rubber bullets, and occasionally live ammunition and shotgun shells; on October 26 in Beddawi, at least two people were seriously injured from military gunfire, and hundreds were arrested in Sur, Zahlé, Jbeil, Beirut, and Beddawi. The cabinet resigned. Following the Beirut port explosion on August 4, 2020 (at least 190 dead, over 6,500 injured), more than 230 people were injured in subsequent protests.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
ArrestsUnconfirmedHundreds (Amnesty)
Injuries in Post-2020-08 Explosion ProtestsUnconfirmedMore than 230 (Amnesty)

Data Record: NAVCO 'October Revolution' 2019 nonviolent and successful (Hariri government resignation), MM 62 events in 2019 (29 violent), ACLED protests 418 in 2018 → 3,400 in 2019.

2. Pre-Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

Expulsion of the Portuguese and rule by the Howayla tribe; the expedition to Bushehr Portuguese, Safavid, and Arab tribal rivalry · 1507–1753

The claim put forward by the Iranian government until 1970 was that 'Bahrain had been part of Persia continuously except for the period of Portuguese occupation from 1507 to 1622.' The Encyclopaedia Iranica notes that while Shah Abbas of the Safavid dynasty expelled the Portuguese in 1622, effective Persian control was not established until 1753 — in that year Sheikh Nasser of Bushire sent an expeditionary force to seize the archipelago from the Arab Howeitat tribe. This period marked a time when sovereignty over the islands passed between external empires and coastal Arab tribes; in the remaining public records, accounts of regime changes rather than individual uprisings by residents are preserved. The Iranian account reads this period as a continuation of Persian suzerainty, whereas the Arab and Bahraini accounts view it as a period of tribal autonomy.

  1. Encyclopaedia Iranica — BAHRAIN iii. History of political relations with Iran · Academic encyclopedia · Orientation: Iranian Studies academic encyclopedia (Columbia University Center for Iranian Studies), citing both Iranian claims and British and Arab rebuttals — https://www.iranicaonline.org/articles/bahrain-iii

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

Conquest of Bahrain by the al-Utub tribe 1783

According to the Encyclopaedia Iranica, in 1783 the Arab al-Utub tribe led by Ahmad al-Khalifa reconquered Bahrain (ending Persian rule from Bushire). Thereafter, Britain concluded a treaty with al-Khalifa in 1820 and controlled Bahrain's external relations through treaties in 1847, 1856, and 1861 and 'exclusive' agreements in 1880 and 1892. Iran did not recognize this conquest or subsequent British protection and claimed sovereignty over the islands again in 1927, 1952, and 1956. In the narrative of the Bahraini monarchy, 1783 marks the founding of the state; in Iran's former official account, it marks the beginning of territorial loss.

  1. Encyclopaedia Iranica — BAHRAIN iii. History of political relations with Iran · Academic encyclopedia · Orientation: Iranian Studies academic encyclopedia, citing Lorimer's Gazetteer (pp. 836-999) — https://www.iranicaonline.org/articles/bahrain-iii

5. Modern Events through the Literature

The National Unity Committee (CNU) Movement and the 1956 Strike and Clashes Protests · 1954–1956

According to Human Rights Watch (HRW) 1997 report, in 1954 Shia and Sunni leaders jointly formed the Committee of National Unity to negotiate with the ruling family for reforms; however, the negotiations broke down. In 1956, during the Suez Crisis, strikes and clashes peaked, and the authorities exiled five committee leaders to Saint Helena. The government and British sides regarded this as a measure to restore order, while later accounts of the movement record it as the first reform movement transcending sectarian lines.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Exiled LeadersUnconfirmed5 exiled to Saint Helena (HRW 1997)
DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data Record: Not included in COW, UCDP, NAVCO, or MM (MM starts in 1990).

  1. Human Rights Watch — Routine Abuse, Routine Denial: Civil Rights and the Political Crisis in Bahrain (1997-06) · Human rights organization report · Orientation: International human rights organization (US-based), critical of government — https://www.hrw.org/reports/1997/bahrain/

March 1965 Labor and Political Unrest Uprising · 1965

The HRW 1997 report notes that labor and political unrest occurred in 1965, with those regarded as leaders being arrested and forcibly exiled. The report does not provide a death toll. Bahrain was then a British protectorate.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed (HRW 1997 reports no figures)

Data Record: Not included in COW, UCDP, or NAVCO. Because this is an event prior to independence, it does not appear under Bahrain's country code.

  1. Human Rights Watch — Routine Abuse, Routine Denial (1997-06) · Human rights organization report · Orientation: International human rights organization (US-based), critical of government — https://www.hrw.org/reports/1997/bahrain/

December 1981 Plot to Overthrow the Regime Uncovered Coup · 1981–1982

On December 13, 1981, the Bahraini government announced the arrest of a 'subversive' group trained in Iran. The Minister of Interior stated that they were affiliated with the Iranian-based Islamic Front for the Liberation of Bahrain and had planned to assassinate officials (Encyclopaedia Iranica, citing Ramazani 1986). HRW's 1997 report notes that approximately 73 individuals were arrested on charges of plotting to overthrow the state with Iranian assistance, and in May 1982 sentences were handed down: three life sentences, 59 years' imprisonment for 59 individuals, and 7 years' imprisonment for 10; it describes this as a 'conspiracy charge' rather than an attempted coup. The Iranian side denied involvement, and Iranica notes that no Iranians were arrested.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Arrests'60 members' (announced by Bahraini Minister of Interior, cited by Iranica)Approximately 73 (HRW 1997)
SentencesUnconfirmedLife 3, 15 years 59, 7 years 10 (HRW 1997)

Data Record: Because Powell & Thyne's coup list includes only executed attempts, this event at the conspiracy stage is not included (empty coups_pt list).

  1. Encyclopaedia Iranica — BAHRAIN iii · Academic encyclopedia · Orientation: Iranian studies academic encyclopedia — https://www.iranicaonline.org/articles/bahrain-iii
  2. Human Rights Watch — Routine Abuse, Routine Denial (1997-06) · Human rights organization report · Orientation: International human rights organization, critical of government — https://www.hrw.org/reports/1997/bahrain/

Uprisings of the 1990s — Petitions for the Restoration of Parliament and Unrest Uprising · 1994–1999

After the government dissolved parliament by decree in August 1975 (the first election was held in December 1973) and allowed indefinite detention up to three years without trial under the National Security Measures Law of 1974, more than 280 individuals—including merchants, lawyers, and writers—signed a petition demanding the restoration of parliament in November 1992. In mid-1994, a second 'popular petition' was signed by 20,000 to 25,000 people within a month, most of whom were Shia. Unrest began in December 1994; two men died in Sanabis from live ammunition, and one civilian and one police officer also died. The US embassy estimated that 500–600 people had been detained by the end of December and that hundreds more were detained in January 1995. By early March 1996, 24 people had died since December 1994, including three police officers (HRW 1997). The government described the unrest as violent acts orchestrated by Iran, while opposition described it as a demand for the restoration of constitutional rule.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Deaths (1994-12 ~ early 1996-03)Unconfirmed24 (including 3 police officers, HRW 1997)
Detentions (end of 1994)Unconfirmed500–600 (US embassy estimate, cited by HRW 1997)
Signatures on the 1994 Popular PetitionUnconfirmed20,000 to 25,000 (HRW 1997)

Data Record: COW and UCDP do not include it because it falls below their thresholds for war and armed conflict; NAVCO 1.3 also lacks a campaign for the 1990s. MM records only one event in 1994 and four in 1996 — fewer events than described in the report (which spans several years).

  1. Human Rights Watch — Routine Abuse, Routine Denial (1997-06) · Human rights organization report · Orientation: International human rights organization, critical of government — https://www.hrw.org/reports/1997/bahrain/

2011 Uprising (Pearl Roundabout) and State of National Security Uprising · 2011

From February 14 to thousands of people gathered at the Pearl Roundabout in Manama and held sit-ins for several weeks (Amnesty International). A state of national safety was declared on March 15 under Royal Decree 2011 No. 18 (BICI Report, Article 180), and the government called in Gulf Cooperation Council (GCC) forces led by Saudi Arabia. The Bahrain Independent Commission of Inquiry (BICI), established by the king, reported in its November 23, 2011 report that 35 deaths were associated with unrest from February 14 to April 15 (Article 848), attributing the deaths of 13 civilians to security forces (Interior Ministry 10, Defense Forces 2, etc.) and the deaths of three police officers to protesters. It also addressed separately 11 deaths whose connection to the events was possible (Article 849). Complaints related to dismissals and discharges numbered 1,624 (Articles 1337 and 1356). Amnesty summarizes that BICI concluded at least 19 people died at the hands of authorities in February–April, including five from torture, and notes that the largest legal opposition parties, al-Wefaq and Wa'ad, were subsequently banned. The government stated it had partially or fully implemented BICI recommendations, while Amnesty finds they have not been implemented.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Deaths (2011-02-14 ~ 04-15)35 (BICI report established by the government, Article 848)At least 19 deaths at the hands of authorities, including 5 from torture (Amnesty 2021, citing BICI)
Additional Deaths Possibly Linked11 (BICI Article 849)Unconfirmed
Complaints of Dismissals and Discharges1,624 (BICI Article 1356)Unconfirmed

Data Record: NAVCO 1.3 records it as the 'Anti-King Hamad Campaign' (2011–2019, nonviolent, failed). COW and UCDP do not include it. MM records 8 events in 2011 and 9 in 2012, while ACLED (2016–) records 3,141 and 3,720 protest events in 2016 and 2017, showing that protests continued after the uprising.

  1.   2. Amnesty International — Bahrain: Dreams of reform crushed 10 years after uprising (2021-02-11) · Human rights organization report · Orientation: International human rights organization (UK-based), critical view of the government — https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2021/02/bahrain-dreams-of-reform-crushed-10-years-after-uprising-2/

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Civil War

Narrative survey pending — not yet written.

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

The expansion of the Saud-Wahhabi forces and the fall of Diriyah due to Ottoman and Egyptian campaigns 1765–1818

According to the Library of Congress Country Studies, in 1744 Muhammad ibn Saud and Muhammad ibn Abd al-Wahhab pledged to establish a state based on Islamic principles; by 1765, the army of the House of Saud had established Wahhabi authority and the rule of the House of Saud over most of Najd. His son Abd al-Aziz continued the expansion, sacking Karbala in 1801 and entering Hejaz in 1803 (Mecca and Medina escaped the destruction that Karbala suffered). In 1816, Muhammad Ali of Egypt, acting under Ottoman authority, led a campaign into Hejaz and quickly recaptured Mecca and Medina; after about two years of resistance, Abd al-Aziz, the ruler, fell along with the capital Diriyah in 1818. Saudi accounts portray this period as state-building and religious reform, while Ottoman accounts depict it as the suppression of a rebellion threatening the holy sites.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Saudi Arabia: The Saud Family and Wahhabi Islam (1992) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress Country Study (1992), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/saudi-arabia/7.htm

5. Modern Events through the Literature

Ikhwan Rebellion Uprising · 1927–1930

The U.S. Library of Congress Country Study notes that the Ikhwan, who had aided Abd al-Aziz (Ibn Saud) in his conquests, attacked non-Wahhabi Muslims within the country and sometimes even Wahhabis, invaded Iraq, and opposed modern technology and foreign presence. By the late 1920s most residents sided with Abd al-Aziz, and he defeated the Ikhwan in a major battle in 1929. The government side views this as establishing border order and sovereignty, while the Ikhwan narrative portrays it as resistance against a ruler who had abandoned religious principles.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations/Other
DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed (the U.S. Library of Congress Country Study does not provide figures)

Data Record: NAVCO 1.3 records this as 'Ikhwan rebellion' (1929–1930, violent, failed). The COW civil war list connects this event to Saudi Arabia's code not to the 'Escobar Rebellion of 1929' in Mexico — an apparent error in combining modern data, noted in the anomaly record.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Saudi Arabia: The Rule of Abd al Aziz (1992) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress Country Study (1992), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/saudi-arabia/10.htm

King Saud's Deposition and King Faisal's Assassination Regime Overthrow · 1964–1975

According to the Library of Congress Country Studies, on November 2, 1964, the ulama issued a final fatwa deposing King Saud and proclaiming Faisal king (a succession change within the royal family and the ulama). On March 25, 1975, Faisal was shot and killed by his nephew; the assassin was a brother of a nephew killed in the 1965 television station incident. In both cases, the monarchy itself remained intact.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths1 king (1975)1 king (1975)

Data Record: Archigos 4.1 records Faisal's departure as 'Irregular — Assassination by Unsupported Individual.' The 1964 deposition of Saud is not recorded as an irregular transition in the Saudi Arabia entry of this data (Faisal's accession is 'Regular'). Neither appears in P&T's list of coups.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Saudi Arabia: The Reigns of Saud and Faisal (1992) · Government Report · Orientation: Library of Congress Country Study (1992), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/saudi-arabia/11.htm

Occupation of the Grand Mosque of Mecca Uprising · 1979–1980

In 1979, an armed group occupied the Grand Mosque of Mecca; when the siege ended after about two weeks, official casualties numbered 229 (AP). The 63 individuals convicted of occupying the mosque were collectively executed in January 1980 — this figure was recorded by AP as Saudi Arabia's largest mass execution until 81 executions on March 8, 2022. The government portrayed it as the suppression of a rebellion that desecrated the holy site; the occupiers presented it as a religious uprising against royal corruption and Westernization.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths (at end of siege)229 (official tally, cited by AP)Unconfirmed
Executions (1980-01)63 (AP)Unconfirmed

Data Record: The UCDP/PRIO Armed Conflict Dataset records this as a 1979 government conflict against 'JSM' (targeting state control, 1 year, fewer than 1,000); COW does not record it because it falls below the war threshold.

2. Pre-Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Civil War

Zanjirid al-Ghazzi's Anti-Ottoman Uprising Early Ottoman Damascus Governorate · 1520–1521

The Mamluk Zanjirid al-Ghazzi returned to the Ottomans and became governor of Damascus. When Selim I died in 1520, he declared himself Sultan of Syria on October 31, 1520, styling himself 'al-Malik al-Ashraf Abu'l-Futuh,' and sought alliances among the old ruling class. In early December 1520, he besieged Aleppo; the city held out until Ottoman reinforcements arrived from Anatolia, and the siege ended in failure by late December as he retreated to Damascus. On January 27, 1521, his army was defeated at the Battle of Mastaba near Damascus, and he was captured and executed (Ryzhenkova 2023). The Ottomans subsequently sacked Damascus, and an Ottoman governor took charge of the city (Fanack). From the Ottoman perspective, it was a rebellion of a vassal; from the Mamluk ruling class's perspective, it was an attempt to restore the old dynasty.

  1. Ryzhenkova, T. A. (2023). Vassal Mamluk rulers of Egypt and Syria in the service of the Ottomans. RUDN Journal of World History 15(1): 22–44 · Academic Article · Orientation: Russian academic journal, based on Arab and Ottoman chronicles — https://journals.rudn.ru/world-history/article/view/33864/en_US
  2. Fanack — Syria: the golden age of the Ottoman Empire in the 16th century · Media · Orientation: Dutch-based independent Middle East outlet, overview without date — https://fanack.com/syria/history-of-syria/syria-the-golden-age-of-the-ottoman-empire-in-the-16th-century/

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

Resistance against Ibrahim Pasha (Egypt) Uprising · 1831–1840

Ibrahim Pasha, son of Egyptian ruler Muhammad Ali, occupied Syria in 1831. The Library of Congress Country Studies notes that he lost the support of the landowning class whose influence he had curtailed and the support of peasants who resisted conscription and taxation, and was eventually driven out of Syria by Ottoman forces.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations · Other
DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data record: Not included in COW, UCDP, or NAVCO (events within Ottoman territory; Syria as a state unit prior to its current configuration).

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Syria: Ottoman Empire (1987) · Government Report · Orientation: Library of Congress Country Study (1987), U.S. government publication — http://countrystudies.us/syria/7.htm

The Great Syrian Revolt (Druze Revolt) and the Bombardment of Damascus Uprising · 1925–1927

Under French mandate, Druze forces led by Sultan Pasha al-Atrash occupied Salqin on July 20 and Suwayda, the Druze heartland, on August 2, 1925. In October they entered the Damascus area, and France subjected the city to systematic bombardment. The U.S. Congressional Research Service records that this bombardment killed 5,000 Syrians and describes the rebellion as having 'collapsed by year's end.' Subsequently, unrest following the death of nationalist leader al-Hanud in late 1935 and a general strike in 1936 led to negotiations on the Syrian–French Alliance Treaty in the same year. The French side remembers it as an operation to restore order, while the Syrian nationalist narrative recalls it as the starting point of the struggle for independence.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Casualties from Bombardment of DamascusUnconfirmed (figures not published by French mandate authorities consulted)5,000 (U.S. Congressional Research Service)

Data Record: NAVCO records only the 1920 'Franco-Syrian War' for Syria and lacks the 1925 Great Druze Revolt. It is also absent from the COW civil war list (this data) — because it falls under a mandate, it does not qualify as an internal war of the state. The year of termination diverges in the literature between late 1925 (Library of Congress) and 1927.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Syria: The French Mandate (1987) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Congressional Research Service (1987), published by the U.S. government — http://countrystudies.us/syria/9.htm

Three Coups in 1949 Coups · 1949

On March 30, 1949, General Husni al-Za'im, Chief of the General Staff of the Army, staged Syria's first coup. The U.S. Congressional Research Service notes that he initially received British support, followed by French support; after dissolving the political parties, he was elected president. Less than five months later, on August 14, General Sami al-Hinnawi arrested Za'im and Prime Minister Muhassin al-Barazi, and both were executed following a trial by the War Council. The temporary government of Husayn al-Attasi returned power to civilians, and women voted for the first time in the November 15–16 elections of 1949; however, the study notes that 'the military watched from behind.' On December 19 of the same year, Adib Shishakli took power (Archigos).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
ExecutionsUnconfirmed2 individuals (Za'im and Barazi, U.S. Congressional Research Service)

Data Record: Powell & Thyne coup data begins in 1950 and lacks the three 1949 coups. Archigos records the coups of Qudsi (1949-03-30), Za'im (08-13), and Hinnawi (12-19) as irregular expulsions by the military — the date of the Hinnawi coup differs by one day between Archigos (8-13) and the study (8-14).

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Syria: After Independence (1987) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Congressional Research Service (1987), published by the U.S. government — http://countrystudies.us/syria/11.htm

Coups and Counter-Coups — 1962, 1963, 1966, 1970 Coups · 1962–1970

On March 28, 1962, a military coup led President Qudsi to resign and the General Staff of the Army to seize executive and legislative power; Qudsi returned on April 10. On March 8, 1963, another military coup occurred, and Salah Jadid became prime minister on March 9. On July 17–18, an estimated 2,000 Nasserists attempted a coup, fighting in Damascus for several hours before being suppressed; eight officers and twelve civilians were tried by court-martial and shot on July 19. On February 23, 1966, Jadid and the regional command seized control of the government; the study describes this as the 'most bloody' coup since 1949 and notes that the general public did not fight for any Baath warlord faction. On November 13, 1970, military units arrested Jadid, Atassi, and Jowaynī. The study characterizes this as 'virtually bloodless,' while Hafez al-Assad described it as 'a natural development of the party's revolutionary movement' (government designation: 'Correction Movement').

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Executions in 1963-07Unconfirmed20 individuals (8 officers and 12 civilians, U.S. Congressional Research Service)
Number of Participants in the 1963-07 Coup AttemptUnconfirmedApproximately 2,000 (U.S. Congressional Research Service)
Deaths in 1966-02UnconfirmedUnconfirmed ('most bloody', no figures)

Data Record: P&T records successes on 1961-09-28, 1962-03-28, 1963-03-08, 1966-02-23, and 1970-11-13; failures on 1963-07-18, 1966-09-08, 1968-08-11, and 1982-01-31; and a success on 1969-03-01. UCDP-299 classifies government-vs.-military-faction violence from 1966 onward as armed conflict.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Syria: Coups and Countercoups, 1961–70 (1987) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Congressional Research Service (1987), published by the U.S. government — http://countrystudies.us/syria/15.htm
  2. Library of Congress Country Studies — Syria: The Baath Redirections of 1966 and 1970 (1987) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Congressional Research Service (1987), published by the U.S. government — http://countrystudies.us/syria/17.htm

Muslim Brotherhood Uprising and the Siege of Hama Uprising · 1980–1982

The banned Sunni organization Muslim Brotherhood attempted to assassinate Hafez al-Assad in 1980 (AFP). On February 2, 1982, under a blackout of information, the crackdown on Hama began and lasted for 27 days; it was entrusted to the president's younger brother Rifat al-Assad. The death toll has never been officially confirmed, with estimates ranging from 10,000 to 40,000, and higher estimates also exist (AFP 2025). A U.S. Congressional Research Service report notes that the government stated in 1982 that it had 'crushed an internal rebellion by Muslim Brotherhood insurgents' and records that more than 500 members of the Brotherhood were released under a general amnesty in 1985. The government describes it as the suppression of an armed uprising, while the Brotherhood and survivors describe it as a massacre of urban residents.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Hama DeathsNone announcedEstimated at 10,000–40,000 (AFP 2025; no confirmed figure)
1985 ReleasesUnconfirmedMore than 500 (U.S. Congressional Research Service)

Data Record: NAVCO 'Muslim Brotherhood' violence and failure 1980–1982, P&T failed coup 1982-01-31, UCDP includes 1982 in its active years. The COW civil war list (this data) contains no entry for the 1980–82 Syrian Civil War.

  1. Arab News (AFP) — 40 years on, Hama survivors recall horror of Assad-era massacre (2025-01-31) · Media · Orientation: Published in a Saudi English-language outlet; centered on survivor testimony — https://www.arabnews.com/node/2588519/amp
  2. Library of Congress Country Studies — Syria: 1982–1987 Political Developments (1987) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Congressional Research Service (1987), published by the U.S. government — http://countrystudies.us/syria/57.htm

2011 Uprising — Daraa Protests Protest · 2011

On March 18, 2011, protests began in Daraa after 15 children were detained and tortured on suspicion of writing anti-government slogans on walls. In a June 1, 2011 report, Human Rights Watch (HRW) concluded that security forces used lethal force against protesters and bystanders mostly without warning, and that this was systematic and part of state policy, constituting crimes against humanity. HRW noted that at least 418 people died in Daraa Governorate and over 887 nationwide according to local activist tallies, though exact figures could not be verified. The Syrian authorities claimed that the Daraa protesters initiated violence and attacked security forces. While HRW reported that the protests were largely peaceful, it also documented cases of residents setting vehicles and buildings on fire and killing security personnel, noting that further investigation was needed.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths (2011-03-18 to mid-May, Daraa Governorate)UnconfirmedAt least 418 (local activist tally, cited by HRW)
Deaths (same period, nationwide)UnconfirmedOver 887 (local activist tally, cited by HRW)

Data Record: NAVCO 'Syrian Uprising' nonviolent failure in 2011; MM records only 3 events for 2011 (discrepancy with numerous large protests — coverage gap).

  1. Human Rights Watch — Syria: Crimes Against Humanity in Daraa (2011-06-01) · Human Rights Organization Report · Orientation: International human rights organization; centered on testimony of victims and protesters — https://www.hrw.org/news/2011/06/01/syria-crimes-against-humanity-daraa

Syrian Civil War and the Collapse of the Assad Government Civil War · 2011–2024

The uprising escalated into an internationalized civil war involving the government, multiple rebel factions, IS, and Kurdish-led forces (PYD/SDF). In a June 2022 report, the UN Human Rights Office (OHCHR) estimated that 306,887 civilians were killed between March 1, 2011, and March 31, 2021. Of these, 143,350 are individually documented cases and 163,537 are statistical estimates, averaging approximately 83 per day. In late November 2024, rebels led by Hayat Tahrir al-Sham (HTS) launched an offensive in Aleppo; on December 7 they seized Homs, and on December 8 they entered Damascus, occupying state broadcasters and freeing prisoners from Sednaya Prison. On the same day, two senior officers told Reuters that Bashar al-Assad had left Damascus by plane; the Syrian government did not officially confirm this at the time (Kyiv Independent, citing Reuters).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Civilian Deaths (2011-03 to 2021-03)Unconfirmed (Syrian government published figures not consulted)306,887 (OHCHR 2022: 143,350 individually documented + 163,537 estimated)
Combat DeathsUnconfirmedCOW 120,020 (as of ongoing basis)

Data Record: COW 'Syrian Arab Spring War of 2011 to present' combat deaths 120,020 (not recorded as ended), UCDP 299 (1966–2025), 13042 (PYD), 13604 (IS), 13809 (SDF); NAVCO 'Syrian Civil War' violence and failure 2011–2019 — the 2024-12 government collapse falls outside the scope of NAVCO coverage (2019). ACLED deaths: 54,122 in 2017 → 6,108 in 2021 → 8,835 in 2025.

  1. UN News — Syria: Decade of brutal war left nearly 307,000 civilians dead (2022-06-28) · International Organization Report · Orientation: UN Human Rights Office tally, direct civilian fatalities only — https://news.un.org/en/story/2022/06/1121532
  2. Kyiv Independent — Rebels enter Damascus, Assad flees (2024-12-08) · Media · Orientation: Ukrainian English-language independent outlet, citing Reuters — https://kyivindependent.com/rebels-enter-damascus-assad-flees/

5. Modern Events through the Lens of Literature

Attempted Coup in Sharjah — Assassination of Ruler Khaled Coup · 1972

In 1965, Sheikh Sakr bin Sultan al-Qasimi was ousted from his position as ruler; in early 1972, less than two months after the federation's inauguration, he seized the palace of Sharjah. According to an excerpt from Chapter 31 of Dubai Ruler Mohammed bin Rashid Al Maktoum's memoir Qissati (My Story), Sheikh Zayed (Abu Dhabi · Federal President) ordered him to 'finish quickly,' and he requested troops from Abu Dhabi and redeployed Dubai forces. When he inquired about Khalid, Sakr replied that 'Khalid is dead,' and the memoir states that Sakr left the UAE with his son and followers. Subsequently, Khalid's brother Sultan bin Mohammed al-Qasimi became ruler. This account is a record of the party taking action (the federal side), and the account from Sakr's side could not be verified in this round.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths1 ruler, Khalid, killed (memoir; other death tolls unrecorded)Unconfirmed

Data Record: Recorded as a failure in Powell & Thyne 1972-01-25. Not included in Archigos irregular leader transitions (not a federal unit but a ruler of a sheikhdom). Not included in COW, UCDP, or NAVCO.

  1. Gulf News — Mohammad Bin Rashid, Qissati ch.31 excerpt (updated 2019-02-20) · Media · Affiliation: UAE pro-government daily, excerpt from the memoir of a participant in the suppression (ruler of Dubai) — https://gulfnews.com/amp/story/uae%2Fgovernment%2Foctober-was-long-then-another-innocent-soul-was-killed-shaikh-mohammad-bin-rashid-1.1547740580997

Sharjah Palace Coup — Abdulaziz Declares Sovereignty Coup · 1987

On June 17, 1987, while ruler Sultan bin Mohammed Al Qasimi was on private residence in the UK, his elder brother Abdulaziz bin Mohammed Al Qasimi announced via state news agency WAM that Sultan had stepped down and occupied the ruler's palace for four days with several hundred mercenary loyalists. Sultan's wife immediately denied it, and Sultan returned to Dubai. MERIP (1987) notes that Sharjah's debt was approximately $1 billion (bank estimates of defaulted debt around $800 million; Sultan's advisory team estimated $340 million in international and $540 million in domestic debt). The ruling family of Dubai said they could not accept the situation, and while Zayed of Abu Dhabi initially seemed to support Abdulaziz, he mediated a compromise as the rebels became isolated. On June 21, the Federal Supreme Council approved a compromise restoring Sultan as ruler and granting Abdulaziz the title of Crown Prince. Saudi Arabia and Iran preferred a return to the status quo ante. The Abdulaziz side cited Sultan's poor economic management as justification, while the federal side concluded with the restoration of the ruling order.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed (no casualties mentioned in MERIP)
Sharjah Debt$340 million international + $540 million domestic (Sultan's advisory team)Approx. $1 billion (MERIP)
Guard SizeUnconfirmed2,400, divided into three factions after the crisis (MERIP)

Data Record: Recorded as a failure in Powell & Thyne 1987-06-16 — MERIP's start date of 6-17 differs by one day. Not included in COW, UCDP, NAVCO, or Archigos.

  1. MERIP — Christian Huxley, A Central American Situation in the Gulf (Middle East Report 148, 1987-09-20) · Academic Journal / Research Institute · Affiliation: US Middle East Studies Information Project, progressive critical perspective — https://merip.org/1987/09/a-central-american-situation-in-the-gulf/

'UAE94' Mass Trial and 'UAE84' Reindictment Protest · 2012–2025

According to Human Rights Watch (HRW), 94 people were detained from March 2012 onward, and in the 2013 'UAE94' trial, 69 government critics (including 8 empty seats) were convicted. HRW views these charges as violating freedom of expression, assembly, and association. On July 10, 2024, the Abu Dhabi Federal Appeals Court issued verdicts in the 'UAE84' case, which had tried at least 84 human rights defenders and political opponents. The UAE government charged the defendants with establishing and operating a secret terrorist organization called the 'Committee for Justice and Dignity,' and state news agency WAM stated in a January 6 communique that the trial was an 'open' one. HRW cited an Emirates Detainees Advocacy Center (EDAC) tally indicating that 40 of the 44 known convictions carried life sentences, and noted that at least 60 defendants had already been convicted in 2013 on the same committee case, raising issues of double jeopardy, while public observation was strictly limited. The government describes it as punishment for a terrorist organization, while human rights groups describe it as punishment for peaceful critics.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
UAE94 Convictions (2013)Unconfirmed (judgment not reviewed)69 people, including 8 empty seats (HRW)
UAE84 Life Sentences (2024-07-10)43 people (WAM, search summary — unverified)40 of 44 known convictions (EDAC, cited by HRW)

Data Record: Not included in NAVCO or MM (MM includes only 3 labor and price protests from 2005–2007). ACLED protests also range from 0 to 6 per year in 2017–2024 — judicial repression does not appear in protest event data.

  1. Human Rights Watch — UAE: Unfair Trial, Unjust Sentences (2024-07-10) · Human Rights Report · Affiliation: International human rights organization, perspective of defendants' rights — https://www.hrw.org/news/2024/07/10/uae-unfair-trial-unjust-sentences

2. Pre-Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

The uprising of Vardan Mamikonian and the Battle of Avarayr Sasanian Persian Domination · 450–451 (followed by the Nvarsak Treaty in 484)

When the Sasanian Persians imposed Zoroastrianism on Armenia, sporadic open revolts broke out from around 450, including the burning of Zoroastrian temples and the murder of priests; there was also a small victory for the Armenians against a small Persian detachment in the summer of 450. The crisis reached its peak in May–June 451 on the plain of Avarayr (now Iranian territory). An Armenian force of about 6,000 under Vardan Mamikonian, son of Hamdastap, was defeated by a much larger Persian army equipped with elite 'invincible' troops and war elephants; Vardan himself was killed in battle. However, as the Sasanians encountered military difficulties on their eastern frontier, the Nvarsak Treaty of 484 granted Armenia greater political autonomy and freedom of religion, and Vardan's nephew Vahan became marzpan (governor) in 485. In Armenian church tradition, Vardan is remembered as a martyr; in Persian sources, the revolt is treated as a rebellion of a province. Accounts of the size of the combat forces differ.

  1. World History Encyclopedia — Mark Cartwright, 'Mamikonian Dynasty' (2018-03-01) · Public History DB · Orientation: Non-profit public history encyclopedia (editorial review), secondary literature summary — Narrative centered on Armenian sources (Yeghiazayr) — https://www.worldhistory.org/Mamikonian_Dynasty/

The 8th-Century Great Arab Revolt Centered on the Mamikonian Family Period of Arab Caliphate Rule · Transferred to Arabs in 653 / Rebellions of the 8th Century

The Byzantine Empire, finding the region difficult to govern, transferred Armenia to the Arabs in 653. Several leaders of the Mamikonian family served as rallying points for important rebellions in the 8th century. In 806, the Arabs installed the Bagratuni family as governors, and later as kings of a semi-autonomous Armenian state. The years and scale of individual rebellions are not recorded in the public sources consulted for this round.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Armenia: Early Christianity (1994) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Congressional Library country study (1994), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/armenia/5.htm
  2. World History Encyclopedia — Mark Cartwright, 'Mamikonian Dynasty' (2018-03-01) · Public History DB · Orientation: Non-profit public history encyclopedia (editorial review) — https://www.worldhistory.org/Mamikonian_Dynasty/

The Fall of the Kingdom of Cilicia Amid Internal Rebellions and Court Intrigues Kingdom of Cilician Armenia · 1375

After the independent kingdoms of Armenian mainland fell to Byzantine and Seljuk invasions in the 11th century, an Armenian kingdom arose on the Mediterranean coast of Cilicia. The Library of Congress Country Study states that internal rebellions and court intrigues brought about the fall of this kingdom, which was destroyed by the Mamluks in 1375. The actors and circumstances of the rebellion are not mentioned in this source.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Armenia: The Middle Ages (1994) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Congressional Library country study (1994), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/armenia/6.htm

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

Revolutionary Party Activities of Ottoman Armenians and the 1890s Massacres Uprising · 1890s–1896

Armenian revolutionary parties formed in Russia and Europe in the early 1890s sent operatives into Ottoman territory to operate. The U.S. Congressional Research Service states that a special order from the Ottoman government in 1895 resulted in the massacre of 300,000 Armenians. The Ottoman government at the time explained this as the suppression of a rebellion by revolutionary forces, while the Armenian side and most studies treat it as an organized massacre by the state. The basis for calculating the number of deaths is not stated in this source.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Deaths in 1895 MassacresUnconfirmed (official Ottoman government tally unconfirmed)300,000 (U.S. Congressional Research Service)

Data record: Because the event took place within Ottoman territory, it is not captured by COW or UCDP's Armenian country codes (Armenia as a country unit has been 1991 and later).

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Armenia: National Self-Awareness (1994) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Congressional Research Service (1994), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/armenia/8.htm

Seizure of Church Property by the Russian Empire and the 1905 Armenian-Tatar Conflict Uprising · 1903–1905

Russian imperial authorities closed Armenian churches and schools in 1903 and confiscated church property. The U.S. Congressional Research Service states that Tatars (Azerbaijanis) massacred Armenians in several cities during the 1905 Russian Revolution. Accounts from the same period in Azerbaijani narratives treat it as mutual violence by both sides. The scale of resistance to the confiscation and the number of deaths in 1905 are not stated in this source.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Deaths in 1905UnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data record: Although after 1816, because it was an event within the Russian Empire, there is no Armenian unit in COW civil war or NAVCO.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Armenia: National Self-Awareness (1994) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Congressional Research Service (1994), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/armenia/8.htm

The Karabakh Movement — Popular Protests and Independence at the End of the Soviet Era Protest · 1988–1991

In 1988, Armenians in Nagorno-Karabakh held large-scale protests demanding the region's unification with Armenia. In the industrial city of Sumgait northwest of Baku, two days of riots left more than 100 Armenians dead. Following a major earthquake that struck northern Armenia in December 1988, Soviet authorities arrested the leadership of the Karabakh Committee amid ensuing chaos. In August 1989, Karabakh Armenians elected their own National Council, declaring secession from Azerbaijan and incorporation into Armenia, while the Supreme Soviet of Azerbaijan abolished the autonomy of both Karabakh and Nakhchivan. When Moscow returned direct rule over Karabakh to Azerbaijan in November 1989, the Armenian Supreme Soviet declared Karabakh part of Armenia in December. In January 1990, the entry of Soviet troops into Azerbaijan was officially justified as a means to prevent pogroms against Armenians in Baku. In September 1991, more than 99% of voters approved independence in a referendum; on September 23, the Supreme Soviet declared full independence; and in October, Ter-Petrosyan was elected president with an overwhelming vote share. The Armenian side describes this movement as self-determination, while the Azerbaijani side characterizes it as separatism motivated by territorial preservation.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Armenian Deaths in Sumgait (1988)Unconfirmed (Soviet authorities' official announcement unconfirmed)More than 100 (U.S. Congressional Research Service)
Support for the 1991 Independence ReferendumMore than 99% (Announced by the Republic of Armenia; cited by U.S. Congressional Research Service)Unconfirmed

Data Record: MM (Clark & Regan) includes only years after 1990 and lacks the 1988–1989 Karabakh protests, which predate the Armenian state unit. In NAVCO 1.3 as well, the 1988 Karabakh movement is absent from the Armenia entry.

  3. Library of Congress Country Studies — Armenia: The Karabakh Crisis Escalates, 1989 (1994) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Congressional Research Service (1994), published by the U.S. government — https://countrystudies.us/armenia/16.htm
  4. Library of Congress Country Studies — Armenia: Independence (1994) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Congressional Research Service (1994), published by the U.S. government — https://countrystudies.us/armenia/18.htm

October 27, 1999, Gunfire at the National Assembly Coup · 1999

On October 27, 1999, five heavily armed gunmen stormed the main chamber of the National Assembly and killed eight prominent politicians, including Prime Minister Vazgen Sargsyan and National Assembly Speaker Karen Demirchyan. The mastermind was former journalist Nairi Hunanyan, and the gunmen claimed they were staging a coup. After negotiating with President Robert Kocharian overnight, they surrendered and were sentenced to life imprisonment in 2003. Hunanyan claimed he acted alone, but some family members and supporters suspected a hidden hand, which those named as suspects denied.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths8 (court and official announcements, cited by RFE/RL)8 (RFE/RL)

Data Record: Not found in Powell & Thyne (P&T) coup list or Archigos irregular transfers — the president remained in office, and the prime minister's death did not lead to seizure of power, so it appears to have been treated outside the definition of a coup (candidate for omission).

  1. RFE/RL — 'Ten Years Later, Deadly Shooting In Armenian Parliament Still Echoes' (2009) · Major Media · Orientation: U.S. government-funded international broadcaster (RFE/RL) — https://www.rferl.org/a/Ten_Years_Later_Deadly_Shooting_In_Armenian_Parliament_Still_Echoes/1862158.html
  2. RFE/RL — 'The Deadly 1999 Attack On Armenia's Parliament' · Major Media · Orientation: U.S. government-funded international broadcaster (RFE/RL) — https://www.rferl.org/a/1875215.html

Disputed Election Protests and March 1 Bloodshed in the 2008 Presidential Election Protest · 2008

In the February 19, 2008 presidential election, the official results were Serzh Sargsyan with 52.8% and Levon Ter-Petrosyan with 21.5%, and Ter-Petrosyan alleged widespread fraud. A continuous sit-in began on Freedom Square on February 21 and lasted approximately nine days. On March 1, police dispersed the sit-in in the early morning; crowds regrouped near the Miasnikyan monument and the French embassy, and clashes broke out from the afternoon onward, with police vehicles set ablaze; police used tear gas and flash grenades to enter around 9:30 p.m., and clashes continued until around midnight. President Kocharian declared a state of emergency for 20 days that night (lifted on March 21), and the Constitutional Court rejected the challenge on March 8. Human Rights Watch observed that police used excessive force in several scenes while also noting instances when police were attacked. The authorities maintain that the protests were illegal and that the police protected constitutional order and public safety.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths10 (8 protesters, 2 police — figures matching official tallies cited by HRW)At least 10 (8 protesters, 2 police and security personnel) — HRW
InjuriesUnconfirmedOver 130 (HRW)
ArrestsUnconfirmedOver 100 (HRW)

Data Record: Recorded in NAVCO 1.3 'March 1st Movement' (2007–2009, nonviolent, partial success). MM records 6 events in 2008, including 1 violent protest; the state response includes 'killings'.

  1. Human Rights Watch — 'Democracy on Rocky Ground: Armenia's Disputed 2008 Presidential Election, Post-Election Violence' (2009-02-25) · Human rights organization report · Orientation: International human rights organization (HRW), perspective on state use of force — https://www.hrw.org/report/2009/02/25/democracy-rocky-ground/armenias-disputed-2008-presidential-election-post-election

Sasna Tsrer's occupation of the Erebuni Police Unit Uprising · 2016

On July 17, 2016, members of the armed group 'Sasna Tsrer' (named after the Armenian epic 'The Warriors of Easter') attacked the Erebuni Police Patrol Unit in Yerevan by ramming its main gate with a truck; three police officers died in the clash. The 31 militants occupied the building for 15 days before surrendering. They were charged with theft of police property, hostage-taking, and other offenses; two of them faced charges of killing three police officers. The militants claimed to be rebels aiming to overthrow the government and refused to plead guilty. On June 5, 2018, the group announced that it had ended armed struggle and would support the Pashinyan government. The government describes this as an armed terrorist hostage-taking; the group and its supporters describe it as an armed uprising.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Police Deaths3 (indictment details, cited by CivilNet)3 (CivilNet)
Occupying MilitantsUnconfirmed31 (CivilNet)

Data Record: Not recorded due to the UCDP armed conflict data gap (no entry based on 25 combat deaths). MM records 3 violent protests out of 4 events in 2016 — appears to be a protest supporting the occupation, but cross-check with MM entries unconfirmed.

  1. CivilNet — 'Sasna Tsrer Calls off Armed Struggle and Will Support Nikol Pashinyan' (2018-06-06) · Major media · Orientation: Independent Armenian online outlet (civil society leaning) — https://civilnet.am/en/news/382534

The 2018 'Velvet Revolution' — Sargsyan's Resignation as Prime Minister Overthrow of the Regime · 2018

After the 2015 constitutional amendment made the prime minister the head of executive power, Serzh Sargsyan, who had completed his presidential term, sought to become prime minister. In response, MP Nikol Pashinyan began a walking protest in Gyumri on March 31 and reached Yerevan on April 13. When Sargsyan was elected prime minister on April 17, protests and arrests expanded; 80 people were arrested that day, and by noon on the 18th, 66 had been arrested; by April 22, the number of those arrested had reached approximately 230. Around 4:30 PM on April 23, Sargsyan posted his resignation letter on a website. On May 8, parliament elected Pashinyan prime minister by 59 to 42 votes, and the protests ceased. Researchers describe this as an instance of popular civil disobedience used as a strategy in post-Soviet space.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
ArrestsUnconfirmed (official police tally unconfirmed)Approximately 230 (by April 22, Global Nonviolent Action Database)
DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data Record: Recorded in NAVCO 1.3 '2018 Anti-government protests' (nonviolent, success); MM records it as 1 of 18 events in 2018 (0 violent). ACLED recorded 369 protests in 2018. Archigos and P&T do not record this as a regular transfer of power.

5. Modern Events through the Literature

Popular Front wave of strikes and Soviet Army entry into Baku in January 1990 ('Black January') Uprising · 1989–1990

Under pressure, the Azerbaijani Communist Party authorities legalized the Azerbaijan Popular Front (APF) in 1989. In the fall of 1989, the APF led a wave of protest strikes against communist rule, and riots broke out in border areas with Iran toward the end of the year. Moscow sent internal security forces, KGB, and military troops to suppress these riots, declaring martial law; the crackdown also targeted opposition groups including the APF. Soviet troops entered Baku on the night of January 19, 1990. The Soviet leadership officially explained this as a measure to prevent pogroms against Armenians in Baku; the Azerbaijani government commemorates January 20 as a day of civilian massacres. A Human Rights Watch report cited in U.S. congressional records characterized the violence of the Soviet troops on the night of January 19–20 as 'collective punishment'.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations and Other Sources
DeathsUnconfirmed (original text unverified due to certificate error on the Azerbaijani Ministry of Foreign Affairs announcement page; Soviet authorities' announcements also unconfirmed)Over 130 (U.S. House Record 2018-02-26, remarks by Rep. Steve Cohen)
Wounded, Arrested, and MissingUnconfirmedOver 700 wounded, 841 arrested, 5 missing (U.S. House of Representatives Record 2018-02-26)

Data Records: MM (Clark & Regan) records only from 1991 onward, so the 1989–1990 events are absent. In NAVCO 1.3, the APF 1989–1990 campaign is also absent from the Azerbaijan entry. COW and UCDP do not record events on Soviet territory prior to 1991.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Azerbaijan: Demands for Sovereignty and the Soviet Reaction (1994) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress Country Study (1994), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/azerbaijan/12.htm
  2. U.S. Congressional Record — Extensions of Remarks, Rep. Steve Cohen, 'Black January' (2018-02-26) · Government Report · Orientation: Record of remarks by a U.S. House Representative (memorial remarks, closer to the Azerbaijani account) — https://www.congress.gov/115/crec/2018/02/26/164/34/modified/CREC-2018-02-26-pt1-PgE222-2.htm

The Overthrow of Mutalibov and the Popular Front's Seizure of Government Buildings in 1992 Regime Overthrow · 1992

President Ayaz Mutalibov resigned under pressure from opposition parties. In May 1992, pro-Mutalibov forces within the Supreme Soviet restored him to the presidency; crowds led by the APF then stormed the government buildings in Baku. Subsequently, in the presidential election, Popular Front leader Abulfaz Elchibey won over 59% of the vote and was elected in a field of five candidates.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Elchibey's Vote Share in the 1992 Presidential ElectionOver 59% (official results, cited by U.S. Library of Congress Country Study)Unconfirmed
DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data Record: Archigos 4.1: Mutalibov returned to power on 1992-05-14–05-18 through an irregular exit (mass protests without foreign support, followed by exile), and Gurbanov entered power irregularly on 1992-05-19. Not present in NAVCO or P&T.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Azerbaijan: Political Instability (1994) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress Country Study (1994), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/azerbaijan/15.htm

The 1993 Military Officers' Uprising and Elchibey's Escape from Baku Coup · 1993

In 1993, an uprising led by disgruntled military officers broke out, and President Elchibey had to leave Baku in mid-June 1993. Seizing the opportunity of the ensuing chaos, Heydar Aliyev returned to power, and elections in October 1993 confirmed him as president. The country study does not name the uprising's leader by name. Elchibey's camp describes it as an expulsion by armed rebels, while the new government describes it as a restoration of order.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data Record: Archigos 4.1: Elchibey was ousted on 1993-06-18 'by foreign-supported rebels'. UCDP 396 records it as government vs. 'forces of Suret Huseynov and OPON' (1993–1995, contested control of the state). P&T does not record 1993 as a coup but only the coup attempts of 1994-10-04 and 1995-03-13 (both failed) — the same event is classified by Archigos as an ouster by rebels and by UCDP as armed conflict.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Azerbaijan: Political Instability (1994) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress Country Study (1994), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/azerbaijan/15.htm

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

The Zaidi Imamate's Anti-Ottoman Uprising and the Expulsion of the Ottomans Ottoman Rule and the Zaidi Imamate · Late 16th Century–First Half of the 17th Century

The BBC chronology states that the Ottomans incorporated part of Yemen into their empire in the 1500s and were expelled in the 1600s. The force leading the expulsion was the Zaidi imamate of the northern highlands, which subsequently ruled North Yemen until the 20th century. Ottoman accounts frame this as a secession of a province undergoing change, while Yemeni accounts portray it as a struggle for self-reliance against foreign rule. The year the uprising began (1597) and the year of Ottoman withdrawal (1635 or 1636) could not be verified from original texts in permitted sources in this round, so they are retained as unverified items.

  1. BBC News — Yemen profile: Timeline · Media · Orientation: UK public broadcaster, timeline summary (detailed sources not presented) — https://www.bbc.com/news/world-middle-east-14704951

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

No documentary record for this period or pending investigation.

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

Ottoman Reoccupation and the Establishment of Imam Yahya's Regime Uprising · 1849–1918

According to the BBC chronology, Aden came under British rule in 1839; the Ottomans returned to the north in 1849; and when the Ottoman Empire collapsed in 1918, North Yemen became independent and was ruled by Imam Yahya. Archigos 4.1 records Yahya's accession as June 4, 1904. The course of the anti-Ottoman uprising by imamist forces from 1904 to 1911 could not be verified from original texts in this round.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations · Other
DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data record: Neither the COW civil war list nor NAVCO 1.3 includes the anti-Ottoman uprising of the imam's forces in Yemen in 1904–1911 (it may have entered under an Ottoman state code — not checked in this round).

  1. BBC News — Yemen profile: Timeline · Media · Orientation: UK public broadcaster — https://www.bbc.com/news/world-middle-east-14704951

1948 Sanaa Uprising — Assassination of Imam Yahya Overthrow of the Regime · 1948

The BBC chronology notes that Yahya was assassinated in 1948, and his son Ahmad succeeded him after defeating 'opponents of feudal rule.' Archigos 4.1 records both Yahya's departure (1948-02-17) and the subsequent accession of al-Wazir as ousted 'by rebels (no external support).' The uprising side claimed constitutional reform, while the imam's side claimed restoration of legitimate succession.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
DeathsUnconfirmedCOW combat deaths 2,000 (dataset value)

Data record: COW records 'Sanaa Revolt of 1948' (combat deaths 2,000); NAVCO 1.3 records 'Yahya Family revolt' (1948, violence, success). Powell & Thyne's coup list begins in 1950 and lacks this event.

  1. BBC News — Yemen profile: Timeline · Media · Orientation: UK public broadcaster — https://www.bbc.com/news/world-middle-east-14704951

The 1962 Revolution and the North Yemen Civil War Civil War · 1962–1970

According to the BBC chronology, Imam Ahmad died in 1962 and was succeeded by his son, but military officers seized power and established the Yemen Arab Republic, which escalated into a civil war between Saudi-backed royalists and Egyptian-backed republicans. The republicans won in 1970. Republican accounts portray this as a revolution, while royalist accounts depict it as a rebellion against the legitimate Imamate.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
DeathsUnconfirmedCOW combat deaths 53,500 (dataset value)

Data record: P&T records the 1962-09-27 coup (success); Archigos records al-Wazir's departure as 'ousted by the military'; COW records the 'North Yemen War of 1962-1970' (internationalized civil war); and NAVCO records the 'Royalists' campaign, each separately.

  1. BBC News — Yemen profile: Timeline · Media · Orientation: UK public broadcaster — https://www.bbc.com/news/world-middle-east-14704951

The Aden Armed Struggle for Independence and the Establishment of South Yemen Uprising · 1963–1969

The BBC chronology notes that Britain withdrew from the south in 1967 after years of armed struggle for independence, and the former territory was integrated into the Yemen Arab Republic; in 1969, a communist coup changed the country's name to the Yemen Arab Democratic Republic and shifted its orientation toward the Soviet bloc.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data record: NAVCO 1.3 records 'FLOSY, NLF in Aden' (independence struggle) and 'Yemeni insurgency' (1955–1957, failed). Archigos records Ali Nasser Muhammad's departure in 1969 as 'ousted by another government actor.'

  1. BBC News — Yemen profile: Timeline · Media · Orientation: UK public broadcaster — https://www.bbc.com/news/world-middle-east-14704951

The 1986 Aden Power Struggle (January 13 Incident) Civil War · 1986

The BBC chronology notes that thousands died in the southern power struggle of 1986, the first-generation leaders virtually stepped down, and Haider al-Abbas al-Atrash took power to pursue unification. Al Jazeera (2015) describes this as a 12-day civil war in 1986 that left thousands dead. Both Ali Nasir Muhammad's and Abd al-Fattah Ismail's sides claim the other initiated the preemptive strike.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
DeathsUnconfirmed'Thousands' (BBC, Al Jazeera) / COW combat deaths 4,000 (dataset value)

Data record: P&T records the 1986-01-13 coup as 'successful'; COW records the 'South Yemen War of 1986' (4,000); NAVCO records a 'leftist' campaign; and Archigos records Ali Nasir's departure as 'ousted by another government actor' — classifying the same event simultaneously as a coup and a civil war.

  1. BBC News — Yemen profile: Timeline · Media · Orientation: UK public broadcaster — https://www.bbc.com/news/world-middle-east-14704951
  2. Al Jazeera — Southern Yemen marks civil war anniversary (2015-01-13) · Media · Orientation: Qatari government-funded broadcaster — https://www.aljazeera.com/news/2015/1/13/southern-yemen-marks-civil-war-anniversary

The 1994 Southern Secession War Civil War · 1994

According to the BBC chronology, the two Yemens unified under Saleh in May 1990 but tensions persisted; in May–July 1994, when Saleh declared a state of emergency and dismissed southern figures including Vice President Ali Salim al-Beidh, they declared southern secession but were defeated by government forces. The government side portrays this as defending unification, while the southern side portrays it as a response to the breach of the unification agreement.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
DeathsUnconfirmedCOW combat deaths 3,000 (dataset value)

Data record: Recorded in COW as 'South Yemeni Secessionist Revolt of 1994' and in NAVCO as 'Democratic Republic of Yemen Secessionist Movement.'

  1. BBC News — Yemen profile: Timeline · Media · Orientation: UK public broadcaster — https://www.bbc.com/news/world-middle-east-14704951

The Houthi Rebellion (the Saada War) Uprising · 2004–2010

The BBC chronology notes that fighting with the northern Shia uprising led by Hussein al-Houthi in June–August 2004 killed hundreds, fighting that recurred in March–April 2005 killed more than 200, Abdul-Malik al-Houthi accepted a ceasefire after clashes in 2007, and an August 2009 government offensive on Sadah displaced tens of thousands. The government side describes it as the suppression of rebellion, while the Houthi side portrays it as resistance against the repression of the Zaidi community.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsUnconfirmedHundreds in 2004, more than 200 in 2005 (BBC) / COW 1st phase 1,000 · 2nd phase 3,000 (dataset values)

Data record: COW records the 1st (2004–2005) and 2nd (2007–2008) Houthi rebellions separately, while NAVCO groups them under 'Al-Houthi Rebellion'.

  1. BBC News — Yemen profile: Timeline · Media · Orientation: UK public broadcaster — https://www.bbc.com/news/world-middle-east-14704951

Anti-Saleh Protests and Transfer of Power in 2011 Protest · 2011–2012

The 2012 World Report by Human Rights Watch records that security forces responded with excessive force to protests in 2011, killing at least 250 people and injuring more than 1,000 (at least 35 of the dead were children), and that at least 45 people died on March 18 when snipers targeted protesters in Sana'a. The GCC mediation proposal offered immunity to Saleh and senior officials in exchange for stepping down. According to the BBC chronology, Saleh agreed in November 2011 to transfer power to Vice President Hadi, and Hadi took office in a sole-candidate election in February 2012.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Protest-Related DeathsUnconfirmedAt least 250 (Human Rights Watch), at least 45 in Sana'a on 3/18

Data record: NAVCO 1.3 'Anti Ali Adduallah Saleh Campaign' (2011–2012, nonviolent, successful). The list of irregular transfers in Archigos 4.1 does not include Saleh's departure in 2012 (appears to have treated the negotiated transfer as a regular transfer). MM protest tallies refer to the generator table.

  1. Human Rights Watch — World Report 2012: Yemen · Human rights report · Orientation: International human rights organization (New York), centered on records of government force use — https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2012/country-chapters/yemen
  2. BBC News — Yemen profile: Timeline · Media · Orientation: UK public broadcaster — https://www.bbc.com/news/world-middle-east-14704951

Houthi Seizure of Sana'a and the Post-2015 Civil War and External Intervention Civil War · 2014–present

According to the BBC chronology, in August 2014 the Houthis seized most of Sana'a and rejected a federal constitution draft; in February 2015 President Hadi fled to Aden; and in March of the same year, a Saudi-led coalition began airstrikes and a naval blockade against the Houthis, marking the full-scale onset of civil war. In January 2018 southern separatist forces backed by the United Arab Emirates seized Aden, and in November 2019 they signed a power-sharing agreement with the government. The UN Office for the Coordination of Humanitarian Affairs (OCHA) estimated in December 2020 that war deaths stood at approximately 233,000 (including indirect causes totaling 131,000), and the UN Development Programme (UNDP) projected in an October 2021 report that if the war continued until 2030, 1.3 million lives would be lost. The Houthi side portrays it as resistance against foreign aggression, while the government and coalition sides portray it as the restoration of a legitimate government.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
War Deaths (Direct and Indirect)Unconfirmed (no official tally by belligerents)Approximately 233,000, including indirect causes totaling 131,000 (OCHA, 2020-12)
Estimated deaths if war continues until 2030—1,300,000 (UNDP, 2021-11)

Data record: COW 'Third Al-Houthi Rebellion of 2014-present' combat deaths of 4,600 — this diverges sharply from OCHA estimates (direct deaths alone are on the order of 100,000). Recorded in NAVCO 'Anti-Houthi Movement' (2015–2019, nonviolent, failed). ACLED data refer to the generator table.

  1. BBC News — Yemen profile: Timeline · Media · Orientation: UK public broadcaster — https://www.bbc.com/news/world-middle-east-14704951
  2. UN News — UN humanitarian office puts Yemen war dead at 233,000 (2020-12-01) · Government report · Orientation: UN official news, OCHA estimate — https://news.un.org/en/story/2020/12/1078972
  3. UN News — Yemen recovery possible if war stops now: UNDP report (2021-11-23) · Government report · Orientation: UN official news, UNDP model estimate — https://news.un.org/en/story/2021/11/1106362

2. Pre-Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

Imam succession disputes and Iranian intervention; expulsion of the Al Said family by Iran Late Yarubid Imamate · Early 18th Century–1742

According to the Library of Congress Country Studies (Persian Gulf States, 1993), the Yarubid family seized Oman's coastal cities around 1650 and entered Muscat, a former Portuguese stronghold. Fighting over the succession in the early 18th century weakened Yarubid rule, and the Yarubids eventually called in Iranian forces, reinstating Iran's influence along the Omani coast. In 1742, the Al Said family, an Ibadhi clan from a coastal city, persuaded the inhabitants to drive out the Iranian forces, and the Ibadhi leadership elevated him as Imam a few years later. The succession dispute is described as overlapping with the opposition of the Hinawi and Gafiri tribal coalition, but the pages consulted in this round do not record the date of that opposition.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Persian Gulf States: The Age of Colonialism (1993) · Government Report · Affiliation: Library of Congress Country Study (1993), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/persian-gulf-states/11.htm

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

The 1913 Imamate Uprising and the 1920 Treaty of Seeb Uprising · 1913–1920

According to the Library of Congress Country Studies, in May 1913 Salim ibn Rashid al-Harthi was proclaimed imam at Tanuf and led a rebellion uniting the Hinawi and Ghafiri tribal groups. Sultan Taimur ibn Faisal, who ascended the throne in October of the same year, inherited 'widespread rebellions among the tribes,' and from 1915 to 1920 the sultan's forces fought the rebel tribes with British financial and material support. In 1920, under the mediation of the British Political Resident in Muscat, a treaty was concluded between the sultan and the tribes (represented by Sheikh Isaa ibn Salih al-Harthi), under which the inland tribes ceased their coastal raids in exchange for complete autonomy. The Country Study describes this as a de facto partition of Muscat and Oman.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data Record: Neither COW Civil War nor NAVCO 1.3 includes the 1913–1920 Imamate uprising under Oman.
