  2. Library of Congress Country Studies — Oman: Taimur ibn Faisal, 1913-32 (1993) · Government Report · Orientation: Library of Congress Country Study (1993), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/persian-gulf-states/59.htm

The Inland Imamate Separatist Movement and British Intervention (the Jebel Akhdar War) Uprising · 1954–1959

According to the Library of Congress Country Studies, in 1954 Imam Muhammad ibn Abd al-Karim al-Khalili died and a new imam was elected; in the same year, when an oil exploration team entered the interior, tribal sheikhs viewed this as a violation of the 1920 Treaty of Seeb. The brother of the new imam sought political and material support from Saudi Arabia, founded the 'Oman Liberation Movement,' and aimed to establish an independent inland state and expel foreign troops. Britain intervened on behalf of the sultan and suppressed the rebels early in 1959; subsequently, it abrogated the treaty and abolished the imamate. The imam's side describes this as the defense of autonomy under the treaty, while the sultan's side describes it as the recovery of territorial unification.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data Record: This conflict is not included in the COW, NAVCO, or UCDP Oman entries (UCDP covers 1946 and later but is not included in this data file).

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Oman: Said ibn Taimur, 1932-70 (1993) · Government Report · Orientation: Library of Congress Country Study (1993), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/persian-gulf-states/60.htm
  2. Library of Congress Country Studies — Oman: Regional and National Security Considerations (1993) · Government Report · Orientation: Library of Congress Country Study (1993), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/persian-gulf-states/102.htm

The Dhofar Rebellion Civil War · 1963/1964–1975

The Library of Congress Country Studies notes that the movement organized under the Dhofar Liberation Front in 1963 erupted into a rebellion inspired by Arab nationalism in 1964. It began as a tribal separatist movement against Sultan Said ibn Taimur but was supported by leftist forces in South Yemen (PDRY), and Marxist leaders took control in 1968, receiving support from the PDRY, the Soviet Union, and China. Its name changed in 1972 and 1974 (People's Front for the Liberation of Oman, PFLO). After 1970, Sultan Qaboos used both economic and military means; Iranian ground forces (up to more than 3,000 troops) and airpower entered Dhofar in 1973 at the request of the Shah of Iran, and the sultan declared victory in December 1975. Following a general amnesty in 1970, up to 2,000 rebels surrendered and were incorporated into the sultan's forces. The rebel side describes it as a liberation struggle against colonial and feudal rule, while the government side describes it as the suppression of an externally supported communist rebellion.

1970 Palace Coup Coup · 1970

According to the Library of Congress Country Study, on July 23, 1970, Qaboos bin Said and marginalized political elites ousted Said bin Taimur in a palace coup; Said retreated to London, where he died in 1972. The security section of the same country study notes that the coup was carried out with the help of British officers.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Oman: Said ibn Taimur, 1932-70 (1993) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress Country Study (1993), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/persian-gulf-states/60.htm

2011 Job and Anti-Corruption Protests Protest · 2011

According to the 2012 World Report by Human Rights Watch, in February 2011 thousands of people began protesting for jobs, an end to corruption, and the dismissal of corrupt high-ranking officials. On February 27, police used tear gas, rubber bullets, and live ammunition against thousands of protesters who attacked and burned a police station in Sohar, killing Abdulrahman al-Ghammari; on April 1, Khalifa al-Alawi (22) was shot in the face with a rubber bullet and died in Sohar. On May 13–14, security forces and the military dispersed sit-ins in Muscat, Sur, and Salalah, detaining hundreds of people for 24 hours in Salalah. The government side described it as law enforcement against public property destruction and road blockades; human rights organizations described it as excessive force and a lack of accountability.

2. Pre-Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

Banu Sakhr's Raid on Damascus Caravan Train Ottoman period Hajj pilgrimage · 1757

The Ottoman Empire paid subsidies to Bedouin sheikhs in the Jordanian desert and entrusted them with the protection of the Hajj caravans traveling from Damascus to Medina and Mecca. In 1757, the Banu Sakhr tribe attacked the Hajj caravan near Dhat al-Hajj in the Jordanian desert, seizing all its supplies. Historian Tyler Kynn (Central Connecticut State University) writes that this raid led to the deaths of 20,000 pilgrims, including an Ottoman princess — many starved or died of thirst after losing their provisions and a drought struck. The figure of 20,000 is a widely repeated estimate and not an exact tally. The Ottoman side attributed it to tribal raids and rebellion, while some studies attribute it to the economic motives of tribes that had lost territory and revenue.

  1. Tyler Kynn, 'Trials and Tribulations on the Early Modern Hajj', MEMOs (Medieval and Early Modern Orients), 2022-07-11 · Academic Commentary · Orientation: Commentary by a university historian; sources for casualty figures are not cited — https://memorients.com/articles/trials-and-tribulations-on-the-early-modern-hajj

5. Modern Events through Literature

Late-Ottoman Bedouin Uprisings and the 1910 Karak Uprising Uprising · 1905, 1910

The Library of Congress Country Studies also notes that 'restive Bedouin frequently rose in revolt' in the late Ottoman period, and records that serious uprisings in 1905 and 1910 were suppressed 'only after considerable difficulty.' The Jordanian Ministry of Culture describes the 1910 Karak uprising as resistance to the Young Turk government's policies of Turkification, increased taxation, and conscription; it names Sheikh Kader al-Mazali and several tribal sheikhs as leaders. Ottoman forces suppressed the uprising and punished the residents, executing some leaders in Damascus and Karak Castle. The Ministry of Culture connects this to later Karak residents appealing to the Sharif of Mecca for help, which it links to the 1916 Arab Revolt — this is a perspective on Jordanian national heritage narrative; Ottoman records treated it as a rebellion suppression.

DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed (no figures in either Ministry of Culture or Library of Congress Country Studies)

  2. Ministry of Culture, Jordan — Karak Uprising 1910 (ثورة الكرك 1910م) · Official Government Data · Orientation: Jordanian government narrative of national heritage, nationalist perspective — https://culture.gov.jo/AR/Pages/ثورة_الكرك_1910م

Adwan Rebellion Uprising · 1923

After the Emirate of Transjordan was established in 1921, Emir Abdullah drew close to Miske al-Fayiz, leader of the Banu Sakhr tribe, and the Adwan tribe of the Baka region resisted this. According to historian Delphine Froment's synthesis, the discontent stemmed from competition with the Banu Sakhr, Hashemite state control over tribal autonomy, taxation including back taxes from 1918–1920 (the Banu Sakhr were exempt), and the appointment of non-Transjordanians to high posts. The movement demanded a constitutional government, a parliament, tax reform, and Transjordanian administration, using the slogan 'Jordan for Jordanians.' Sultan Adwan occupied western outposts with about 300 cavalry and 500 infantry, severed communications, and advanced on Amman on September 15, 1923; he was defeated after about an hour of fighting at Jubayha. Britain supported the government with two armored cars of the Royal Air Force, and the Arab Legion under Peake stood by. The sultan and his sons fled to Jabal Druze in Syria and were pardoned in March 1924. Subsequently, taxes for 1918–1920 were canceled, and all tribes, including the Banu Sakhr, were taxed. Froment notes this as the last case of a tribal leader raising an army to defend autonomy and marching on Amman.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed (no figures in Froment's synthesis)

Data Record: Not included in COW Civil War list (threshold 1,000 combat deaths) or NAVCO. Archigos records Abdullah I's entry as 'imposed by external force.'

  1. Delphine Froment, 'La rébellion Adwan (1923)', Les clés du Moyen-Orient, 2015-06-03 · Academic Commentary · Orientation: French Middle East commentary outlet, written by a researcher in history at the École Normale Supérieure — https://www.lesclesdumoyenorient.com/La-rebellion-Adwan-1923.html

Assassination of Abdullah I Regime Overthrow · 1951

On July 20, 1951, King Abdullah was assassinated as he entered the Al-Aqsa Mosque in Jerusalem for Friday prayers. His grandson, Prince Hussein, was beside him. The Library of Congress Country Studies identifies the assassin as a 'known' Palestinian hired by relatives of the former Grand Mufti of Jerusalem, Hajj Amin al-Husseini (an opponent of Abdullah); he also shot at Hussein before being killed by the king's bodyguards. The same document notes that 'no organized political unrest followed the assassination.' The throne passed to Talal (the new constitution was promulgated in January 1952; he accepted a recommendation to abdicate in August) and then to Hussein, who took the constitutional oath in May 1953.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths1 king, 1 assassin (as described in Library of Congress Country Studies)Unconfirmed

Data Record: Archigos records Abdullah al-Hussein's departure as 'Irregular — assassination by an individual without support.' Not included in the coup data (P&T) — personal assassinations do not fall under the definition of coups.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Jordan: Hashemite Kingdom of Jordan (1989) · Government Report · Orientation: Library of Congress Country Study (1989), published by the U.S. government — https://countrystudies.us/jordan/10.htm

Baghdad Pact Opposition Riots and the 1957 Zarqa Incident Coup · 1955–1957

In December 1955, when the Hashim al-Majali cabinet announced plans to join the Baghdad Pact, three days of protests and riots broke out in Amman. The Arab Legion restored order, Majali's cabinet resigned within about a week, and Jordan did not join the treaty. In March 1956 Hussein dismissed Glubb Pasha as commander of the Arab Legion. In the general election of October 21, 1956, the National Socialist Party won the most votes and Sulayman Nabulsi became prime minister; but in April 1957 Hussein demanded the cabinet's resignation under constitutional authority. Around this time, remarks by the army commander Ali Abu Nuwar were taken as an ultimatum that 'the new cabinet must have the army's approval,' and rumors circulated that the king had been killed at Zarqa. Hussein went to Zarqa with Abu Nuwar to calm the troops; the troops demanded Abu Nuwar's execution, and he left the country the next day. The Ibrahima al-Husayn cabinet then banned all party activity. The Library of Congress Country Studies does not call the event a coup but notes that the loyalty of combat units was decisive — royal accounts have described it as a coup attempt, while Abu Nuwar's account has described it as a mutiny.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data Record: Powell & Thyne record April 13, 1957 and July 1, 1958 as failed coups. The 1958 event is included only in tabular form because the source text for this round (Library of Congress 11.htm) does not cover it.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Jordan: Hussein's Early Reign (1989) · Government Report · Orientation: Library of Congress Country Study (1989), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/jordan/11.htm

Black September — Civil War between the Jordanian Armed Forces and Palestinian Militias Civil War · 1970–1971

After the Six-Day War, Palestinian militant groups (Fatah) grew in strength within Jordan. On June 9, 1970, Prime Minister Rifai and Arafat agreed that Fatah would have freedom of movement and withdraw from major cities, but clashes continued; in early September, guerrillas seized key sites including the Zarqa refinery. On September 6, the PFLP hijacked TWA and Swissair passenger planes. On September 16, Hussein reconfirmed martial law, formed a military cabinet under Major General Muhammad Daoud, and gave full authority to Field Marshal Habis al-Majali. For about ten days, fighting was mainly between the Palestine Liberation Army and the Jordanian Arab Army; Syria sent about 200 tanks to aid Fatah but lost more than half of its armor and withdrew from September 24 onward. A ceasefire was reached on September 25; Hussein and Arafat signed it in Cairo on September 27, and under the Amman Agreement of October 13, Fatah recognized Jordanian sovereignty while the government promised amnesty. In April 1971, the military drove Fatah out of Amman, attacked its last stronghold around Az Zarqan on July 13, and on July 19 the government announced that it had destroyed all northern strongholds and arrested 2,300 of 2,500 Fatah fighters. In November of the same year, the 'Black September' organization assassinated Prime Minister Wasfi al-Tall in Cairo. The Jordanian government remembers the event as the recovery of state sovereignty; the Palestinian side remembers it as a massacre and expulsion.

Combat DeathsUnconfirmedCOW 1,750
Fatah Arrests (1971-07)2,300 of 2,500 (Jordanian government announcement)Unconfirmed

Data Record: COW 'Black September War of 1970' (combat deaths 1,750, internationalized, government victory) and NAVCO 'Palestinian activists' (1970, violence · failure) include the event. The UCDP/PRIO armed conflict data does not include this civil war as a conflict between the Jordanian government and Palestinian groups — a coverage difference between datasets. The 1971 phase is treated in COW as part of the single year 1970.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Jordan: The Guerrilla Crisis (1989) · Government Report · Orientation: Library of Congress Country Study (1989), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/jordan/14.htm

April 1989 Southern Price Riots (Habat Nisan) Protest · 1989

Less than 48 hours after the government raised fuel and most basic commodity prices in April 1989, riots broke out in Ma'an and spread to Karak and Tafileh, reaching Salt, 13 miles west of Amman (bread, sugar, and rice were exempt from the increases). The Christian Science Monitor reported that the increases were part of an International Monetary Fund program to restructure Jordan's $6.5 billion external debt. Security forces, with the help of the military, quickly restored order; King Hussein replaced Prime Minister Zaid al-Khayyat and entrusted a transitional cabinet to Zaid bin Shaker, instructing preparations for the first general election in 22 years. One Jordanian official described it as 'no taxation without representation,' a classical formula. The general election was held in November 1989.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed (reports of 5–6 deaths exist but the texts for this round were not consulted)

Data Record: Mass Mobilization (MM) records Jordan from 1994 onward and does not include the 1989 riots (variations in start years by country in MM). The event is also absent from NAVCO.

  1. Christian Science Monitor — 'Signal King Hussein Can't Ignore. Jordan: Riot Aftermath', 1989-05-01 · Major Media · Lean: US daily, centrist — https://www.csmonitor.com/1989/0501/olami.html

2011–2012 Reform Protests and the November 2012 Fuel Price Riots Protest · 2011–2012

As protests against prices, unemployment, and corruption continued in early 2011, the king dismissed the Samir Rifai cabinet on February 1 and named Marouf al-Bakhit prime minister. On March 24, the 'March 24 Movement,' which demanded reforms, began a sit-in at Amman's roundabout (Dawwar al-Dahiliah), and the following day, counter-protesters carrying King Hussein's picture threw stones; police and gendarmes then dispersed the sit-in with batons and water cannons. A 55-year-old man, Kariy Jami al-Sa'ad, died, and more than 100 were injured. The General Intelligence Directorate stated that 'the area was evacuated for security reasons after clashes on both sides,' and the cause of death was not officially announced. On November 13, 2012, the government announced a 53% increase in household gas prices, an approximately 12% increase in gasoline, and an 11% increase in public transport fares to secure a $2 billion IMF loan and reduce the fiscal deficit; violent protests broke out across 12 governorates, with at least 20 government buildings—including courts—burned. In Irbid, armed assailants attacked a police station, killing one armed civilian and injuring several police officers, and police reported arresting at least 120 people nationwide. Amnesty International recorded on December 10, 2012, that 116 people detained after the protests were released by royal decree.

  4. Amnesty International — Urgent Action MDE 16/001/2013 · Human rights report · Lean: International human rights organization — https://www.amnesty.org/en/wp-content/uploads/2021/06/mde160012013en.pdf

2018 Tax Law Opposition Protest and Demonstrations Protest · 2018

After concluding a three-year $723 million credit agreement with the IMF in 2016, the government pursued reforms to reduce public debt (37 billion dollars, 95% of GDP). Fuel prices rose five times in 2018 alone, and electricity tariffs increased by up to 55%. When the government introduced a bill raising income tax by at least 5%, trade unions such as the teachers' union and professional associations such as the Engineers Association opposed it, and protests continued for four days in Amman and other cities. On June 4, Prime Minister Hani al-Mulki submitted his resignation to the king, who accepted it and appointed Omar al-Razzaz, a former World Bank official, to form a new government. A spokesman for the teachers' union called it 'a change of face,' and protests continued even on the day after the resignation.

DeathsNot reportedNot reported (no casualty figures in Al Jazeera article)

Data Record: ACLED recorded a large increase in Jordan protest events, from 111 in 2017 to 683 in 2018 and 743 in 2019. MM records only one event for 2018 — the aggregation criteria differ significantly between datasets. Not included in NAVCO (up to 2013).

  1. Al Jazeera — 'Jordan PM Hani al-Mulki resigns amid mass protests over tax bill', 2018-06-04 · Major Media · Lean: Qatari government-funded broadcaster — https://www.aljazeera.com/news/2018/6/4/jordan-pm-hani-al-mulki-resigns-amid-mass-protests-over-tax-bill

2. Pre-Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

The Abbasid Revolution — Abu Muslim's uprising and the occupation of Iraq Late Umayyad · 747–750

The Library of Congress Country Studies notes that, supported by Khurasan forces, Abu Muslim's army attacked the Umayyad caliphate and occupied Iraq, and in 750 Abu al-Abbas was installed as caliph. The uprising is described as a movement that gathered the grievances of non-Arab converts outside Umayyad rule and Shi'i supporters. The same document notes that the Abbasid dynasty later made Baghdad its capital. From the Umayyad side, it reads as a rebellion; from the Abbasid narrative, as the restoration of legitimacy.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Iraq: The Abbasid Caliphate, 750-1258 (1988) · Government Report · Orientation: Library of Congress Country Study (1988), published by the U.S. government — https://countrystudies.us/iraq/16.htm

Amin and Mamun Succession War Abbasid Dynasty · 811–813

Harun al-Rashid's two sons, Amin and Mamun, fought for the caliphate. The Library of Congress Country Studies notes that 'Amin had the support of the Iraqis, while Mamun had the support of the Iranians' and records that Mamun occupied Baghdad in 813. No figures on troops or casualties are provided.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Iraq: The Abbasid Caliphate, 750-1258 (1988) · Government Report · Orientation: Library of Congress Country Study (1988), published by the U.S. government — https://countrystudies.us/iraq/16.htm

Zanj Rebellion Abbasid Dynasty · 869–883

Ali ibn Muhammad, a black slave (Zanj) working in the southern Iraqi marshes, gathered his people and established a state. The Library of Congress Country Studies notes that the Zanj 'gained control of much of southern Iraq and southwestern Iran' and that the rebellion was not suppressed until 883, leaving 'great suffering.' No figures are provided. From the Abbasid government side, it is treated as a rebellion; in later scholarship, as an uprising against the slave labor system.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Iraq: The Abbasid Caliphate, 750-1258 (1988) · Government Report · Orientation: Library of Congress Country Study (1988), published by the U.S. government — https://countrystudies.us/iraq/16.htm

The Independence of Basra and Marsh Sheikhs and the Resistance of Bābān Kurds Ottoman Period · Around 1625–1701

The Library of Congress Country Studies notes that, between 1625 and 1668 and again from 1694 to 1701, local sheikhs ruled Basra and the marshlands 'virtually ignoring the Ottoman governor in Baghdad.' During the same period, Bedouins from Najd increased, making attacks on settled areas 'unstoppable,' and the confederation of Muntafiq, Shammar, Anayzah, and Bani Lam tribes expanded their power. In the north, the Bābān dynasty organized Kurdish resistance, leaving the Ottomans unable to maintain even nominal suzerainty over Iraqi Kurdistan. No specific battle or casualty records are present in this document.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Iraq: The Ottoman Period, 1534-1918 (1988) · Government Report · Orientation: Library of Congress Country Study (1988), published by the U.S. government — https://countrystudies.us/iraq/18.htm

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

No literature records for this period or under investigation.

5. Modern Events through the Lens of Literature

The Great Revolution of 1920 (Ath-Thawra al-'Iraqiya al-Kubra) Uprising · 1920

In the spring of 1920, a funeral gathering for a Shia mujtahid turned into a political rally, and anti-colonial uprisings began in late May upon news of the mandate. In July, Mosul rose up, and the uprising spread south along the Euphrates. The U.S. Library of Congress Country Study notes that Sunnis and Shiites, tribes and urban dwellers came together 'for the first time,' but the effects were limited because southern tribes could not mount organized operations. Britain restored order with air bombers and reinforcements from India and Iran; Iraq was in a state of anarchy for about three months. The uprising led to the replacement of the military government with an Arab provisional government under British advisers, which provided the backdrop for the establishment of the Hashemite monarchy at the 1921 Cairo Conference. In Iraqi nationalist accounts, it is treated as an independence revolution; in British records, as the suppression of unrest.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations and Other Sources
DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed (no figures in the U.S. Library of Congress Country Study)

Data Record: Not listed in COW civil war list or NAVCO — this was a period when Iraq did not have a country code as a British mandate.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Iraq: World War I and the British Mandate (1988) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress Country Study (1988), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/iraq/19.htm

1933 Simmele Massacre and 1936 Bakr Sidqi Coup Coup · 1933–1937

In July 1933, about 800 armed Assyrians headed toward the Syrian border but were turned back by Syrian forces. Under General Bakr Sidqi's command, troops killed approximately 300 Assyrians, including women and children, in the Assyrian village of Simmele (Sumayl). Following a decision in 1934 to allocate funds to conscription rather than southern dams, tribal uprisings broke out; Sidqi's forces suppressed them, marginalizing the Shia community. In 1936, Sidqi, along with politicians Hikmat Sulaiman and Abu Tima, overthrew the Hashimi cabinet, which had been in power for 18 months. The Library of Congress Country Studies describes this as 'the first coup in the modern Arab world.' Sidqi was assassinated by a military faction in August 1937. The Assyrian community remembers Simmele as a massacre, while the Iraqi government at the time described it as suppression of rebellion.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Simmele DeathsUnconfirmedApproximately 300 (Library of Congress Country Study)

Data Record: Powell & Thyne coup data begins in 1950 and lacks the 1936 coup. The COW civil war list also lacks the 1933 and 1935–36 events (estimated to fall short of the 1,000 combat deaths threshold).

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Iraq: Iraq as an Independent Monarchy (1988) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress Country Study (1988), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/iraq/20.htm

1941 Rashid Ali Coup and British Intervention Coup · 1941

In April 1941, Rashid Ali and four generals ousted Nuri al-Said and Regent Abd al-Ilah, who fled to Transjordan. The British landed troops in Basra and, with the assistance of the Arab Legion of Transjordan, restored Abd al-Ilah to the regency. A ceasefire was concluded on May 30; the generals were later executed, and Rashid Ali went into exile. Iraqi nationalist accounts portray this as resistance against reflection, while British accounts depict it as a pro-axis coup.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data Record: Not recorded in Powell & Thyne (1950–). UCDP/PRIO does not include it because records begin in 1946.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Iraq: Iraq as an Independent Monarchy (1988) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress Country Study (1988), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/iraq/20.htm

1948 Wathbah and the 1952 Intifada Protests · 1948, 1952

The 1948 Wathbah (leap) erupted in January 1948 as a protest against the Portsmouth Treaty (a new treaty with Britain), coinciding with rising prices and bread shortages. In 1952, protests spread against economic hardship, crop failures, and the absence of direct elections; the government declared martial law, banned all parties, suspended newspapers, and imposed curfews. The Library of Congress Country Study notes that while oil revenues rose from $32 million in 1951 to $112 million in 1952, little of it reached ordinary Iraqis.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data Record: Neither NAVCO nor MM includes it (MM covers only from 1990 onward).

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Iraq: Iraq as an Independent Monarchy (1988) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress Country Study (1988), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/iraq/20.htm

1958–1968 Coup Chain Coup · 1958–1968

Following the fall of the monarchy on July 14, 1958, coups and coup attempts continued for a decade. The Library of Congress Country Study only notes that the Ba'ath Party had a difficult time in the 'Mosul and Kirkuk incidents' of 1959; it records that after taking power in 1963, the Ba'ath was ousted by a 'bloodless coup' by Arif and a group of officers in November 1963, 'not even a year later.' In 1968, Abd al-Razzak al-Naif and Izzat al-Douri successfully overthrew the Arif regime, and the Ba'ath took power within weeks. Coup dates and outcomes follow the Powell & Thyne data table (4 successes, numerous failures).

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
1963-11 Coup DeathsUnconfirmedBloodless (Library of Congress Country Study)
1959 Mosul Uprising Combat DeathsUnconfirmedCOW 1,200

Data Record: P&T records successes on 1958-07-14, 1963-02-08, 1963-11-18, and 1968-07-17, along with failures such as 1959-03-07. The Ba'ath's ouster of al-Naif on 1968-07-30 is not recorded separately in P&T — it appears to be bundled into the 7-17 entry. COW classifies the 1959 Mosul uprising as a civil war with 1,200 combat deaths.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Iraq: Coups, Coup Attempts, and Foreign Policy (1988) · Government report · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress Country Study (1988), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/iraq/22.htm

Operation Anfal and the Halabja Chemical Attack Civil War · 1987–1988

According to Human Rights Watch's 1993 report, Operation Anfal began with an attack on PUK headquarters in Serkelu-Bergalu on February 23, 1988, and proceeded through eight stages (the third phase of the Germaan operation being the most brutal), ending with a general amnesty announcement on September 6. HRW notes that rural Kurds killed in Anfal 'number at least 50,000, and may be twice that.' Operations from 1987 to 1989 destroyed approximately 2,000 villages and at least 12 small towns and administrative centers. In the Halabja chemical attack on March 16, 1988, between 3,200 and 5,000 residents died; HRW classifies this as retaliation for Peshmerga occupation of Halabja with Iranian Revolutionary Guard support rather than part of Anfal. The Iraqi government at the time characterized the operation as suppression of rebellion.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Anfal DeathsUnconfirmed (Iraqi government did not announce)At least 50,000, up to approximately 100,000 (HRW 1993)
Halabja DeathsUnconfirmed3,200–5,000 (HRW 1993)
COW 5th Iraqi Kurds War (1985–88) combat deathsUnconfirmed4,000

Data Record: COW's 'Fifth Iraqi Kurds War of 1985-1988' records 4,000 combat deaths — civilian massacres (HRW 50,000–100,000) fall outside the definition of combat deaths and thus diverge widely. UCDP 271 (KDP·PUK) includes it.

  1. Human Rights Watch — Genocide in Iraq: The Anfal Campaign Against the Kurds (1993), Introduction · Human rights organization report · Orientation: International human rights organization (U.S.-based) — https://www.hrw.org/reports/1993/iraqanfal/ANFALINT.htm

March 1991 Uprising (Intifada) Uprising · 1991

Riots broke out in Basra on March 1, 1991, the day after the Gulf War ceasefire, spreading within days to Karbala, Najaf, Hillah, Nasiriyah, Amarah, Samawa, Kut, and Diwaniyah. In the north, a Kurdish uprising began around March 4 in Rania, seizing all northern cities except Kirkuk and Mosul within ten days. Government forces recaptured Kirkuk on March 28–29, Dahuk and Arbil on March 30, and Zakho on April 1; on April 5, the Revolutionary Command Council announced that 'all acts of agitation, destruction, and rioting in all cities have been completely crushed.' HRW (Middle East Watch) records that government forces fired indiscriminately into residential areas, killing thousands of unarmed civilians and summarily executing hundreds of young men in the south, while insurgents also executed suspected security personnel. Approximately 2 million people fled between March and early April.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsNot announcedThousands to tens of thousands (HRW 1992 — introduction 'tens of thousands,' Chapter 2 'thousands,' explicitly unverifiable)
Arrests15,105 (14,005 pardoned, 1,100 to be tried — Iraqi government)Many unaccounted for (HRW)

Data Record: COW's 'Iraqi Shiite Rebellion of 1991' records 1,000 combat deaths; the same war appears in two rows in the data file. The northern Kurdish uprising is included in UCDP 271.

  1. Human Rights Watch / Middle East Watch — Endless Torment: The 1991 Uprising in Iraq and Its Aftermath (1992) · Human rights organization report · Orientation: International human rights organization (U.S.-based) — https://www.hrw.org/reports/1992/Iraq926.htm

October 2019 Protests (Tishrin Movement) Protest · 2019–2020

From October 1, 2019, protests against corruption, unemployment, and public services broke out in Baghdad and southern cities. The UNAMI Human Rights Office reported that in the October 1–9 protests 'at least 157 people were killed by violence during the protests' and that 5,494 people, including security forces, were injured, documenting credible reports of deliberate killings of unarmed protesters and excessive use of force. A follow-up UNAMI report on the second wave of protests (October 25–November 4) tallied at least 97 protester deaths, while media tallies reached at least 254 by early November.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths (2019-10-01~09)Unconfirmed (no government figures cited in UNAMI report)At least 157 (UNAMI Human Rights Office)
Injuries (2019-10-01~09)Unconfirmed5,494, including security forces (UNAMI)

Data Record: Not present in NAVCO (up to 2013). See ACLED and MM annual tables.

2. Pre-Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

Abbasid Revolution — The Khurasani Army and Abu Muslim Late Umayyad · 747–750

The Library of Congress Country Studies notes that the Abbasid forces who overthrew the Umayyad Caliphate in 750 rose 'in the name of the descendants of al-Abbas, an uncle of Muhammad, and of the Hashimite family.' The same document records that the Abbasid army was 'composed largely of Khurasanians under the leadership of the Iranian general Abu Muslim' and that the movement received support from both Sunnis and Shiites. No figures for troops or casualties are given. From the Umayyad perspective, it is a rebellion; from the Abbasid narrative, it is a restoration of legitimacy.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Iran: Islamic Conquest (1987) · Government Report · Orientation: Library of Congress Country Study (1987), published by the US government — https://countrystudies.us/iran/9.htm

Isma'ili (Nizari) mountain strongholds and assassination of Seljuk officials Seljuk period · Late 11th century to mid-13th century

The Library of Congress Country Studies notes that a 'serious internal threat' to the Seljuk dynasty came from the Isma'ilis. It records that the Isma'ilis held the region for over 150 years from their mountain strongholds and built their power by sending supporters to assassinate key officials. The document contains no specific dates or casualty figures. Sunni and Seljuk sources describe it as a heretical rebellion, while Isma'ili tradition portrays it as self-defense of the faith community.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Iran: Islamic Conquest (1987) · Government Report · Orientation: Library of Congress Country Study (1987), published by the US government — https://countrystudies.us/iran/9.htm

Uprising of Afghan tribes and capture of the capital Late Safavid · 1722

The Library of Congress Country Studies notes that in 1722 'a small number of Afghan tribesmen, after winning a series of easy victories, entered the capital itself and occupied it.' This uprising by Afghan forces from the eastern marches of the Safavid dynasty brought effective rule to an end. The document contains no figures for the duration of the siege or casualties. From the Safavid perspective, it is a rebellion and invasion; from the Afghan narrative, it is resistance to religious and tax oppression.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Iran: The Safavids, 1501-1722 (1987) · Government Report · Orientation: Library of Congress Country Study (1987), published by the US government — https://countrystudies.us/iran/11.htm

Assassination of Nader Shah and tribal power struggles Afshar period · After 1747

The Library of Congress Country Studies notes that Nader Shah was 'killed in 1747 by chieftains of his own tribe, the Afshar,' and that thereafter 'a state of anarchy and a struggle for supremacy' ensued among the Afshar, Qajar, Afghan, and Zand tribal chiefs. The progress year by year and casualty figures are not given.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Iran: The Safavids, 1501-1722 (1987) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress Country Study (1987), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/iran/11.htm

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

Struggle for Power Among Zands, Qajars, and Tribes After Karim Khan's Death Around 1779–1795

The Library of Congress Country Study notes that after Karim Khan Zand's death, a power struggle among the Zand family, the Qajars, and several tribal groups 'plunged the country into disorder and disrupted economic life.' This struggle led to the establishment of the Qajar dynasty (1795). No figures for individual battles or casualties are provided.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Iran: The Qajars, 1795-1925 (1987) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress Country Study (1987), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/iran/12.htm

5. Modern Events through the Literature

Boycott of Tobacco and the Constitutional Revolution Uprising · 1890–1909

When the tobacco concession was granted to a British company in 1890, clergy, merchants, and commoners protested; when the high-ranking cleric Mirza Hassan Shirazi issued a fatwa against smoking, the ban was 'universally observed' and the shah rescinded the concession (the US Congress Library does not record the year of protest separately). In June 1906, some 10,000 merchants took refuge inside the British legation in Tehran and staged a sit-in; in August, the shah issued a decree promising a constitution. An elected parliament convened in October to draft the constitution, which the shah signed on December 30, 1906. In June 1908, Mohammad Ali Shah bombarded the parliament building with a Russian-led Persian Cossack Brigade and closed parliament. Resistance coalesced in Tabriz, Isfahan, Rasht, and elsewhere; in July 1909, constitutionalist forces marched on Tehran from Rasht and Isfahan and deposed the shah. The royalist side describes it as restoring order; the constitutionalists describe it as defending the constitution.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Number of protesters in the 1906 sit-in at the British legationUnconfirmed10,000 (US Congress Library Country Study)
Combat deaths in the 1908–09 Constitutional WarUnconfirmed1,500 (COW)

Data Record: NAVCO records the 1905–06 constitutional revolution as nonviolent and successful, and the 1908–09 constitutionalists as violent and successful. COW 'Iranian Constitution War of 1908-1909' (combat deaths 1,500). The tobacco boycott (early 1890s) appears in no dataset.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Iran: The Qajars, 1795-1925 (1987) · Government Report · Orientation: US Congress Library Country Study (1987), published by the US government — https://countrystudies.us/iran/12.htm
  2. Library of Congress Country Studies — Iran: The Constitutional Revolution (1987) · Government Report · Orientation: US Congress Library Country Study (1987), published by the US government — https://countrystudies.us/iran/13.htm

The 1921 Coup and Suppression of the Reza Shah Regime Coup · 1921–1936

The US Congress Library Country Study notes that in February 1921 a force led by Reza Khan entered Tehran and seized power. Thereafter, the army was used 'to pacify the country and control the tribes'; the shah forced many tribes to settle and had powerful tribal chiefs executed, arrested government opponents, and imprisoned or exiled some clerics. The same document records that in 1936 troops violated the sanctity of the Imam Reza shrine in Mashhad, 'killing dozens of worshippers and injuring many.' (This event is called the 1935 Goharshad Mosque incident in other sources.) The government side describes it as modernization and establishment of order; the opposition side describes it as authoritarian rule.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths in the Mashhad shrine incidentUnconfirmedDozens (US Congress Library Country Study)
Combat deaths in the 1921–22 Simko rebellionUnconfirmed2,000 (COW)

Data Record: Archigos records Sultan Ahmad's 'non-regular exit by the military' on 1921-02-21 and Reza Khan's non-regular accession. It is not in P&T (1950–). COW 'Simko Rebellion of 1921-1922' (Shikak Kurds, 2,000) is included, but the Gilan Jungle movement is not.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Iran: World War I (1987) · Government Report · Orientation: US Congress Library Country Study (1987), published by the US government — https://countrystudies.us/iran/14.htm
  2. Library of Congress Country Studies — Iran: The Era of Reza Shah (1987) · Government Report · Orientation: US Congress Library Country Study (1987), published by the US government — https://countrystudies.us/iran/15.htm

Overthrow of the Mossadegh Government in 1953 Coup · 1953

The US Congress Library Country Study notes that CIA's Kermit Roosevelt entered Iran secretly, and that the operation was 'a joint British-American operation to bring down Mossadegh, code-named Ajax.' As planned, on August 13 the shah appointed Zahedi as prime minister; however, the operation seemed to fail at first, the shah left the country, and Zahedi went into hiding. 'After four days of unrest,' the tide turned: on August 19 pro-shah military units and street crowds defeated the Mossadegh side. No casualty figures appear in this document. The royalist side describes it as a popular uprising; the Mossadegh-supporting side and later studies based on declassified documents describe it as a foreign intervention coup.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed (no figures in the US Congress Library Country Study)

Data Record: P&T records 1953-08-13 as a successful coup (the date of Zahedi's appointment). Archigos codes Mossadegh's ouster on 1953-08-19 as 'departure by other government actors, without foreign support,' which differs from the joint British-American operation described by the US Congress Library.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Iran: Mossadeq and Oil Nationalization (1987) · Government Report · Orientation: US Congress Library Country Study (1987), published by the US government — https://countrystudies.us/iran/17.htm

The Iranian Revolution Regime Overthrow · 1977–1979

In January 1978, seminary students took to the streets in Qom and clashed with police; several protesters were killed. Protests that began in Qom and Tabriz in February spread to 'dozens of cities' by summer. A fire at the Rex Cinema in Abadan killed more than 400 people; the Library of Congress Country Studies notes that, after the revolution, evidence pointed to religiously motivated students setting the blaze, but opponents circulated the belief that it was the work of SAVAK. On September 8, troops fired on protesters in Jaleh Square in Tehran, and the death toll 'was certainly far higher than the official figure of 87'. Public-sector workers went on large-scale strike from September onward, and hundreds of thousands participated in the December Moharram marches in Tehran and the provinces. The Shah left the country on January 16, 1979, citing a 'short vacation.' The royalist side describes the unrest as agitation by foreign powers and extremist elements, while the revolutionary side describes it as the Islamic Revolution.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Jaleh Square (Black Friday) Deaths87 (Iranian government official)Far higher than official figures (Library of Congress Country Studies, figures not cited)
Rex Cinema Fire DeathsUnconfirmedMore than 400 (Library of Congress Country Studies)
1978-12 Moharram March ParticipantsUnconfirmedHundred of thousands (Library of Congress Country Studies)

Data Record: NAVCO records the 'Iranian Revolution' (1977–79) as nonviolent and successful, while Archigos records Mohammad Reza Shah's abdication on 1979-01-16 as 'popular protests, no foreign support.' The COW civil war list contains no entry for the revolution itself.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Iran: The Coming of the Revolution (1987) · Government Report · Orientation: Library of Congress Country Study (1987), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/iran/21.htm

Armed clashes and mass executions immediately after the revolution (Kurds, Mojahedin) Civil War · 1979–1983

The Library of Congress Country Studies notes that a June 28, 1981 bombing of the Islamic Republican Party headquarters killed 73 people, including Beheshti, head of the Supreme Court and party secretary-general, along with four ministers and 27 lawmakers; the government blamed the Mojahedin, but no group claimed responsibility. In the Amol uprising in July 1981, at least 70 guerrillas and Revolutionary Guardsmen were killed before the crackdown. In September, the Mojahedin sent young supporters into the streets, hoping for a general uprising, and on September 27 they attacked Revolutionary Guards positions with machine guns and rocket-propelled grenades. 'Executions of fifty people a day became routine, and there were days when more than a hundred were executed.' Kurdish forces controlled parts of the countryside and moved in and out of major cities at night, but the government regained control of urban areas by seizing Buchan in November 1981. The government side describes this as the suppression of counterrevolution and terrorism, while opposition forces describe it as the seizure of the revolution and repression.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Islamic Republican Party Headquarters Bombing Deaths (1981-06-28)7373 (Library of Congress Country Studies)
Executions (12 months after Banisadr's impeachment)Unconfirmed (authorities did not announce all executions)2,946, 'conservative estimate' (Amnesty International, cited by the Library of Congress)
Anti-Khomeini Coalition War combat deaths (1979–83)Unconfirmed10,000 (COW)

Data Record: COW 'Anti-Khomeini Coalition War of 1979-1983' (10,000). UCDP 205 (KDPI), 338 (MEK, etc.), and 339 (APCO, 1979–80). NAVCO records KDPI (1979–96) and the Mojahedin (1981–2001) as violent and unsuccessful.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Iran: Terror and Repression (1987) · Government Report · Orientation: Library of Congress Country Study (1987), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/iran/26.htm

Post-2009 Presidential Election Protests (Green Movement) Protest · 2009

Protests continued in response to the results of the June 2009 presidential election. According to an August 11, 2009 AP report, Moussavi's aide Ali Reza Beheshti submitted a list of 69 dead to parliament, while authorities had stated that approximately 30 people had died. The government describes this as unrest and rioting, while the protest side describes it as protests against electoral fraud.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsApproximately 30 (Iranian authorities, cited by AP)69 (Moussavi camp list, AP 2009-08-11)

Data Record: NAVCO's 'Green Revolution and Day of Rage' (2009–13) is recorded as nonviolent and partially successful. MM records 21 events in 2009 (11 violent), the highest among 1990–2020.

  1. Associated Press via NBC News — 'Iran opposition leader says 69 dead in unrest', 2009-08-11 · Major Media · Orientation: U.S. wire report (citing opposition figures) — https://www.nbcnews.com/news/amp/wbna32369661

November 2019 Protests over Fuel Price Increases Protest · 2019

Protests began on November 15, 2019, after the government suddenly announced fuel price hikes. Amnesty International recorded 304 deaths in 37 cities across eight provinces during the crackdown period of November 15–19, with more than 220 dying on November 16–17. Amnesty believes the actual death toll was higher and notes that authorities arbitrarily detained thousands of protesters. Iranian authorities have not released an official death toll six months later, explaining that it took time to classify victims according to their degree of involvement in the protests.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsNot announced (as of 2020-05; Iranian authorities)At least 304 (Amnesty International 2020-05)
DetentionsUnconfirmedThousands (Amnesty International)

Data Record: NAVCO '2019 Iranian protests' (nonviolent, failed). ACLED's 2019 total of 464 deaths includes non-protest political violence and is an annual aggregate.

  1. Amnesty International — 'Iran: Details released of 304 deaths during protests six months after security forces' killing spree', 2020-05 · Human rights organization report · Affiliation: International human rights organization (UK-based) — https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2020/05/iran-details-released-of-304-deaths-during-protests-six-months-after-security-forces-killing-spree/

2022 Mahsa Amini Death and Protests (Women, Life, Freedom) Protest · 2022–2023

Nationwide protests erupted after Mahsa Amini died three days after her arrest in September 2022. In its first report in March 2024, the UN Human Rights Council fact-finding mission stated that 'credible figures indicate that up to 551 protesters were killed by security forces.' Thirteen days later, a special report by the Iranian presidency counted 281 deaths (202 civilians and 79 law enforcement/security personnel), classifying 112 of the civilians as bystanders and 90 as 'involved in terrorist acts and attacks.' The government describes it as an externally orchestrated riot, while protesters and the UN mission describe it as a crackdown on peaceful protests.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths281 — Civilians 202, security personnel 79 (Iranian Presidency, 2024-03-17)Up to 551 protesters (UN fact-finding mission, 2024-03)

Data Record: Not present in NAVCO (up to 2019). ACLED: 714 deaths and 300 political violence events in 2022. Official figures include security personnel, while UN figures count only protesters, so the counting bases differ.

  1. AFP via JusticeInfo.net — 'Iran says fewer than 300 died in 2022 Mahsa Amini protests', 2024-03-18 · Major media · Affiliation: French news agency report published by Swiss legal specialist outlet — https://www.justiceinfo.net/en?p=129687

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

Maccabean Revolt Hellenistic Period · 167–140 BCE

The Library of Congress Country Studies notes that King Antiochus IV Epiphanes of the Seleucid Empire desecrated the Temple in 167 BCE, and that this provocation led to 'a successful Jewish revolt under Judah Maccabee.' The same document records that the Hasmonean dynasty began in 140 BCE. No casualty figures are given. In Jewish tradition, it is regarded as a defense of faith and the recovery of independence; on the Seleucid side, it is treated as a rebellion.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Israel: Hellenism (1988) · Government Report · Orientation: Library of Congress Country Study (1988), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/israel/5.htm

First Jewish–Roman War (Great Revolt) Roman Period · 66–73 CE

The Library of Congress Country Studies notes that the Zealots challenged Roman rule in 66 CE, that Jerusalem and the Second Temple were besieged and captured and destroyed by Roman legions (70 CE), and that the last Zealot resistance forces died at Masada in 73 CE. No casualty figures are given in this document. In Roman sources, it is remembered as the suppression of a provincial revolt; in Jewish tradition, as the destruction of the Temple and a national catastrophe.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Israel: Hellenism (1988) · Government Report · Orientation: Library of Congress Country Study (1988), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/israel/5.htm

Bar Kokhba Revolt Roman Period · 132–135 CE

The Library of Congress Country Studies notes that Hadrian's repressive edicts 'triggered the Bar Kokhba revolt of 132–135, which was suppressed by the Romans.' No casualty figures are given.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Israel: Hellenism (1988) · Government Report · Orientation: Library of Congress Country Study (1988), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/israel/5.htm

5. Modern Events as Recorded in Literature

Early Mandate Unrest (April 1920 Uprising) Uprising · 1920

The Library of Congress Country Study notes that Haj Amin al-Husseini was 'a principal figure in the April 1920 uprising.' The document contains no location or casualty figures. This unrest occurred amid Arab opposition to British mandate rule and Jewish immigration. In Arab accounts, it is treated as the beginning of national resistance; in Jewish accounts, as violence against the community.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations · Other
DeathsUnconfirmedUnverified (no figures in U.S. Congressional Library Country Studies)

Data record: Because this was a British Mandate period, Israel does not appear under the country code in COW, NAVCO, or P&T.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Israel: The Arab Community During the Mandate (1988) · Government report · Orientation: U.S. Congressional Library Country Study (1988), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/israel/15.htm

Palestinian Arab Revolt in Palestine, 1936–1939 Uprising · 1936–1939

The U.S. Congressional Library Country Study notes that an April 1936 attack by Arabs on a Jewish bus sparked a series of events that escalated into a major revolt, and the Arab Higher Committee put forward three basic demands and declared a general strike. The British immediately dispatched the Peel Commission, whose report recommended partitioning Palestine into a Jewish state and an Arab state. The revolt broke out again in the fall of 1937; the British suppressed it with 'harsh measures,' dissolving the Arab Higher Committee and exiling most Palestinian Arab leaders. No casualty figures appear in this document. The Palestinian side describes it as the Great Uprising (al-Intifāḍa al-Kubrā), the British side as the suppression of unrest, and the Jewish side as a period of communal defense.

ItemOfficial announcementsIndependent investigations · Other
Deaths (Arab, Jewish, British)UnconfirmedUnverified (no figures in U.S. Congressional Library Country Study)

Data record: Because this was a mandate period, Israel does not appear under the country code in COW's civil war list or NAVCO. UCDP 207 (IZL) is a conflict between Jewish armed groups and Britain after 1946.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Israel: The Palestinian Revolt (1988) · Government report · Orientation: U.S. Congressional Library Country Study (1988), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/israel/17.htm

Resistance and repression in the occupied territories (1967–1981) Protest · 1967–1981

The U.S. Congressional Library Country Study notes that when Israeli troops entered Gaza in 1967, the territory was 'seething with discontent,' and Israel imposed harsh security measures to suppress widespread unrest. In the 1976 West Bank municipal elections, pro-PLO candidates won almost all major cities. In 1980, six yeshiva students were killed by Arab gunfire in Hebron; this was followed by a chain of violence, government crackdowns, and the expulsion of three Arab leaders from Hebron. In 1981, civil administrator Mison dismissed pro-PLO mayors, dissolved the mayors' national committees, and closed two Arabic newspapers and Birzeit University. The Israeli government describes it as maintaining order; the Palestinian side describes it as resistance against occupation.

ItemOfficial announcementsIndependent investigations · Other
1980 Hebron shooting deathsUnconfirmed6 yeshiva students (U.S. Congressional Library Country Study)

Data record: UCDP 234 (Israeli government vs. Palestinian armed groups) includes this period. NAVCO records only the 1981–82 Golan Druze resistance as located in Israel.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Israel: The Occupied Territories (1988) · Government report · Orientation: U.S. Congressional Library Country Study (1988), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/israel/32.htm

First Intifada Uprising · 1987–1993

In December 1987, mass uprisings against the occupation began in Gaza and the West Bank. B'Tselem's statistical table records 1,376 Palestinians killed by Israeli security forces and 115 Palestinians killed by Israeli civilians in the occupied territories (including East Jerusalem) from December 1987 to September 2000 (verified only through search excerpts in this round). The Israeli government describes it as suppressing riots and terrorism; the Palestinian side describes it as a popular uprising.

ItemOfficial announcementsIndependent investigations · Other
Palestinian deaths at the hands of security forces (1987-12~2000-09)Unconfirmed1,376 (B'Tselem statistical table, search excerpt)
Israeli deathsUnconfirmedUnverified (original B'Tselem table not consulted)

Data record: NAVCO does not include the First Intifada as located in Israel (it appears to have been classified under Palestine). UCDP 234 records the armed conflict aspect. MM notes that Israeli sources are blank.

  1. B'Tselem — Statistics: Fatalities in the first Intifada (Palestinians killed in the Occupied Territories) · Human rights report · Orientation: Israeli human rights organization (critical of occupation) — https://www.btselem.org/node/206391

2. Before the Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

The Kartli Uprising and the Attack on Bakhtrioni Fortress Kingdom of Kartli under Safavid rule · 1659–1660

According to an entry in the Georgian Encyclopedia edited by the Georgian National Academy of Sciences, the uprising forces attacked Persian Qizilbash troops, with decisive roles played by mountain peoples such as the Tush, Pshav, and Hevsur. The attack on Bakhtrioni Fortress took place at night; the uprising forces also attacked the Qizilbash garrison within the walls of Alaverdi Monastery, and the defenders of both strongholds suffered heavy defeats. The encyclopedia dates this battle to 1660; other accounts (such as the Orthodox Synaxarion) date it to September 1659, so the year is off by one year. On the page consulted in this round, there is no commander's name. The uprising side fought against Safavid rule and Qizilbash settlement as resistance; the Safavid account could not be verified in this round.

  1. Georgian Encyclopedia (Georgian National Academy of Sciences) — The Battle of Bakhtrioni · Public History DB · Orientation: Georgian National Academy of Sciences, Georgian perspective — https://georgianencyclopedia.ge/en/form_eng/438

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

August Uprising of 1924 Uprising · 1924

According to the entry on the Georgian Encyclopedia (Georgian edition), an 'Independent Committee' formed by Georgian political parties in 1922 led the uprising, and the date set at the joint conference in July was changed from August 17 to August 29 later. The authorities began arresting organizers from August 7, and the uprising broke out a day earlier than planned on August 28 in Chiatura. Uprising forces lost in major areas; some were shot dead, others exiled to Siberia, and 146 inmates were shot dead at Metehi prison on August 31 and September 3. The U.S. Library of Congress Country Studies also notes that 5,000 nobles were executed as punishment for the 'Menshevik rebellion' in 1924. The uprising side described it as the recovery of independence lost to the Red Army invasion in 1921, while Soviet authorities described it as the suppression of a counter-revolutionary rebellion.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations and Other Sources
ExecutionsUnconfirmed (Soviet official figures unverified)4,000–5,000 is the most common estimate; reports also exist of 7,000 punished (Georgian Encyclopedia); 5,000 nobles executed (U.S. Library of Congress Country Studies)

Data Record: The COW civil war data Georgia entry contains only two items for 1991–1993, and the 1924 uprising (Soviet era) is not included in the Georgia country entry.

  1. Georgian Encyclopedia — აგვისტოს აჯანყება (August Uprising) · Public History DB · Orientation: Georgian National Academy of Sciences Institute of Encyclopedic Studies, Georgian perspective — https://www.georgianencyclopedia.ge/ka/form/19964
  2. Library of Congress Country Studies — Georgia: Within the Soviet Union, The Interwar Years (1994) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress Country Study (1994), U.S. government publication — http://countrystudies.us/georgia/9.htm

1956 Anti-De-Stalinization Protest Protest · 1956

The U.S. Library of Congress Country Studies notes that in 1956 Georgians protested against Khrushchev's de-Stalinization policies, and hundreds died in the process. The pages consulted in this round do not specify date, location, or the entity responsible for suppression.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations and Other Sources
DeathsUnconfirmedHundreds (U.S. Library of Congress Country Studies)

Data Record: As an event from the Soviet era, it is absent from COW, NAVCO, and MM Georgia entries.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Georgia: Post-Stalin Politics (1994) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress Country Study (1994), U.S. government publication — http://countrystudies.us/georgia/11.htm

Suppression of the April 1989 Tbilisi Protests Protest · 1989

According to the U.S. Library of Congress Country Studies, in April 1989 Soviet troops dispersed a peaceful protest outside the Tbilisi Government House, and 20 Georgians died under unclear circumstances, mostly women and children. The study places this event within the context of rising nationalism.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations and Other Sources
DeathsUnconfirmed20, mostly women and children (U.S. Library of Congress Country Studies)

Data Record: MM protest data includes Georgia from 1990 onward, so the 1989 event is absent. The 1989 Georgian campaign is also absent from NAVCO 1.3.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Georgia: Nationalism Rises (1994) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress Country Study (1994), U.S. government publication — http://countrystudies.us/georgia/15.htm

First South Ossetia Conflict Civil War · 1990–1992

According to the Library of Congress Country Studies, in December 1990 Gamsakhurdia abolished South Ossetia's autonomous status, and the ensuing conflict continued throughout 1991, causing thousands of casualties and displacing tens of thousands of refugees on both sides of the Georgian–Russian border. A ceasefire mediated by Yeltsin was established in July 1992; Ossetian and Georgian forces and six Russian battalions monitored it. In July 1993 the South Ossetian government ended negotiations and warned of renewed fighting, but the ceasefire held until early 1994. The Ossetian side describes it as a struggle for self-determination against the abolition of autonomy; the Georgian government side describes it as a territorial integrity issue.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
CasualtiesUnconfirmedThousands (Library of Congress Country Studies)
RefugeesUnconfirmedTens of thousands (Library of Congress Country Studies)

Data Record: UCDP records the South Ossetian conflict (ID 393) as three active years in the 1992–2008 range, while NAVCO 1.3 records a 1991–1992 secession campaign. The COW civil war list contains no entry for South Ossetia (appears to fall below the threshold of 1,000 combat deaths).

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Georgia: Threats of Fragmentation, South Ossetia (1994) · Government Report · Orientation: Library of Congress Country Study (1994), U.S. government publication — http://countrystudies.us/georgia/19.htm

The Civil War in Tbilisi and the Ouster of Gamsakhurdia Regime Overthrow · 1991–1993

According to the Library of Congress Country Studies, National Guard commander Tengiz Kitovani refused Gamsakhurdia's order to disband the National Guard and led most of its troops outside Tbilisi. After government forces dispersed protesters on December 22, 1991, intense fighting broke out in central Tbilisi; the National Guard and Mkhedrioni surrounded Gamsakhurdia and his supporters in the parliament building, and heavy gunfire and explosions severely damaged the city center. Gamsakhurdia left the city early in January 1992. A Congressional Research Service (CRS) report refers to this event as a 'bloody coup of 1992.' Gamsakhurdia's supporters describe it as a military coup against an elected president, while opponents describe it as an uprising against authoritarian rule.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsUnconfirmedCOW 'Georgia War of 1991-1993' combat deaths: 5,000 (dataset value)

Data Record: COW records it as a government versus 'Reform Movement' civil war (1991–1993), while UCDP records it as a government versus 'National Guard and Mkhedrioni; Zviadists' (ID 380). Archigos 4.1 records Gamsakhurdia's ouster as 'overthrow by rebels without external support,' but the Powell & Thyne coup list contains no entry for Georgia — the CRS report's use of the term 'coup' diverges from the dataset classification.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Georgia: The Struggle for Control (1994) · Government Report · Orientation: Library of Congress Country Study (1994), U.S. government publication — http://countrystudies.us/georgia/17.htm
  2. Congressional Research Service RS21685 — Coup in Georgia [Republic]: Recent Developments and Implications (2003-12-04) · Government Report · Orientation: Congressional Research Service, U.S. policy perspective — https://www.everycrsreport.com/files/20031204_RS21685_f6e1e5754e6a52605c55200d0839ff2f00933cfc.html

War in Abkhazia Civil War · 1992–1993

According to the Library of Congress Country Studies, in August 1992 Georgian National Guard troops were dispatched to Sukhumi, the capital of Abkhazia, and fighting between Abkhazian and Georgian forces killed hundreds. In September 1993 Abkhazian forces surrounded and captured Sukhumi; many refugees fled across the Russian border or to other parts of Georgia. The study notes that unknown numbers of Russian soldiers and volunteers fought on the Abkhazian side, and it reports that Shevardnadze accused Yeltsin of supporting separatists and weakening Georgian security. The Abkhazian side has viewed Russia as a long-standing protector and describes the war as a defense of self-determination; the Georgian government side describes it as a separatist movement supported from abroad.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsUnconfirmedHundreds (Library of Congress Country Studies, published 1994); COW 'Abkhazia Revolt' combat deaths: 8,046 (dataset value)
Refugees/Displaced PersonsUnconfirmed'Large numbers' (Library of Congress Country Studies, figures not stated)

Data Record: COW records it as 1992–1993 (title reads 1992–1994) and internationalized; UCDP records it as 1992–1993 (ID 392, 1 war year with over 1,000 fatalities); NAVCO 1.3 records a 1992–1994 secession campaign 'failed' — the outcome in which Abkhazia effectively gained control diverges from NAVCO's classification of 'failure.' There is also a large gap between the study's 'hundreds' and COW's 8,046.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Georgia: Abkhazia (1994) · Government Report · Orientation: Library of Congress Country Study (1994), U.S. government publication — http://countrystudies.us/georgia/20.htm

Rose Revolution Regime Overthrow · 2003

According to a U.S. Congressional Research Service (CRS) report, following the November 2 election on November 2, 2003, large-scale protests broke out in the capital when final results were announced on November 20. On November 22, when Shevardnadze convened the new parliament, protesters entered the chamber and drove him from the podium; after reaching an agreement with the opposition through mediation on the evening of November 23, he resigned, and Nino Burjanadze served as acting president until the election. Sakashvili called it the 'Rose Revolution' to mean that the change of government was nonviolent. The report title characterizes the event as a 'coup.' The opposition described it as a popular revolution against electoral fraud, while Shevardnadze's side portrayed it as an extra-constitutional seizure of power.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsUnconfirmedNo mention of deaths in CRS report

Data record: NAVCO 1.3 records the campaign as 'successful', and Archigos 4.1 records Shevardnadze's departure (2003-11-23) as 'mass protests without external support'. MM: 3 events in 2003.

  1. Congressional Research Service RS21685 — Coup in Georgia [Republic]: Recent Developments and Implications (2003-12-04) · Government Report · Orientation: Congressional Research Service, U.S. policy perspective — https://www.everycrsreport.com/files/20031204_RS21685_f6e1e5754e6a52605c55200d0839ff2f00933cfc.html

2008 August War (South Ossetia) Civil War · 2008

The report of the independent international fact-finding mission established by the EU Council (IIFFMCG, headed by Heidi Tagliavini) (2009) states that the Georgian military's shelling of Tskhinvali on the night of August 7–8 was the beginning of large-scale hostilities, but it was the culmination of long-standing tensions, provocations, and events. The report sets out the figures released by each party side by side. The Georgian side reported 170 military personnel, 14 police officers, and 228 civilians killed, with 1,747 wounded; the Russian side reported 67 military personnel killed and 283 wounded; and the South Ossetian side reported 365 killed (combining military and civilian figures). Approximately 850 people lost their lives. Russian officials initially stated that approximately 2,000 South Ossetian civilians had been killed but later revised this to 162. Approximately 135,000 people were displaced from their homes. The Georgian side described it as a defense against the Russian invasion, while the Russian and South Ossetian sides described it as peacekeeping and protection of residents against Georgian attacks; the report does not attribute full responsibility to either side.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Deaths (Georgia)170 military personnel, 14 police officers, 228 civilians (Georgian government announcement)Total announced by parties approximately 850 (IIFFMCG)
Deaths (Russia)67 military personnel (Russian government announcement)Total announced by parties approximately 850 (IIFFMCG)
Deaths (South Ossetia)365 (announced by South Ossetian side); civilian casualties initially approximately 2,000 → 162 (announced by Russian officials)Total announced by parties approximately 850 (IIFFMCG)
DisplacementUnconfirmedApproximately 135,000 (IIFFMCG)

Data record: Although this is an interstate war (Georgia-Russia), it appears in this data file only as the 2008 active year of the UCDP South Ossetia conflict (ID 393, internationalized civil war) and as NAVCO 1.3 'Third South Ossetia Campaign (successful)'. It is not listed in the COW civil war list.

2024 Foreign Influence Law and Protests Against Suspension of EU Accession Negotiations Protest · 2024

According to the 2025 World Report of Human Rights Watch, when a bill on 'foreign influence transparency' was tabled in April, tens of thousands continued to protest; police arrested hundreds on minor criminal charges, and courts fined many after formal trials. Police used tear gas, water cannons, and pepper spray; there were reports of rubber bullets on May 1, and more than ten journalists and media workers were injured. Of the 182 detainees visited by the National Ombudsman's Office, 93 alleged police misconduct, and 73 cases were referred to the Special Investigative Service. In November, when the ruling party suspended efforts to launch EU accession negotiations until late 2028, nationwide mass protests broke out; hundreds were arrested on minor criminal or criminal charges, and dozens were hospitalized. The ruling party described the law's purpose as promoting transparency, while human rights organizations view it as targeting independent entities and the press.

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

The 1867–1868 Doha Raid by Bahrain and the Qatari Counterattack Uprising · 1867–1868

According to the U.S. Congressional Research Service's country analysis, a large Bahraini force raided Doha and Wakra in 1867, and Qatar counterattacked. In 1868, British Political Resident Colonel Lewis Pelly imposed a settlement, which the analysis describes as implicitly recognizing that Qatar was a distinct entity from Bahrain. The Bahrain (Al Khalifa) side described it as an exercise of suzerainty over the Qatar peninsula, while the Qatari side described it as resistance against that rule.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations and Other Sources
DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data Record: Neither COW nor NAVCO includes a Qatar entry (prior to the formation of the modern state).

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Qatar: Historical Background (1993) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Congressional Library Country Study (1993), U.S. government publication — http://countrystudies.us/persian-gulf-states/68.htm

Ottoman Troops Expelled from Doha in 1893 Uprising · 1893

According to the Library of Congress Country Study, Ottoman troops came to Doha in 1893 to arrest Kassim ibn Muhammad, who had refused to establish a customs house, and fighting broke out; Kassim's supporters drove off the Ottomans. The pages consulted in this round contain no casualty figures or names of battle sites. The Qatari account describes it as resistance against foreign rule, while the Ottoman account portrays it as a response to the defiance of a local ruler.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data Record: There is no Qatar entry in the COW civil war and interstate war data files.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Qatar: Historical Background (1993) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Congressional Library Country Study (1993), U.S. government publication — http://countrystudies.us/persian-gulf-states/68.htm

1956 Doha Protests and 1963 General Strike of the National Unity Front Protest · 1956; 1963

According to the Library of Congress Country Study, in 1956 approximately 2,000 protesters marched through Doha against the British and the ruler's entourage, and police presence was subsequently strengthened. In the late 1950s, declining oil revenues increased tensions within the royal family and in society. On April 19, 1963, a shooting incident in which one person was killed by a relative of the ruler, Ahmad ibn Ali, led to the formation of the National Unity Front, which launched a general strike demanding the curtailment of the ruler's privileges, the recognition of labor unions, and expanded social services. Ahmad imprisoned 50 prominent figures and exiled the front's leaders. The protesters and strikers described it as protest against royal privilege and foreign influence, while the ruler responded in the name of maintaining order.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
1956 Protest ParticipantsUnconfirmedApproximately 2,000 (Library of Congress Country Study)
1963 ImprisonmentUnconfirmed50 prominent figures (Library of Congress Country Study)

Data Record: The MM protest data cover only events from 1990 onward and thus contain no entries for the 1956 or 1963 events (the MM Qatar entry contains only one event in 2001). NAVCO 1.3 also lacks an entry.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Qatar: Historical Background (1993) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Congressional Library Country Study (1993), U.S. government publication — http://countrystudies.us/persian-gulf-states/68.htm

1972 Abdication of Ahmad by Khalifa ibn Hamad Coup · 1972

According to the Library of Congress Country Study, on February 22, 1972, Khalifa ibn Hamad deposed Ahmad ibn Ali while the latter was hunting in Iran. The khalifa received tacit support from the Al Thani family and Britain, as well as political, financial, and military backing from Saudi Arabia. The country study does not describe violence.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsUnconfirmedNo description of violence in the country study

Data Record: Powell & Thyne record a successful coup on 1972-02-22, while Archigos 4.1 records it as 'overthrow by other government actors without external support' — the country study's account of Saudi backing and British tacit support conflicts with Archigos's classification of 'no external support'.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Qatar: Historical Background (1993) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Congressional Library Country Study (1993), U.S. government publication — http://countrystudies.us/persian-gulf-states/68.htm

Bloodless 1995 Palace Coup and 1996 Counter-Coup Attempt Coup · 1995–2001

According to an Amnesty International Urgent Action document (2005-12-19), the current Amir deposed his father in a bloodless coup in June 1995 and an attempt to bring down the Amir's government in 1996 failed. Suspects were arrested multiple times in the mid-to-late 1990s; many were held incommunicado until trial, and some alleged that they were subjected to torture to extract 'confessions.' Eighteen received life sentences at first instance in February 2000 but were sentenced to death by the Court of Appeal in May 2001; most were known as military officers loyal to the deposed Amir. Two others sentenced to death in the same case, Sheikh Hamad bin Jassim bin Hamad al-Thani and another, were pardoned and released. The document states that the defendants numbered 19 Qatari nationals and one Saudi. The government portrayed it as judicial proceedings on an attempt to overthrow the state, while Amnesty described it as a trial lacking fairness.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Death Sentences (Court of Appeal, May 2001)Unconfirmed (no official announcement by the Qatari government confirmed)18 death sentences upheld + 2 pardoned (Amnesty International, 2005)
DeathsUnconfirmedThe 1995 coup was 'bloodless' (Amnesty International)

Data Record: Powell & Thyne record a successful coup on 1995-06-27 and an unsuccessful one on 1996-02-20, while Archigos 4.1 records the khalifa's departure on 1995-06-27 as 'overthrow by other government actors without external support.' The date of the 1996 attempt is given as February 14 night in some accounts and conflicts with P&T's February 20 (the original text for this round was not verified).
