3. 1760–1815 — Literature

Challenges to the governance system in the 1760s and the Al Khalifa family's departure 1760s

According to the Library of Congress Country Studies, the Al Sabah ruling family was chosen by a family council consulting with merchants and tribal elites, and there was 'a single major and failed challenge' to this governance system in the 1760s. As a result of the challenge, the Al Khalifa family left Kuwait and later came to rule Bahrain. The country study page does not specify the specific course of the challenge, the scale of conflict, or casualties.

5. Modern Events through the Literature

Mubarak's Seizure of Power in 1896 Regime Overthrow · 1896

According to the Library of Congress Country Studies, Mubarak seized power in 1896 and killed his brother-rulers who preceded him. The Country Studies notes that Mubarak was wary of Ottoman intentions because of the pro-Ottoman allied forces connected with his brothers, which led him to seek an alliance with Britain in 1899. The details of the process of his rise to power are not recorded on this page.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsUnconfirmedBrothers of the ruler (Country Study — no number of persons stated)

Data record: Archigos and Powell & Thyne cover events from 1816 onward, but because this event predates the inclusion of Kuwait as an independent state (1961), it is not included in this data file.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Kuwait: Government and Politics, Ruling Family (1993) · Government Report · Orientation: Library of Congress Country Study (1993), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/persian-gulf-states/26.htm

The 1938 Muzhailis Movement (Legislative Council Movement) Uprising · 1938–1939

The Library of Congress Country Studies notes that in 1938 a major uprising broke out among merchants who feared that the ruling family would monopolize new oil revenues. The merchants held legislative elections in June to push for reforms, and the Legislative Council governed for six months before being suppressed by the ruler and his tribal supporters. The Country Study calls this Kuwait's first democratization movement, and it describes how the framers of the 1962 Constitution also established an elected body 'influenced by the precedent of the 1938 Legislative Council.' The event can be described as merchants demanding reform and power-sharing, while the ruler sought to reclaim his authority.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data record: Because this is an event from the British protectorate period prior to independence (1961), it is not included in Kuwait under any of the COW, NAVCO, or MM datasets.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Kuwait: Government and Politics, Ruling Family (1993) · Government Report · Orientation: Library of Congress Country Study (1993), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/persian-gulf-states/26.htm
  2. Library of Congress Country Studies — Kuwait: Legislature (1993) · Government Report · Orientation: Library of Congress Country Study (1993), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/persian-gulf-states/28.htm

The 1989–1990 Constitutional Restoration Movement (Diwaniya Movement) Protest · 1989–1990

According to the Library of Congress Country Studies, the National Assembly was dissolved in August 1976 and revived through elections in 1980; after the parliament attacked members of the ruling family over the collapse of the Souk Al-Manakh stock market in 1982, it was suspended again in 1986. In 1989, former members of the dissolved parliament organized to demand the restoration of the parliament and the revival of the suspension clause of the 1962 Constitution; merchants, professionals, liberals, and Islamists joined them. The organization took place largely through diwaniyas, social gatherings held in the homes of prominent families, and the movement grew into 'a series of popular anti-government protests.' The government announced that it would create a National Assembly with 50 elected seats and 25 appointed seats instead of restoring the parliament; the opposition rejected the elections, but the assembly was formed through elections in June 1990. The Country Study notes that the assembly became meaningless when Iraq invaded shortly thereafter.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Arrests · InjuriesUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data record: Because MM v5.1's Kuwait annual records begin in 1991, there are no records of the 1989–1990 protests. Not included in NAVCO either.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Kuwait: Legislature (1993) · Government Report · Orientation: Library of Congress Country Study (1993), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/persian-gulf-states/28.htm

Internal Resistance During the Iraqi Occupation and Post-Liberation Martial Law Uprising · 1990–1991

According to the Library of Congress Country Studies, approximately 7,000 Kuwaiti soldiers fled to Saudi Arabia during Iraq's invasion in 1990; the rest were killed, taken prisoner, or joined an internal resistance movement. The Iraqi occupation included 'a systematic campaign of terror that included extra-legal killings, torture, and other inhumane treatment,' and as late as early 1993 approximately 850 Kuwaitis remained missing. Martial law was declared for the first time after liberation; during the four-month martial law period, up to 5,800 people (mostly non-Kuwaitis) were detained on suspicion of collaboration. In May–June 1991, approximately 300 people were tried by military courts, with 115 convicted and 29 sentenced to death, which was later commuted to life imprisonment following international criticism. The U.S. Department of State estimated that 45–50 foreigners, including Palestinians, died from torture at the hands of police and the military. The occupation also led to 'open discontent' with the ruling family itself, and criticism focused on the family's exile abroad during the occupation period. The Kuwaiti government side describes it as punishment of collaborators, while human rights organizations describe it in terms of the lack of legal guarantees and confessions obtained under torture.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other Sources
Missing Kuwaitis (early 1993)UnconfirmedApproximately 850 (Library of Congress Country Study)
Deaths from Torture After Liberation (Foreign Nationals)Unconfirmed45–50 (U.S. Department of State estimate, cited in the Country Study)
Detention on Suspicion of Collaboration During Martial LawUnconfirmedUp to 5,800 (Country Study)

Data Record: UCDP records this event as an interstate territorial dispute between the Iraqi government and the Kuwaiti government (ID 371, 1990–1991, over 1,000 fatalities in two years) — internal resistance movements and post-liberation martial law detentions are not recorded as separate items in the dataset.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Kuwait: Regional and National Security Considerations (1993) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress country study (1993), published by the U.S. government — https://countrystudies.us/persian-gulf-states/98.htm
  2. Library of Congress Country Studies — Kuwait: Government and Politics, Ruling Family (1993) · Government Report · Orientation: U.S. Library of Congress country study (1993), published by the U.S. government — https://countrystudies.us/persian-gulf-states/26.htm

2011 Bidun Protest and National Assembly Breach Incident Protest · 2011

According to Human Rights Watch's World Report 2012, from February 2011 hundreds of biduns (stateless residents) protested against the government's failure to process citizenship applications; security forces dispersed the protests with water cannons, tear gas, smoke grenades, and concussion grenades, beating some protesters and detaining dozens. The same report records the dispersals in February and April separately. On the night of November 16, 2011, thousands of protesters breached the main entrance of parliament demanding the resignation of Prime Minister Sheikh Nasser al-Muhammad al-Sabah and entered the chamber, while protest leader MP Musallam al-Barrak also demanded the dissolution of parliament (reports cited by AFP and BBC). According to reports, five protesters were injured. The ruler (Emir) called the 'occupation' a 'planned act of destruction' on November 16 and accepted the resignation of the prime minister and cabinet on November 28, allowing them to remain as an interim government (UPI). Protesters described it as a protest against corruption allegations, while the ruling side described it as the occupation of state institutions.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Injuries (November 16)Unconfirmed5 protesters (reports cited by AFP and BBC)
Bidun Protest DetentionsUnconfirmedDozens (HRW World Report 2012)

Data Record: MM v5.1 records 4 protests in Kuwait in 2011 (1 violent).

  3. UPI — Kuwait emir's about-face rankles activists (2011-11-28) · Major media · Orientation: U.S. wire service report — https://www.upi.com/Top_News/World-News/2011/11/28/Kuwait-emirs-about-face-rankles-activists/90071322522776/

2012 'March of the Dignity of the State' (protest against electoral law amendment) Protest · 2012

According to HRW (2012-12-27), on October 15, 2012, thousands gathered in Irada Square near parliament and both protesters and police suffered injuries. On October 19, the Emir issued a decree amending the electoral law to reduce the number of votes cast by each voter from four to one, and on October 20, the 'March of the Dignity of the State' took place. The Ministry of Interior announced on the same day that it would 'absolutely not permit any protest, march, assembly, or sit-in for any reason or motive,' but changed its stance on October 21 to allow protests only in Irada Square. HRW reported that masked riot police used tear gas and acoustic grenades without warning and beat protesters during arrests, detaining 'dozens' on October 20. In the second march moved to Mishrif on November 4, thousands were dispersed, and in a bidun protest in Tayma on December 10, eight people were arrested. The Ministry of Interior stated that protesters blocked traffic and threw stones, while more than 20 people interviewed by HRW said the protests were largely peaceful. In October, the Emir dissolved parliament and set the date for the December 1 election; the Islamist, liberal, and nationalist opposition boycotted the election (HRW World Report 2013). The same report notes that approximately 180 biduns and Kuwaitis were prosecuted for participating in protests in 2011–2012.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Number of Participants (October 15)UnconfirmedThousands (HRW)
Protest-Related Trials (2011–2012)UnconfirmedApproximately 180 (HRW World Report 2013)

Data Record: MM v5.1 records 11 protests in Kuwait in 2012, the highest among 1991–2019 (violent protesters: 1 case).

2. Pre-Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

Janissary Rebellion and the Abdication of Mustafa II Ottoman Empire, Köprülü Era · 1703

According to the Library of Congress Country Study (Türkiye, 1995), Mustafa II (reigned 1695–1703) 'was forced to abdicate by Janissaries who had rebelled'. The study describes that during the reign of Ahmet III (reigned 1703–1730), effective control over the government passed to military leaders. The page does not record the location, course, or casualties of the rebellion.

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

The Abdication of Abdulaziz in 1876 Regime Overthrow · 1876

According to the Library of Congress Country Studies, the 'powerless sultan' was deposed by a fatwa (Islamic legal opinion) obtained by Midhat Pasha in 1876, and a new sultan promulgated a constitution based on European models in December of the same year. The country study does not state whether force was mobilized for the deposition.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data Record: Archigos 4.1 records both the departures of Abdulaziz and Murat V in 1876 as abnormal changes (see data table).

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Turkey: External Threats and Internal Transformations (1995) · Government Report · Orientation: Library of Congress Country Study (1995), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/turkey/10.htm

The Young Turk Revolution of 1908 and the Enver Coup of 1913 Regime Overthrow · 1908–1913

According to the Library of Congress Country Studies, in July 1908 military units in Macedonia rebelled demanding the restoration of a constitutional government, and the Committee of Union and Progress (CUP) aimed to restore the 1876 constitution. In 1909 Abdulhamit II abdicated and was succeeded by his brother Mehmet V. In January 1913, a coup organized by Enver Pasha brought down the liberal government. The country study page contains no account of the 1909 counter-revolution (the March 31 incident) or casualty figures.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data Record: The COW civil war list records a 'Constitutionalist' vs. government war in 1908–1909, and Archigos 4.1 records the departures of Abdulhamit II and Mahmud Shevket Pasha as abnormal changes.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Turkey: The Young Turks (1995) · Government Report · Orientation: Library of Congress Country Study (1995), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/turkey/11.htm

The May 27, 1960 Coup and the Yassıada Trial Coup · 1960–1961

According to the Library of Congress Country Studies, on May 27, 1960, troops led by Army Chief of Staff Cemal Gürsel seized major government buildings and communications facilities and arrested President Bayar and Prime Minister Menderes. The National Unity Committee, composed of 38 officers who organized the coup, ruled the country, and Gürsel temporarily held the posts of president, prime minister, and defense minister. In October 1960, trials began on Yassıada Island in the Bosphorus for approximately 600 former government officials and Democratic Party cadres; all were convicted except about 100, and 15 death sentences were handed down. Menderes and two former ministers were hanged.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Yassıada Trial Defendants · Death SentencesUnconfirmedApproximately 600 defendants, 15 death sentences, 3 executed (country study)

Data Record: Powell & Thyne include the 1960-05-27 coup, and Archigos 4.1 records Menderes's departure as an abnormal change. P&T also record coup attempts on 1962-02-22 and 1963-05-20 (original text not verified in this round).

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Turkey: Turkey after Atatürk (1995) · Government Report · Orientation: Library of Congress Country Study (1995), U.S. government publication — https://countrystudies.us/turkey/15.htm

The 1971 'Memo Coup' and Political Violence in the Late 1970s Coup · 1971–1980

According to the Library of Congress Country Studies, on March 12, 1971, military leaders led by General Cevdet Güreli delivered a memorandum to President Şınay demanding a 'strong and reliable government,' an event known as the 'memo coup.' Political violence in the late 1970s claimed more than 20 lives per day until the mid-1980s, and some sources estimate that up to 2,000 people died from political violence in the two years of 1978–1979. The same country study notes that hundreds of Alevis were killed in sectarian clashes in Kırıkkale and Çorum in 1978–1979.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Political Violence Deaths (1978–1979)UnconfirmedUp to approximately 2,000 (some sources cited in the national study)
Levantine sectarian conflict Alawi deaths (1978–1979)UnconfirmedHundreds (national study)

Data record: Powell & Thyne records 1971-03-12 as a coup, while Archigos 4.1 records Demirel's (1971) departure as an irregular change. The political violence of 1978–1979 is not recorded by COW or UCDP as civil war or armed conflict.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Turkey: Crisis in Turkish Democracy (1995) · Government report · Orientation: US Library of Congress country study (1995), published by the US government — https://countrystudies.us/turkey/16.htm
  2. Library of Congress Country Studies — Turkey: Kurds (1995) · Government report · Orientation: US Library of Congress country study (1995), published by the US government — https://countrystudies.us/turkey/28.htm

September 12, 1980 Coup Coup · 1980–1983

According to the Library of Congress Country Studies, on the morning of September 12, 1980, the military seized control of the country, and a five-member National Security Council led by General Ecevit was appointed, with Ecevit recognized as head of state. In the first few weeks after the coup, approximately 30,000 people were reported arrested; one year later, about 25,000 remained in detention, and two years later, about 10,000, some without formal indictment. Among those convicted on terrorism charges, some were sentenced to death (the national study does not specify the number). In a referendum on November 7, 1982, the new constitution received 91.4% approval, and Ecevit took office as president for a seven-year term on November 9. The same national study notes that the military regime of 1980–1983 passed laws banning the use of Kurdish and possession of Kurdish materials. The military side describes it as the end of political violence, while those detained describe it as mass detention.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Arrests (first few weeks after the coup)UnconfirmedApproximately 30,000 (national study — citing reports)
Approval Rate for the 1982 Constitution Referendum91.4% (cited in the national study)Unconfirmed

Data record: Powell & Thyne records 1980-09-10 as the coup date, which is two days off from the September 12 date in the national study. Archigos 4.1 records Demirel's (1980) departure as an irregular change.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Turkey: Military Intervention and the Return to Civilian Rule (1995) · Government report · Orientation: US Library of Congress country study (1995), published by the US government — https://countrystudies.us/turkey/17.htm
  2. Library of Congress Country Studies — Turkey: Kurds (1995) · Government report · Orientation: US Library of Congress country study (1995), published by the US government — https://countrystudies.us/turkey/28.htm

Kurdistan Workers' Party (PKK) Armed Struggle Civil War · 1984–

The Library of Congress Country Studies notes that the armed uprising of the Kurdistan Workers' Party (PKK) began in 1984. No death toll is given on this page. The Turkish government describes it as a response to separatist terrorism, while the PKK describes it as a struggle for Kurdish national rights.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
DeathsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed

Data record: COW records the PKK conflict separately for 1984–1986 and the 'Kurds' conflict for 1991–1999, while NAVCO 1.3 records the 'Kurdish rebellion' and the 'Renewed Kurdistan Worker's Party (PKK)' campaigns as failures. UCDP armed conflict records are referenced in the data table.

  1. Library of Congress Country Studies — Turkey: Kurds (1995) · Government report · Orientation: US Library of Congress country study (1995), published by the US government — https://countrystudies.us/turkey/28.htm

July 15, 2016 Coup Attempt Coup · 2016

According to an update by state-run Anadolu Agency (AA) on July 20, 2016 (reported by Bianet), 240 people died in the coup attempt, including 62 police officers, 5 soldiers, and 173 civilians, with 1,535 injured; of the police fatalities, 55 were in Ankara, 5 in Istanbul, and 2 in Muğla. According to the same tally, 1,350 officers, 6,319 enlisted personnel, 210 security personnel, 1,481 judges and prosecutors, and 650 civilians were detained, with 7,375 still in detention. The tally changed several times over the days immediately following the event. The government side describes it as an armed attack on the constitutional order.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Deaths (July 20, 2016 tally)240 (62 police, 5 soldiers, 173 civilians, state-run AA)Unconfirmed
Injuries1,535 (state-run AA)Unconfirmed

Data record: Powell & Thyne records 2016-07-15 as a coup attempt. The AA tally of 240 does not include deaths on the side that participated in the coup.

2. Pre-Industrial Revolution (~1759) — Records of Uprisings and Peasant Wars

Zahir al-Umar's Siege of Tiberias and Seizure of Acre Ottoman Period (First Half of the 18th Century) · 1738–1746

EBSCO Research Starter (Fred Strickert, 2023) notes that Zahir al-Umar, a member of the Jezzar tribe, inherited his father's tax-farming territory upon his father's death in 1706. It states that in 1738–1743, Shereif Pasha, the governor of Damascus, attacked Tiberias to seize it, but Zahir held out. After Esad Pasha succeeded him in 1743, a peace of approximately 14 years ensued between Damascus and Galilee. In 1746, Zahir seized Acre by force, killed the tax farmer there, and took over his post. No casualty figures are given. From the perspective of the Ottoman center, it is treated as a secession of local forces; in the Palestinian national narrative, it is regarded as the beginning of an autonomous regime.

  1. EBSCO Research Starters — Zāhir al-'Umar's Stronghold in Galilee (Fred Strickert, 2023) · Academic Reference Material · Tone: US academic database overview, neutral narration — https://www.ebsco.com/research-starters/history/zahir-al-umars-stronghold-galilee

3. 1760–1815 — Literature

Zahir al-Umar's Autonomous Regime and War with the Ottoman Governor 1768–1775

EBSCO Research Starter (2023) notes that in 1768, while the Ottomans were at war with Russia, they granted Zahir authority over Acre, Nazareth, Tiberias, Safed, and all of Galilee. In 1770, Ali Bey, an ally of Egypt, sent troops to Gaza and Jaffa; in 1771, Usman Pasha, the governor of Damascus, attacked Zahir but was defeated by Zahir at Jacob's Ford and lost Jaffa. After the Ottomans concluded a peace with Russia in 1774, on May 20, 1775, Muhammad Bey occupied Jaffa, and on May 24, Zahir left Acre, returning upon Muhammad Bey's death on June 12. In the same year, while Zahir was demanding back taxes, he was killed amid a power struggle between his confidants Ibrahim al-Sabagh and Ahmad Aga al-Dinkeyzli. No casualty figures are given. From the Ottoman perspective, it is remembered as the suppression of rebel forces; in the Palestinian national narrative, it is recalled as the end of local autonomy.

  1. EBSCO Research Starters — Zāhir al-'Umar's Stronghold in Galilee (Fred Strickert, 2023) · Academic Reference Material · Tone: US academic database overview, neutral narration — https://www.ebsco.com/research-starters/history/zahir-al-umars-stronghold-galilee

5. Modern Events as Seen in the Literature

1834 Palestinian Peasant Uprising (against Ibrahim Pasha's rule) Uprising · 1834

The abstract of a thesis from Hebron University (Mu'tazz Abd al-Jawad Abidin, 'Palestinian Peasants Revolt Against Egyptian Rule in 1834') states that in the mid-1830s Palestinian residents turned from passive obedience to open revolt against the rule of Muhammad Ali Pasha (under his son Ibrahim Pasha). The causes were conscription, taxes such as the indut tax, administrative and fiscal measures that forcibly disarmed the population and abolished local privileges. The uprising spread to Jerusalem, Nablus, and Hebron; peasants were the main force, joined by local notables and Bedouin tribes. Ibrahim Pasha's army suppressed the uprising; leaders such as Sheikh Kassim al-Ahmad of Jabal Nablus were executed, and local leaders and scholars were exiled to Egypt. The abstract states that the uprising's goal was the expulsion of Egyptian forces and the return of Ottoman rule. The uprising is described by the Egyptian authorities as a suppression of rebellion, and in Palestinian narratives as a peasant uprising.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations / Other
Deaths · DeportationsUnconfirmedUnconfirmed (no figures in the abstract)

Data record: 1816 onward, but not included in COW or NAVCO (Ottoman-Egyptian rule period; no country code).

  1. Hebron University Theses — Mu'tazz Abd al-Jawad Abidin, Palestinian Peasants Revolt Against Egyptian Rule in 1834 · Academic Thesis · Orientation: Palestinian university thesis (Arabic), Palestinian perspective — https://dspace.hebron.edu/jspui/handle/123456789/1630

First Intifada Uprising · 1987–1993

In December 1987, mass uprisings against the Israeli occupation began in Gaza and the West Bank. NAVCO 1.3 classifies this as a nonviolent movement based in Palestine (1987–1990, partial success). B'Tselem's statistical table counts 1,376 Palestinians killed by Israeli security forces (281 under age 17) and 115 Palestinians killed by Israeli civilians in the occupied territories (including East Jerusalem) from December 1987 to September 2000 (verified only via search excerpt in this round; the original page returned a 429 response). The Israeli government describes it as suppression of riots and terrorism, while the Palestinian side describes it as a popular uprising.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations / Other
Palestinian deaths at the hands of security forces (1987-12~2000-09)Unconfirmed1,376, including 281 under age 17 (B'Tselem statistical table, search excerpt)
Palestinians killed by Israeli civiliansUnconfirmed115 (B'Tselem statistical table, search excerpt)

Data record: NAVCO records it as 'nonviolent · partial success,' but a separate entry for the same period, 'Palestinian Liberation' (1973–2019), is recorded as 'violent · failure.' UCDP and COW have no PSE country code field (recorded only under Israel's UCDP 234).

  1. B'Tselem — First Intifada fatality tables (search excerpt) · Human rights organization report · Orientation: Israeli human rights organization (critique of occupation orientation) — https://www.btselem.org/statistics/first_intifada_tables

Hamas-Fatah armed conflict and the seizure of Gaza Civil War · 2007–2008

Human Rights Watch (2008-07-29 press release) states that Hamas seized control of Gaza in June 2007, and since then serious human rights violations have been committed against Fatah supporters in Gaza and against Hamas supporters in the West Bank. Following a bomb explosion at a beach cafe in Gaza City on July 25, 2008, which killed a four-year-old girl and five al-Qassam Brigades fighters, the Gaza authorities arrested approximately 200 Fatah members and supporters and shut down more than 100 civil society organizations, charities, and sports clubs. In Gaza at least three people died in custody, and in the West Bank up to 100 suspects linked to Hamas were detained during the same period, with hundreds arrested without warrants over the past 12 months, and at least one detainee appears to have died, according to HRW. Hamas blamed Fatah for the explosion, which Fatah denied. The Gaza authorities reported disciplining more than 700 police officers, while the West Bank authorities reported disciplining them but did not specify the number, according to HRW.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations / Other
Deaths in custody in Gaza (2007-06~2008-07)Gaza authorities: more than 700 police officers disciplined (death toll unannounced)At least 3 (Human Rights Watch)
Detained in Gaza after the 2008-07 explosionApproximately 200 (Hamas Interior Minister Shiyam, 2008-07-28)Approximately 200, many soon released (Human Rights Watch)
Killed in the June 2007 Gaza fightingUnconfirmedUnconfirmed (original text of this round not consulted)

Data record: Although an internal Palestinian armed conflict, it is not recorded under a PSE entry in COW, UCDP, P&T, or Archigos (no state code). A case where virtually partitioned territory does not register in regime-change datasets.

  1. Human Rights Watch — Occupied Palestinian Territories: New Arrests Highlight Abuses by Hamas, Fatah (2008-07-29) · Human rights organization report · Orientation: International human rights organization (US-based), critical of both sides — https://www.hrw.org/news/2008/07/29/occupied-palestinian-territories-new-arrests-highlight-abuses-hamas-fatah

Return March (Gaza fence protests) Protest · 2018

According to UN News (2019-02-28), the UN Human Rights Council Independent Fact-Finding Mission stated that more than 180 Palestinians were killed by gunfire during the 'Great Return March' protests starting at the Gaza fence on March 30, 2018, and that 6,106 were injured by live ammunition, while 3,098 were injured by metal-coated rubber bullets, tear gas canisters, and other munitions. The same article reported that four Israeli soldiers were injured in the protests, and that one Israeli soldier died outside the protest site on the day of the protests. The committee judged that there was 'no justification' for the use of live ammunition. The official response of the Israeli government is not recorded in this article (other media reports state that Israel rejected the report entirely, but the original text has not been verified). Hamas and the protest organizers describe it as a nonviolent popular march, while the Israeli side describes it as an attempt by Hamas to breach the border.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Palestinian Deaths (2018-03-30~)Unconfirmed (original Gaza Ministry of Health figures not consulted)More than 180 (UN Fact-Finding Mission, UN News)
Palestinian InjuriesUnconfirmed6,106 by live ammunition + 3,098 by other means (UN Fact-Finding Mission)
Injuries to Israeli SoldiersUnconfirmed (original IDF announcements not consulted)4 injured, 1 killed outside protest site (UN Fact-Finding Mission)

Data record: This overlaps with the year when ACLED recorded a sharp increase in protests (1,314 in 2018, 2.4 times the 559 in 2017) and political violence (3,920 in 2018, 4.6 times the 856 in 2017). It is not included in NAVCO or MM.

  1. UN News — Gaza probe finds 'reasonable grounds' Israeli forces committed international human rights violations (2019-02-28) · International organization report · Orientation: UN official news, conveying conclusions of the Human Rights Council fact-finding mission (Israel rejected the mission itself) — https://news.un.org/en/node/1033742

Gaza War since October 2023 Civil War · 2023–

Following Hamas-led attacks on Israel on October 7, 2023, Israeli military operations in Gaza have continued. The UN Office for the Coordination of Humanitarian Affairs (OCHA) situation update on October 16, 2024 (Day 376) cites the Gaza Ministry of Health to record Palestinian deaths at 42,409, approximately 1.9 million internally displaced persons (about 90% of Gaza's population), and Israeli evacuation orders covering approximately 84% of Gaza's area. OCHA notes that 'figures not yet verified by the UN are attributed to their sources,' and that the Ministry of Health detailed breakdown includes only identified fatalities, lagging behind the total. The number of Israeli casualties is not recorded in this round's pages. The Israeli government describes it as a self-defense war to eliminate Hamas and return hostages, while the Palestinian side describes it as mass civilian slaughter.

ItemOfficial AnnouncementsIndependent Investigations & Other
Palestinian Deaths in Gaza (2023-10-07~2024-10-16)42,409 (Gaza Ministry of Health, cited by OCHA)Unconfirmed (original independent tallies not consulted)
Internally Displaced Persons in GazaUnconfirmedApproximately 1.9 million (UN estimate, OCHA)
Israeli Casualties (including 2023-10-07)Unconfirmed (original Israeli government announcements not consulted)Unconfirmed

Data record: ACLED records Palestinian deaths as 22,925 in 2023, 30,435 in 2024, and 16,904 in 2025. The ACLED cumulative total for 2023–2024 (53,360) cannot be directly compared to the Ministry of Health figures for the same period due to differences in counting timeframes and scopes. COW and UCDP data contain no PSE code entries.

Scope of Application of Six-Layer Discourse — The narrative of this monograph series spans from Layer 1 (data and records) to Layer 5 (interpretations through prior research). It does not provide prescriptions, moral evaluations, leader assessments, or predictions. Layer 6 is reserved exclusively for Cheonghae (淸海).
